# PREFACE

The following lectures are an attempt to outline the religious attitude and life of Muslims, as opposed to the systematic theology of Islam. I published a sketch of the development of the latter some years ago; this present volume may be regarded as a complement to that work.

I am very aware that its contents will be a surprise to many. Instead of being an Eastern replica—however humble—of Mr. William James's *Varieties of Religious Experience*, as might reasonably be expected, it will probably suggest to most readers the *Human Personality* of the late F. W. H. Myers. But no other approach was possible. Easterners have never learned the art of ignoring everything except the normal and the constantly repeatable; they have kept a mind open to infinite possibilities, and those infinite possibilities have continued to happen to them. Naturally, then, instead of their religion gradually limiting itself to emotions stirred by ethical aspirations, it has retained a very lively feeling of contact with an actual spiritual world, which exists in itself and is not in a process of dependent becoming.

Therefore, in the search for interpretive analogies, it was necessary to turn not to our metaphysical systems or to our religious philosophy, but rather to what we commonly call—whether in jest or in earnest—the occult. Consequently, these analogies had to be sought chiefly in the *Proceedings of the Society for Psychical Research* and similar publications. The case of Muhammad himself,

<summary>In this preface, the author explains that the lectures focus on the lived religious experience of Muslims rather than formal theology. He notes that the study explores the "occult" and psychical phenomena because Islamic religious life maintains a strong sense of contact with a spiritual world.</summary>
<keywords>Islam, Muslims, theology, William James, F. W. H. Myers, Society for Psychical Research, the occult, spiritual world, Muhammad</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's preface, discussing the use of psychical research analogies and detailing the Arabic sources used for the study.</meta>

# PREFACE

For example, he can be illustrated and explained much more completely by the phenomena of so-called <term>trance-mediumship</term> than by any other hypothesis. In the light of what we know now about such matters, even Sprenger’s most capable and learned investigation has been rendered completely obsolete. And it is noteworthy, further, that the theory of <term>veridical hallucinations</term> worked out by Gurney and Myers is essentially the same as that of <term>al-Ghazzālī</term> and <term>Ibn Khaldūn</term>.

But my use of these analogies has been so extensive that in order to avoid misconceptions, some statement of my own views is necessary. As far as someone who has had no first-hand experience can judge, I am compelled to regard <term>telepathy</term> as proven. Again, as far as one who is neither a physicist nor a magician can, I regard the proof of what Dr. Maxwell has called <term>telekinesis</term>—the movement of objects at a distance without contact—as approaching certainty. On the other hand, I know of no satisfactory proof of communications by <term>discarnate spirits</term>. I hope that this personal statement, offered in explanation and defense, may be excused.

References have been kept to a minimum, but have been given exactly for all the Arabic texts used. Of <term>Ibn Khaldūn’s</term> *Prolegomena*, I have consulted the Beirut and Bulaq texts and de Slane’s translation. The last, made from Quatremère’s text, is the most complete of all; the Beirut text has considerable omissions, both intentional and accidental, and some amazing blunders. Since it is the most accessible and seemingly usable text, it may be worth saying that it cannot be trusted regarding either its consonants or its vowels. The other references

<summary>The author explains his use of psychical research—specifically telepathy and telekinesis—as frameworks for understanding Islamic spiritual phenomena while clarifying his skepticism regarding spirit communication. He also evaluates the reliability of various editions and translations of Ibn Khaldūn’s Prolegomena.</summary>
<keywords>trance-mediumship, veridical hallucinations, telepathy, telekinesis, discarnate spirits, Ibn Khaldūn, Prolegomena, al-Ghazzālī, Arabic texts, Sprenger</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page concludes Duncan B. Macdonald’s preface, detailing his methods for translating and paraphrasing Arabic sources, as well as providing acknowledgments for the work.</meta>

# PREFACE

explain themselves to the Arabist; for him also I inserted a number of important words in translation; but not, I trust, so many as to annoy the general reader. Of necessity, I have had to reproduce much from Arabic authors. For the most part, this is condensed and paraphrased with as faithful a rendering of the thought as possible. But, from time to time, portions of simple translation occur; these are always indicated. My own inserted comments will also be recognizable. It is hardly necessary to add that in these ten lectures, barely a beginning has been made on the subject. But I venture to hope that I have sketched the essential outlines, and that future research will bring additions and corrections in detail. Very little has so far been done in this field; but several articles in Hastings's *Dictionary of Religion*, at present in preparation, will contribute to it; and all signs indicate a renewed general interest in Islām.

I have again to express my indebtedness to my colleague, Professor Gillett, for his counsel in philosophy, and to my wife for much patient labor in copying and for her suggestions and criticism in arranging and correcting the text. Without her shared interest and her knowledge of the general subject, this book might never have taken form, and in her hands I now fittingly leave it.

->DUNCAN B. MACDONALD<-

HARTFORD, CONN.,
October, 1908

<sig>I</sig>

# LECTURE I

## THE ATTITUDE OF THE SEMITES TOWARD THE UNSEEN WORLD; PROPHECY AS A SEMITIC PHENOMENON AND ESPECIALLY AMONG THE ARABS

<note>A hand-drawn box surrounds the following quote and the first two sentences of the paragraph.</note>

You may remember how Robertson of Brighton used to say, speaking of his sermons and their inspiration, “I cannot light my own fire; I must convey a spark from another’s hearth.” The same idea and the same expression occur in Islam. Muhammad, following the usage and speech of the desert, tells (*Qur’an*, xx, 10; xxvii, 7) how Moses left his family and went aside to the Burning Bush to seek from it a firebrand, a *qabas*, for their own fire. From this, *iqtibās*, or “seeking a firebrand,” continues in the rhetorical language of Islam to describe such borrowing of inspiration from one's predecessors. Permit me, then, having both Christian and Muslim authority, to quote—as a text for these lectures—a couple of sentences from Mr. William James’s *Varieties of Religious Experience*. These sentences precisely state the thesis that I propose to present to you as illustrated in Islam. <header>RELIGIOUS ATTITUDE AND LIFE IN ISLAM</header>

ception of the Unseen is much more immediate and real to the Oriental than to the western peoples. I use these two terms in the broadest fashion. But the cause is not at all obvious, and many ingenious students of race and racial characteristics have been shipwrecked by it. There are also, on both sides, significant modifying elements which seem, from time to time, almost to overturn the general law. If we say that the Semitic peoples, as a race, believe in and bow in reverence to an Unseen world, we may be met by the curious skepticism of the Arabs themselves—a skepticism which nearly baffled Muḥammad, and which appears today more or less throughout the entire desert. The Arabs show themselves not as particularly credulous, but as hard-headed, materialistic, questioning, and doubting; they scoff at their own superstitions and customs and are fond of testing the supernatural—in a word, tempting God—and all this in a curiously light-minded, almost childish fashion. They had diviners, it is true, as we shall see later, and were ruled partly by their guidance, but these always had to be prepared to allow tests of their powers and were regarded with general suspicion. For the Arab, nothing succeeded like success, as Muḥammad discovered, and there was no reserve of faith to carry them over the cracks in the supernatural scheme. They demanded signs from Muḥammad, and their ideas of signs were of the crudest, least spiritual description; the

Jews, in their most difficult times, did not have the same blindness to non-material things as these Arabs. On the other hand, consider Europe and the faith that developed there. Everywhere, we repeatedly find both the possibility and the reality of exactly the same absolute submission to and acceptance of an immediately present unseen world, which we usually attribute to the devout East. Later, I will have to tell you many stories—strange to the point of grotesqueness, simple to the point of childishness, devout to the point of ecstasy, and marvelous to the point of madness—about Eastern saints and their experiences. However, I venture to say that you can find parallels for all of them, down to the last detail, in the *Legenda Aurea* of Jacobus de Voragine, the archbishop of Genoa in the late thirteenth century.

Take, for example, the pickiness regarding their burial place so often displayed by saints after their death. The exact same trick on their part—making their coffin so heavy that it could not be lifted until the bearers had decided to grant their wishes—is found in the lives of the saints of both the East and West, and several times in the *Legenda Aurea*.<note>1. For example, Vol. IV, p. 170, and Vol. VII, pp. 145, 169, of the</note> Furthermore, it was not the West, but the supposedly devout East, which came up with the cynical counter-trick of spinning the coffin around rapidly until the saint had lost his sense of direction and did not know where he was being carried.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of religious attitudes, moving from the comparison of saints to the intellectual struggles against skepticism in the life of al-Ghazzālī and the fundamental differences between Eastern and Western thought.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>6</page-num>
<header># RELIGIOUS ATTITUDE AND LIFE IN ISLĀM</header>

Again, consider the case of al-Ghazzālī, perhaps the greatest constructive theologian in the Muslim church, who died in 1111 A.D. As I hope we shall see in more detail later, he had to fight against the most absolute unbelief throughout his entire life. In his earlier days, it was within himself. At one point, he reached the depths of complete skepticism and doubted even the workings of his own mind and the axioms of reason. And when, through the light of the mystic, he was able to find his own way again, he found the masses of people around him falling into similar unbelief. The creeds had broken down; the law of Islām was no longer respected; its divine origin was criticized or doubted; the nature and reality of prophecy were questioned. It was his work to repair the breaches in the Muslim Zion, and the fact that Islām still exists is largely due to him. It would be easy to add other testimonies. In Islām, as in Christendom, whoever seeks an age of faith always looks backward.

The truth is, I am persuaded, that we commonly view this acknowledged difference between the East and West from the wrong perspective and are governed by the wrong word. It is not really faith that is at issue here, but knowledge; it is not the attitude toward God, but the attitude toward law. The essential difference in the Oriental mind is not credulity regarding unseen things, but an inability to construct a system regarding visible things. It has been well said that the

<summary>The author highlights the life of al-Ghazzālī, who saved Islam from widespread skepticism, to argue that the East struggles with intellectual doubt just as the West does. He suggests the true difference between the two cultures lies not in their faith in God, but in their systematic approach to the physical world.</summary>

<keywords>al-Ghazzālī, skepticism, Muslim Zion, Islam, Christendom, Oriental mind, faith vs. knowledge, religious philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that the fundamental difference between the Eastern and Western minds lies in their perception of systematic knowledge and natural law, rather than in their degree of religious faith.</meta>

<page-num>7</page-num>
<header>THE SEMITES AND THE UNSEEN WORLD</header>

The Oriental possesses an astonishing keenness for viewing, grasping, and analyzing a single point; once he has finished with that point, he can take up a series of others in the same way. But these points remain separate for him; he does not coordinate them. They may be contradictory, but that does not trouble him. When he constructs systems—as he often does—it is by taking a single point and spinning everything out of it, rather than by taking many points and building them up together. Thus, he may criticize one point and be quite indifferent to the resulting necessity, for us at least, of criticizing other points. A good enough example is the Oriental method, which I have just mentioned, of thwarting a saint’s whim regarding his burial place. There is no great devout feeling there; no awe at the intrusion of the unseen, or at their closeness to the direct working of God. There is simply the fact of this obstinate, if deceased, saint, and, “Well, we’ll try to rattle him,” as we might imagine them saying in the slang of the bazaar. Familiarity breeds contempt. The supernatural, to them, is the familiar—the usual; only it is not subject to law, and they never imagine that it could be. The most they can do is to set their wits against it in detail.

Start, then, with this: that the difference in the Oriental is not essentially religiousness, but the lack of a sense of law. For him, there is no immovable order of nature. *“The army of unalterable law”*—

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "shell" of natural law, contrasting the Western scientific perspective with the Eastern religious worldview where the supernatural is an immediate reality.</meta>

<page-num>9</page-num>
<header>THE SEMITES AND THE UNSEEN WORLD</header>

shell. He knows that the supernatural has often peered through it at him. Our ghost stories and strange experiences are everyday things for him, which he never dreams of investigating, because he never doubts them. Our investigations are really attempts to bring these things under the rule of law; at that, he would simply shrug his shoulders.

This being so, it is evident that anything is possible for the Oriental. The supernatural is so near that it may touch him at any moment. There is no surprise, and therefore only a small test is needed for verification. In the case of our investigators of occult phenomena, spiritualism, and the like, the trouble is that no test, however complete, is ever truly enough. Our attitude is that there must be something wrong. But even the pagan Arabs—as light-minded and materialistic as they were—accepted their soothsayer if he told them even one thing that they were certain he could not have known on his own. For them, the idea that he was a soothsayer was not practically unthinkable. Give them good evidence, such as they would accept in ordinary life, and they would accept anything. There are some things that we, bound by our sense of natural law, cannot accept. And once the Oriental has been touched in this way—once he has had an impulse, however mysterious, in a certain direction—there may be no limits to the results. For example, it has been a favorite subject for argument, at great length, how much the person—

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the influence of Muhammad's personality, specifically addressing how his emergence convinced his followers that the barrier between the natural and supernatural had been broken.</meta>

<page-num>10</page-num>
<header>10 RELIGIOUS ATTITUDE AND LIFE IN ISLĀM</header>

ality of Muḥammad had to do with the Muslim movement; how much Islām is his individual creation, or merely a product of his times and circumstances. The fact is, I suspect, that the Arabs were just in this state of unstable equilibrium. His personality was strong enough to convince them—at least a sufficient number of them—that the shell had broken and the supernatural had come near. Once the idea is started that this man is a messenger from God and that his words are the words of God, the oriental mind carries it out to its utmost limits. A theory of all things in heaven and earth would be developed from this single idea. Other things might not agree with it; they would simply be set aside. The Oriental feels no need to explain everything; he simply ignores what is incompatible; and he does so conscientiously, for he sees only one thing at a time. This is not deduction; it is eduction. The idea is an egg from which a complete explanation of life is hatched. For example, once given the idea of Muḥammad, it was not long before the Muslim mind reached the conviction that he must have been the first of all creatures, created before all worlds, and existing from the beginning of time—here we have exactly the Arian doctrine of the person of Christ. Further, the fact of him became so overpowering that in a tradition Allāh is made to declare:
> “Had it not been for you, I would not have created the worlds.”

*The inability, then, to see life steadily and see it as a whole—to understand that a theory of life must cover all the facts—and the tendency to be overwhelmed by a single idea and blinded to everything else: there, I believe, lies the difference between the East and the West.*

But I have kept you too long with my own speculations, which are uncertain and probably erroneous in many ways. The one certain thing in all of this is the thinness of the shell that separates the Oriental from the Unseen. I turn, then, to the standard ways that shell is broken, as recognized by Islām.

These may be roughly classified as follows, though the divisions, I fear, will often be found to overlap: prophets, diviners, magic and talismans, appearances of the Jinn, dreams, and saints.

First, then, are prophets and prophecy. Here I can begin on familiar ground. The Hebrews—a Bedawi <note>Bedouin</note> tribe that abandoned the desert and turned, more or less, to an agricultural life—exhibit the essential characteristics of Arab prophetism. Nowhere does their unity with Arabia appear more strongly, and yet nowhere is the essential difference in the religious nature of the Hebrews more marked. A figure such as Elijah—at least as far as the Old Testament has preserved his legend for us—must have appeared again and again in the earlier desert, and certainly appeared among the saints of Islām. <meta>This page continues the comparison between the "Sons of the Prophets" in Hebrew tradition and the dervish fraternities in Islam, using an analogy involving the poet William Wordsworth to explain how great prophets emerge from a common cultural environment.</meta>

to time we have fleeting glimpses of, can be exactly paralleled by the darwīsh <note>dervish</note> fraternities of Islām. Their relations with the people, their ceremonies and customs, their way of life, and their ecstasies and religious fervors were evidently exactly the same. In a word, the soil from which the great prophets sprang was the same among both the Hebrews and the Arabs.

Let me illustrate this vital matter of the soil and the growth from it with a parallel from creative literature. I will take the case of a single poet, though the broad literature of an entire people always exhibits the same phenomena. The mind of Wordsworth was a constant poetic soil, and from it sprang all manner of plants in a lush and bewildering tangle. Most of these were scrub and brush—undergrowth that was often commonplace and even grotesque. Only a small circle of devoted followers finds delight there. But above that scrub and brush, great trees rise from time to time, as glorious in their unique and peaceful beauty as any in the history of English literature. We cannot tell what favorable influences intervened there; the processes of the poet's mind are as mysterious as that spirit of the Lord which descended upon the Hebrew prophet. But at one time, as someone has said—harshly but not untruly—the voice of Wordsworth is that

> of an old half-witted sheep,
> Which bleats in articulate monotony
> And indicates that two and one are three.

What spirit touched Wordsworth then, we do not know; but we do know that some relationship existed between his painful struggles and those lofty flights.

So, when we turn from the common spirit of prophecy to the great Hebrew prophets, how wide the difference is! Isaiah—any of the Isaiahs—rises from the howling, frenzied mob of <term>nebhî'îm</term>; he is part of them, and yet not of them. He could participate in their frenzied rituals, yet his head was high above their sensory fogs; his brain and conscience were never swept away by their gusts of passionate ecstasy. Similarly, Samuel moved clear-eyed through the cloudy atmosphere of the religious life of his companions. He and those like him had seen the Lord, and the beauty of holiness belonged to them. In these lectures, I will not often have the opportunity for comparison, much less for <term>apologetics</term> <note>formal defenses of religious doctrines</note>. Let me take this chance to say, as firmly and broadly as I am able

<meta>This page continues the author's argument regarding the unique nature of the Hebrew prophets compared to other Semitic religious figures, specifically contrasting them with Muhammad and Al-Ghazzali.</meta>

<header>RELIGIOUS ATTITUDE AND LIFE IN ISLĀM</header>

is that, while the soil of Semitic prophecy is one, I know of no appearance anywhere else in the Semitic world like that of the great prophets of the Hebrews. They stand as distinct from their soil as love in a Christian marriage stands from the lust of the flesh, and the relationship is much the same.

In Islam, some few attempted the same heights, but never reached them. Muhammad—a figure who now seems strangely sympathetic and attractive, and then again repellently weak—occasionally in his early life shows touches of the ethical glory of Amos, but he never saw the vision of love found in Hosea. In his later life he fell, and it is not for us to judge him. Perhaps, if Jeremiah had come to rule some small but conquering remnant of Judah with absolute power, he, too, might have fallen. If Isaiah had risen from being a <term>wazīr</term> <note>a high official or minister</note> in Jerusalem to becoming a <term>sulṭān</term>, his robes might have been stained by the flesh and his soul by ambition. But, entirely apart from the last unhappy ten years of Muhammad’s life, he did not belong to the noble fellowship of the Hebrew prophets.

I have already mentioned Al-Ghazzālī. He was a man of the same intellectual rank as Augustine. Yet he was himself a <term>darwīsh</term>, and he took part in their religious exercises. He understood these through sympathy, and in a treatise which I have translated elsewhere,¹ he applied the methods of science to the

But although his mind was probably sharper than that of any Hebrew, and although he understood the essence of the matter, yet no one can mistake the difference in atmosphere between his writings and those of the Old Testament. In the latter, there is the freshness of life and, despite everything, of hope; he is an ascetic scholastic, and all his effort is to gain assurance of the world to come. Whatever the cause may have been, it was fortunate for the Hebrews that they were not blinded to the facts and duties of this life by the vision of another. *Islām*, like medieval Europe, could think of nothing but the unending hereafter with its sharply divided joy or misery.

Yet, despite all this, the soil was the same, and we must start from there. But here we encounter another question. How broad was that soil? How much of the life, thought, emotional output, and literature, in a sense, of the people, is to be included in this broad prophetism? Let me address this with another question. Why is it that we do not find anything in the surviving remains of Hebrew literature except what is directly or indirectly religious? Furthermore, and even more pointedly, even if, by some strange chance, all their secular literature has been lost—and there is some fairly secular material still in the Old Testament—why is there almost no mention of poets among them? not. Did they classify and name poets in some other way? Did they put them in some other category? Furthermore, they did have stories, common among the people, about their heroic age and their great warriors and deliverers. Through what channels were these passed down? Who played the role of the wandering gleemen, skalds, bards, and minstrels of medieval Europe? We cannot doubt that such people existed. The desert knows them to this day. May I suggest another possible answer? Was their role filled by *nebhî'îm*, either alone or in groups? Were poetry and legend—their production, preservation, and transmission—all contained within the Schools of the Prophets? You might say this is as absurd as grouping together mendicant friars and the gleemen of Europe. Sometimes, even these did come together in very strange ways, but that was exceptional in Christendom. In the Semitic world, I venture to say, it was the rule, and this can be proven regarding the desert.

What did the ancient Arabs believe about the nature of poetry, and what was their attitude toward the poet as a person? Since Ignaz Goldziher’s investigations, published in his *Arabische Philologie*, Part I, there can be no doubt regarding the answers to these questions. The answers provided by Arabic sources are also the same ones suggested by the analogy of other primitive peoples. Poetry is magical utterance, inspired by powers from

## THE SEMITES AND THE UNSEEN WORLD

the Unseen, and the poet is partly a soothsayer, partly an advisor and counselor, and partly a hurler of magical formulas against his enemies. The most common and primitive Arabic word for poet is *shā'ir*, which simply means "one who perceives" or "one who knows." In meaning, it is parallel to the Hebrew *yiddi'ônî*, but that Hebrew word never evolved from the concept of divination to that of poetic expression. On the other hand, the Hebrew *môshēl*—which in Arabic suggests only a proverb, likeness, or parable—has come to mean a specific type of poet: one who utters reproaches and curses whose words are certain to take effect. In Hebrew history, the outstanding example of the *môshēl*, and an example of the Arabic *shā'ir* (poet) in this aspect of his activity, is the remarkable figure of Balaam. So, in the Semitic world, the bard and the prophet unite. Balaam was evidently believed to stand in a very real relationship with the unseen world, a connection that gave his words supernatural force—provided they were once spoken and not stopped at his lips by a higher power. Similarly, the poet of the Arabs drew his knowledge, wisdom, skill, and destructive speech from his relationship with the Jinn. For the pagan Arabs, these beings were like the fauns, nymphs, and satyrs of the classical world; they often seem to have been regarded as minor deities. Islam has accepted them as a class of created beings and envisioned them partly as Muslim, partly as unbelievers, and partly as

diabolical in nature. Such, then, is the situation in a nutshell.

But let me illustrate in detail. A good example is found in the stories told about Ḥassān ibn Thābit, a close personal follower of Muḥammad and, in a sense, his poet laureate. Muḥammad, in general, was opposed to poetry; the poets were mostly opposed to him; but Ḥassān upheld his cause with a certain kind of poetry and was especially useful in replying to satirical and abusive attacks. However, this Ḥassān, while still a young man in the days before Islām and before he had composed any verses, was initiated into poetry by a female Jinnī. She met him in one of the streets of Medīna, leaped upon him, pressed him down, and compelled him to utter three verses of poetry. After that, he was a poet, and his verses came to him—as they did to other Arab poets—from the direct inspiration of the Jinn. He refers to his "brothers of the Jinn" who weave artistic words for him, and he tells how weighty lines have been "sent down" to him from heaven during the night. The curious thing is that the expressions he uses are exactly those used for the "sending down"—that is, the revelation—of the *Qur’ān*. Evidently, in his case, there was a struggle between the idea of the Jinn—those half or wholly pagan spirits—as inspirers and the divine inspirations of the angels.

Furthermore, the story goes that Muḥammad used to set up a pulpit for him in the mosque and stand by

But by the "Holy Spirit" here, you must not understand any concept like that of the third person of the Christian trinity. For Muḥammad, the phrase referred only to the angel messenger who brought him his revelations. The theological consequences of the lack of the concept of the Holy Ghost—the Lord and Giver of Life—in Islām were extensive, but this is not the place to discuss them. Here, Muḥammad simply attributed to Ḥassān the same kind of inspiration that he had himself, and that is remarkable enough.

Another point to observe is the close parallel between the terms used in the story of Ḥassān’s initiation and those of the first revelation to Muḥammad. Just as Ḥassān was thrown down by the female spirit and had verses pressed out of him, so the first utterances of prophecy were pressed from Muḥammad by the angel Gabriel. And the resemblances go even further. He was, in truth, a poet of the old Arabic type, though lacking the skill of writing verse, and with his whole being dedicated to the prophetic side of poetry. Add to this a strange mixture of Jewish and Christian concepts, and you have the key to understanding Muḥammad.

I do not need to go into the details of the many stories told about the interactions between poets and their inspiring spirits: how a poet would sit helpless, lacking an idea, until his "comrade" would call to him from the corner of the room; how another, in desperate need, saddled his camel, rode off into the desert, and upon reaching a certain place, dismounted and cried out, "Come to the aid of your brother, your brother!" how the aid arrived quickly, the poet lay down, and did not get up until he had composed one hundred and thirteen lines. There are many such stories, which were later told in jest.

Regarding the poet sitting as a judge like Samuel, Dr. Goldziher can quote no case from pagan Arabia. But that is certainly due to our very incomplete sources. It must be regarded as significant that in very early Muslim times, the poet al-Akhṭal, though a Christian, sat in the mosque of his tribe as a judge. Evidently this points at once to old pre-Muslim custom, and to a religious authority and dignity surrounding the poet.

We must not, therefore, think of the poet as being given this position because of any respect for the beauty or strength of his verses, or even for his human insight and wisdom in matters of tribal conduct and politics. The idea that the Arab tribes so respected their poets—at least in the first instance—because of their keen artistic sense, their appreciation of the beauties of poetry, must be given up. Their attitude was much more practical. The distinctiveness of the poet from other men had struck them. So, too, had the way in which his verses came to him, out

of the sky apparently, apart from his labor and will. We must remember that the Arab poet was a lyrist, first and last; intensely subjective and personal regarding both himself and his hearers. When he sang before the tribe on the day of battle and onset, it was as though a spirit sang through him. When he brooded in the council and then suddenly arose and flung out his judgment in clanging words and ringing rhymes, it was like the utterance of a god. From time to time, too, in the intense nervous sensitivity of the Arab race in the keen desert air, there fell upon him cataleptic rigors, swoons, and dreams, from which he returned with strange words in his mouth. If anyone could hear or see the <term>Jinn</term> in the desert stillness and solitude, or in the dark recesses of the mountains, it would be he, with his strained nerves and burdened imagination. Often, as with <term>Socrates</term>, his own decision must have come as if with a voice from the outside, and it would take little to add a visible form. This mysterious side of human nature, in which the nerves and the senses conspire to mislead, is only gradually being explained to us, but we know enough of its possibilities to see fully how the <term>Arabs</term> thought their poets were illuminated from the Unseen, and could make little, if any, distinction between them and diviners and prophets.

*shāʿir*, “poet,” as we have called him, the *kāhin* or diviner, and the *ʿarrāf*, also a kind of diviner. All were supernaturally guided, but the last was on the lowest step. He told—again like Samuel—about stolen things, and where wandering beasts might be found. Curiously enough, we find him consulted, too, as a physician; perhaps with the thought of lost or stolen health. The *kāhin* foretold the future and secret things generally. He was limited mostly to a certain sanctuary—you will remember, of course, that *kāhin* is exactly the Hebrew *kôhēn*, “priest”—and there he had to be consulted. He was, as Goldziher, following Wellhausen, well puts it, an institution. The *shāʿir*, on the other hand, was free. He was the counselor of his people, and his counsel was inspired from the Unseen, by the Jinn, exactly as was the case with the others. But he was also a man and a warrior, free as the desert, and bound to no sacred shrine, no Urim and Thummim. Not only wisdom came to him but words, beautiful or fiery and terrible; which could give life or death by a mysterious power in them, but also give delight by their sheer loveliness. And this belief long survived the coming of Islām. The oriental poet cannot rid himself of the faith that verses come from without. His method is inspirational, not that of the labor of the file. ### RELIGIOUS ATTITUDE AND LIFE IN ISLĀM

al-Khaḍir, that undying wandering saint, the most picturesque figure in Muslim mythology, who journeys through the earth, rescuing, guiding, and counseling. Even as late as the seventh Muslim century, we find a Hanbalite theologian—belonging to the most narrow sect of all—arguing that the *Qur'ān* must be uncreated; otherwise, it would be no better than poetry which, as is generally accepted, God uses to inspire the poet. But if the poet were not a religious man, or if the attitude toward all poetry were hostile, then its inspiration was easily sought elsewhere. The Jinn and the devils have become hopelessly confused in Islām, and we can never be sure whether, by the word *shayṭān* ("devil"), an Arabic writer means the personal evil spirit borrowed from Christianity and Judaism, or merely a malignant member of the Jinn. So it was easy to say that the inspiration of poetry came from the devil, and even to brand all poetry as the *Qur'ān* of the devil.

But all that occurred long after our period, and we must go back to the "winged words" of the old Arab poets. Our connection with Balaam is not yet absolutely proven, but it must be beginning to unfold before you. It is well known how, among primitive peoples, there has always been a belief that a certain supernatural power resides in words. To them, words are things; they are strict realists. Thus, once a curse is spoken, it is an existing entity which must strike and settle somewhere; if not against the one

whom it is hurled, then the hurler himself. In Islam, this has endured longer than anywhere else. I doubt whether in the scholastic theology of any other people you could find passages like the following:

> When two men curse one another, the curse falls on him who deserves it; if neither deserves it, then it returns and falls upon the Jews, who conceal what God has revealed.

And again:

> When a curse is sent against anyone, it goes toward him, and if it finds access to him, it enters into him. But if it finds no access, it returns to its Lord, to whom belong Might and Majesty, and says, "O my Lord, so-and-so sent me against so-and-so, but I find no access to him; so what do You command me?" He then says, "Return to where you came from."

But all this is only a reduction to a system and method of a belief which Islam, from the beginning, has held unshaken, as did the earlier Semitic faiths before Islam. It meets us among the Hebrews; there, the story of Balaam is unmistakable. And in early Arabia, it was the custom that the poet of a tribe, on the day of battle, should advance and recite satirical and abusive verses against the opponents. This was not simply to hearten his own tribe, or to strike the other with shame and confusion. There was a magical power in his words, and they often show the traces, as preserved in the *dīwāns* of the greater poets, of simple cursing.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the magical power of the spoken word and the role of the ancient poet as an inspired figure whose utterances carried supernatural weight.</meta>

# RELIGIOUS ATTITUDE AND LIFE IN ISLĀM

Similarly, among the Hebrews, Goliath mocked and ridiculed (*ḥērēph*) the armies of Israel. In all this, it was a spirit that had entered the poet and spoke through him. Hence the magical effectiveness of his words; he was only the channel of communication through which the unseen world worked. Gestures and symbols also often helped. As long as the hands of Moses were held up, even mechanically, the Israelites triumphed over Amalek. In later Muslim times, certain poets gained a reputation for having particularly unlucky tongues. Whoever they cursed, some misfortune befell; and we even have evidence of them using certain symbolic actions and styles of dress for this purpose.

I believe I have now made fairly clear the Semitic belief that the poet was inspired—was a *vates*, in short—and that his poem, or rather his song, was a *carmen*, a charm. For further details, I would refer you to the groundbreaking paper by Dr. Goldziher, which I have already cited. Whether you agree with my further explanation for the lack of specific references to poets and poetry in Hebrew literature—that they are grouped into the general category of prophecy and prophets—does not greatly matter for my current purpose. I now take for granted that for the early Arabs and Hebrews, poetry and prophecy both stem from inspiration from the Unseen and are, for many purposes, practically the same thing.

It highlights how, in early Arab and Hebrew traditions, poetry and prophecy were considered essentially the same, originating from the unseen world.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the magical power of the poet's words and transitions to the role of the kāhin (soothsayer) in Arab and Hebrew tradition.</meta>

# THE SEMITES AND THE UNSEEN WORLD

<margin>✓</margin>Before dealing directly with the position of the prophet among the Arabs and in Islām, it may be appropriate here to discuss the *kāhin*, or soothsayer. As I have already said, this word is the exact linguistic equivalent of the Hebrew *kôhēn*, and—without entering into the disputed questions surrounding that word—I would only remind you that Potiphar, Jethro, and David's sons are all called *kôhēns* in the Old Testament. In Arabia, the matter is much simpler. The *kāhins* were soothsayers associated with a sanctuary, or sometimes with a tribe, and they played much the same role as Eli and Samuel at Shiloh. All mysterious and obscure matters seem to have been referred to them. They were judges, but they also foretold the future and the Unseen. How real this was to the Arabs of Muḥammad's time is evident from the fact that he felt compelled to admit their foreknowledge, if only in part, and to attribute that part—likely in agreement with Arab belief—to the help of the Jinn.

But what most claims our attention regarding them is the consistent form of their speech, which was also the form of speech for all mysterious knowledge limited to a narrow circle and professionally guarded. Just as the Greek oracles were expressed in verse, the oracles of the Arab *kāhins* were delivered in that primitive verse called *sajᶜ*, literally "pigeon-cooing." You will remember in Isaiah (8:19) how the *Yiddeᶜônîm* chirp and mutter. The word there

It describes how these figures served as judges and foretellers who used a specific rhythmic prose called sajᶜ to deliver their mysterious oracles.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of the term *saj‘* (rhymed prose) and its relationship to both Hebrew and Arabic traditions of prophecy and divination.</meta>

# RELIGIOUS ATTITUDE AND LIFE IN ISLĀM

for "mutter" (*hāgā*) is also used for the cooing of a pigeon, and there seems to be little doubt that we have an allusion to a similar phenomenon. This *saj‘*, which has now become the standard rhetorical form of language in Islām, consists essentially of a series of short phrases in prose—that is, without a fixed meter, but possibly with rhythm—all rhyming together. If the rhythm is made to follow a rule, monorhymed verse appears; take away the rhymes, and you have more or less rhythmical prose. This rhymed prose, then, was the essential characteristic of the speech of the *kāhins*, and is clearly a very basic first step toward verse. You will recall that it appears from time to time in Hebrew. Riddles and similar forms are written in it; and some long passages, such as Job, chapter 10, and Proverbs, chapter 31, show monorhyme, though incompletely. But among the Hebrews, there is no such limitation of it to messages dealing with the mysterious and the Unseen, as we find among the Arabs at the time of Muḥammad and just before. For the Hebrews, it seems to be simply a literary form—if we may speak of literature where written letters are not necessarily required. Among the Arabs, poetical form had fully developed, with all its variety of meters, and the primitive *saj‘* survived as the vehicle for only the most basic modes of poetry: the shamanistic utterances of the *kāhins*. But that this *saj‘*, in those early days, was fully recognized as a form of poetry

<summary>This page explains the Arabic poetic form known as saj‘ (rhymed prose), comparing its usage as a shamanistic tool for Arab soothsayers with its occasional literary appearance in the Hebrew Bible.</summary>

<page-num>31</page-num>

(*shi'r*) and not as such a separate literary form as it became in later *Islām* is perfectly clear.

The reason for this leads us directly to Muḥammad. What should we think of him as a literary artist? What literary form current in his time did he inherit? The answer is very simple and will be immediately clear to anyone who reads even a few lines of the *Qur'ān*, especially its older portions. The *Qur'ān* is written in rhymed prose throughout. The rhymed portions—verses, as we may call them—vary greatly in length. In the earlier chapters, these verses are short, just as the style is vivid and fiery; in the later chapters, they are of a burdensome length, prosaic and slow, and the rhyme often enters with a most absurd effect. It is very clear that Muḥammad’s first utterances were in the genuine *kāhin* form and *kāhin* spirit; they erupted from him as if under uncontrollable external pressure. Here is a curious narrative from the pagan times which provides an excellent picture of a *kāhin* under prophetic influence.

King Ḥujr, the father of the great pre-Islāmic poet, Imr al-Qays, had severely oppressed the Banū Asad and driven them from their territory. The author of the *Aghānī*—an immense collection of pre-Islāmic and early post-Islāmic history, legend, and song—then continues as follows in his biography of Imr al-Qays (Vol. VIII, 66):

Then the Banū Asad advanced until, when they were a

<meta>This page continues the narrative regarding the Banū Asad’s rebellion against King Ḥujr, using a specific historical account to illustrate the behavior and speech patterns of a pre-Islamic soothsayer (kāhin) during a prophetic trance.</meta>

<page-num>32</page-num>

A day’s journey from Tihāma, their *Kāhin*, who was ʿAwf ibn Rabīʿa, prophesied and said to them, “O my servants!” They said, “At your service! O our Lord!” He said, “Who is the king, the ruddy one, the all-conqueror, the unconquered, among camels as if they were a herd of gazelles, with no noise by his head? It is he! His blood is scattered wide! He, tomorrow, is the first of those stripped and plundered!” They said, “Who is it, O our Lord?” He said, “If my laboring soul were not troubled, I would tell you plainly that it is Ḥujr.” Then they all mounted, every beast—trained and untrained—and the sun had not yet risen upon them when they came upon the army of Ḥujr and charged his tent.

The story goes on to describe how the words of the *kāhin* were fulfilled to the letter, but we have no further interest in that. Our point is the manner and tone of this prophecy. The word which I have translated as “he prophesied,” *takahhana*, means “a prophetic fit came upon him”; it is evident that he, for the time, was beside himself. The form of his utterance is the rhymed prose (*sajʿ*) of which I have spoken—the language peculiar to the ecstatic life. You will notice he speaks to the people not as their peer, but directly as their God; they are his “servants,” or more strictly, “slaves.” They reply with the formula used only to a God: “At your service! O our Lord!” (*Labbayka yā rabbanā*). The phenomena of the double personality are most curious. At one moment, his voice is the direct voice of God; at another, he is hindered by his struggling and troubled human soul. The metaphor used is that of boiling water and high-running waves.

Now, all this is exactly paralleled in Muḥammad’s early utterances. They create pictures like this, and they are as though spoken by Allāh himself. His later utterances were shaped in this form only because he had begun that way. That was the manner in which prophets delivered their message; he had started in that way and had to continue it to the bitter end. Probably, if Muḥammad had been in a state to realize from the start everything that was implied by using this form, he would have done anything rather than use it. It immediately identified him with the *kāhins* as a class and—as someone possessed by a *Jinnī*, which was the only way his contemporaries could explain him—connected him directly with the old Arabian paganism and polytheism from which he was striving to break free. But the spirit came upon him in his hours of weakness and solitude, and naturally the form it took and its manifestations were those characteristic of appearances and workings from the Unseen in the world of his time. There can be no doubt that he was subject to fits of some kind. The narratives are too precise, and his own fears were too clearly genuine. His first thought was that he was possessed by a *Jinnī*—which, given his beliefs, meant an evil spirit—and only gradually did he become convinced that this was divine inspiration and not diabolical obsession.

But it is clear that these seizures, to which he was prone, and his general condition puzzled him to the

But one very singular group of traditions shows him puzzling over the case of a Jewish boy named Ibn Ṣayyād, who exhibited exactly the same phenomena as he himself did. Naturally, the subject is extremely obscure; the scholars of tradition have no liking for it. But for that very reason, these narratives may be taken as genuine. The boy had just reached puberty—that is, he was some twelve or thirteen years old. He was prone to epileptic or cataleptic fits, and during these, he was wrapped in a rough cloak¹ and lay muttering to himself. In this way, he was thought to have revelations and appears to have been regarded by the Jews of al-Madīna as a prophet of their own. One tradition is that Muḥammad met him playing with other boys, struck him on the back with his hand, and said, "Do you testify that I am the messenger of God?" He looked at him and said, "I testify that you are the messenger of the Gentiles." Then he continued, "I testify that I am the messenger of God." There is enough humor in this picture of one prophet trying to investigate another using the methods of the Society for Psychical Research, but for the boy, it was not a humorous situation. Muḥammad apparently satisfied himself that the boy was not dangerous. He became a Muslim and was still alive in the year 63 of the *Hijra*. But all his life, this suspicion followed him, and although one of his sons passed down traditions which are accepted,¹ he himself was ostracized. The poet al-Farazdaq once took refuge at al-Madīna and unwittingly entered the house of Ibn Ṣayyād; he found that the people would have nothing to do with him.² Other traditions³ show him complaining of this and pointing out that he was a Muslim with children, living in both al-Madīna and Mecca—none of which would be possible for the Antichrist. But others, again, show him with a certain malicious sense of his own importance and a fondness for scaring people. His diseased personality—without Muḥammad’s genius—is clearly depicted.

To return to Muḥammad, it is plain, as I said, that he recognized here phenomena similar to his own, but was gradually satisfied that no danger lay in them, regardless of how they were to be explained.

But, despite his greatest efforts to emphasize this distinction, his opponents called him a poet—clearly thinking not of the later artistic poet who wrote verses in correct meter (which Muḥammad was naturally quite incapable of doing), but of the ecstatic poet who was in contact with the Unseen. Or they called him possessed by a *<term>Jinnī</term>*, based on the same idea; or, what struck closest of all, a *<term>kāhin</term>*, or soothsayer. He was a *<term>kāhin</term>*, but with an enormous difference—the same difference that separated what I have called the "soil of prophetism" among the Hebrews (the mass of *<term>nebhî’îm</term>* with their ecstatic excitement, lacking ethical content or clear religious ideas) from the great reforming and constructive figures like Amos, Hosea, Isaiah, and Jeremiah.

But again I must caution myself: Muḥammad cannot be compared to these latter figures on any absolute scale. The comparison only holds in how both contrast with their common origins. What raised Muḥammad above those origins were two ideas: the duty to care for the poor through almsgiving and helpfulness, and the unity and absolute sovereignty of <term>Allāh</term>. The entire <term>polydaemonistic</term> scheme—in which a single God remained somewhere in the background—that the Arabs seem to have reached, vanished. No intercessor with that one God remained; there were no beings from whom revelations might come. When an angel spoke with him—<term>Gabriel</term>, the <term>Holy Spirit</term>, or whatever the term might be—there was no semi-divine personality involved. On one hand there was <term>Allāh</term>; on the other, His creation, including angels, <term>Jinn</term>, devils, and men. Even the concept of a shared nature with God, such as we find among the Hebrews in the <term>Benê Elôhîm</term> (the Sons of God), vanished with him. The angels were created from light—that is their only distinction. <term>Allāh</term> is enthroned alone—the Creator, Ruler, and Destroyer—and there is none like Him.

But having swept away in one stroke all lesser beings from whom revelations could come—having apparently closed the unseen world to man and fixed a gulf that none could pass—he bridged that gulf with another stroke and drew man immediately into the presence of God. God Himself, the One, reveals Himself to man through prophets and other means, and man, through prayer, can come directly to God. This is <term>Muḥammad’s</term> great glory. The conception of a fatherhood of God, of a genetic relationship, runs through the <term>Hebrew prophets</term> and breaks down His aloofness and separateness. On the one hand, the conceptions of a suffering God who has borne our flesh and knows its sorrows, and on the other hand, of a <term>Holy Spirit</term>—the immanent God who works in mankind—form the soul of the Christian church. But <term>Islām</term> can come to these only by breaking with <term>Muḥammad</term>.

As we shall see abundantly hereafter, the devout life within the <term>Muslim church</term> led to a more complete <term>pantheism</term> than the <term>Christian trinity</term> ever did. In the struggle to bring God and His creation together, the creation had to become an aspect of the Creator and finally vanish into Him. Only in this way could the crass <term>dualism</term> be overcome, and that <term>monism</term>—which is the basis or result of all <term>mysticism</term>—be reached. There are stray expressions which suggest that <term>Muḥammad</term>—a devout soul, if ever there was one, and a mystic in spite of his creed—was himself adrift on that sea and was nearing that shore. But his brain, oriental to the core, was never troubled by contradictoriness; <term>Allāh</term> could be throned apart in unapproachable grandeur and yet be near to every human heart. ## LECTURE II
# THE MUSLIM CONCEPTION OF PROPHECY AND SOOTHSAYING

It is time now to turn to the Muslims themselves and ask what they understand by prophecy—what a prophet is to them. In presenting an answer to you, I choose a statement not by a theologian but by a historian—a historian, it is true, with significant philosophical leanings. This is Ibn Khaldūn, who was born in Tunis and lived a restless life—part statesman, part scholar, part lawyer. He served on an embassy at one time to Peter the Cruel at Seville, and at another to Tīmūr in his camp before Damascus, and died in Cairo as chief justice in A.D. 1406. His great work was a *Universal History*, which, as a history, does not deserve much praise. But he prefixed to it an introduction, his justly celebrated *Muqaddima*, which was unique in its own time and for at least three centuries afterward. For an evaluation of it as a contribution to philosophical history, or rather as the foundation of that science, I refer you to Robert Flint's *Historical Philosophy in France and French Belgium and Switzerland*. Professor Flint was not an Arabist, but he was all the better able to evaluate the value of a book that alone would be enough to prove the scientific importance of Arabic literature. From this point on, I shall translate a significant portion

<summary>This page introduces the Muslim perspective on prophecy through the lens of the 14th-century historian Ibn Khaldūn and his influential work, the Muqaddima. The author highlights Ibn Khaldūn's varied career and his contribution to the philosophy of history.</summary>
<keywords>Ibn Khaldūn, prophecy, Muqaddima, Islamic history, Robert Flint, Tunis, Cairo, Universal History, Arabist</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of Ibn Khaldūn’s work, specifically focusing on his attempt to systematize supernatural phenomena and his psychological theories regarding the human mind.</meta>

<page-num>42</page-num>
<header>RELIGIOUS ATTITUDE AND LIFE IN ISLĀM</header>

...from it. It gives a very fair account of the final result of the centuries of theological development that preceded it—a result that remains to this day. In this work, Ibn Khaldūn voices the universal consensus of Islam.

You will observe that he is as absolute a supernaturalist as Muhammad himself or any primitive Semite. Yet he tries to reduce his supernaturalism to a system, if not entirely to rationalize it. He in no way denies the reality of those rifts in the shell of nature through which the unseen world touches us. But he tries to explain the method in such a way as to bring it into accordance with the processes we see occurring around us. On the one hand, this leads him to a strange semi-evolutionary doctrine which he borrowed from the Aristotelian philosophers; on the other hand, it leads to a doctrine of the human mind that seems halfway between phrenology and the more modern and fashionable hypothesis of "subliminal selves." In fact, I think that once you have heard what he has to say, you will agree with me that he had some very interesting psychological ideas, and that he would probably have been in close sympathy with Mr. William James’s *Varieties of Religious Experience*. His weak point is on the constructive side, and our modern speculations would have strengthened that. His different “souls” would pass easily into different "selves," but it may be that he would have had to invert their order. Yet his doctrine of the use of magic mirrors, crystal balls, and

<summary>The author examines Ibn Khaldūn’s effort to rationalize supernaturalism through a system that blends Aristotelian philosophy with psychological theories. Ibn Khaldūn’s ideas on the human mind are compared to modern concepts like subliminal selves and the work of William James.</summary>
<keywords>Ibn Khaldūn, supernaturalism, Aristotelian philosophy, phrenology, subliminal selves, William James, Varieties of Religious Experience, psychology, Islam</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Ibn Khaldūn’s theories, moving from his psychological views on tools like magic mirrors to his formal definition of prophecy and the role of the Prophet as a supernatural intermediary.</meta>

<header>PROPHECY AND SOOTHSAYING</header>

The same appears to be in absolute accord with our psychology.

He opens his section on inspiration and vision as follows:¹

> Know that God has chosen certain individuals from mankind whom He has graced with communion with Himself and has given such a constitution that they may know Him. He has made them to be the means of access between Him and His creatures, so that they may instruct people as to what is best for them, may exhort them to accept their guidance, may keep them from the Fire, and may guide them in the way of salvation. In that which God gives to them—consisting of knowledge—and exhibits in agreement with what they say—consisting of interruptions of the order of nature—there is also a narrative concerning things hidden from mankind. There is no path to the knowledge of these things except from God through their mediation; and they do not know it except through God’s instruction. The Prophet has said, “I know only what God has taught me.” Know further that truthfulness is an essential and necessary element in what they tell in that way, for reasons which will become plain to you in connection with the explanation of the essential nature of prophecy.

Here, you will see, is a frankly supernatural definition, and the purpose of prophecy is to save men from the Fire. This is one of the most puzzling paradoxes in Islam. As to recognizing, using, and enjoying this world, Islam is a most practical religion, but on its doctrine of salvation, it is absolutely and entirely other-worldly. Ibn Khaldūn then goes on to provide

<summary>Ibn Khaldūn defines prophecy as a supernatural phenomenon where God selects specific individuals as intermediaries to guide humanity toward salvation. This highlights a central paradox in Islam: its practical approach to daily life contrasted with its strictly other-worldly focus on the afterlife.</summary>

Polytheists used to accuse the prophets of being possessed by Jinn. They would say, “He has a familiar¹ of the Jinn.” But it was only because of what they had observed of the outward appearance of these states that the truth of the Prophet was obscured to them. “He whom God leads astray has no guide.”²

Because of its importance, I have translated this very closely. You will observe the acknowledgment of the practical identity in outward appearance between a prophet and one possessed by a Jinnī. From the traditions, I could quote many other descriptions of Muḥammad’s appearance under inspiration, but they would not serve any useful purpose. The individual traditions, with their details, are often suspicious, and it is practically impossible to filter them out. This is especially true of Muḥammad’s earlier life at Mecca, at a time when there can be no question of his honesty. But it cannot be doubted either that he fell into absolute trance conditions in later life when he was consciously manipulating his revelations to suit his purpose. It is of these later times that Ibn Khaldūn gives us here, as it were, a philosophical collective photograph. He was probably aided also by similar phenomena which he had observed among his own contemporaries. The feeling of weight and the choking in the throat seem to be characteristic of all phenomena of the subconscious life. A more

<meta>This page continues the discussion regarding the physical signs of Muhammad's inspiration, moving from Ibn Khaldūn’s theories to a modern scholarly analysis of his symptoms as potential evidence of medical or psychological conditions.</meta>

<warning>Thin diagonal scratch or hair across the lower half of the text area; text remains legible.</warning>

A precise interpretation of these symptoms has not yet been reached. Weil, in 1862, tried to prove that they pointed to epilepsy as opposed to catalepsy. More recently, Professor Margoliouth, in his *Life of Muḥammad* (p. 46), has argued the same, based on symptoms such as unconsciousness, the sound of a bell, the belief that someone is present, a resulting headache, violent perspiration, and others—such as turning the head to one side, foaming at the mouth, and the face turning red or white—all of which are characteristics of epilepsy. But as Sprenger (Vol. III, p. 65) rightly points out, the traditions are too contradictory to provide a certain basis. What is certain is the existence of some pathological condition in Muḥammad, resulting in trances, and it is not at all impossible that Sprenger's judgment (Vol. I. pp. 207 ff.)—that he suffered from some form of hysteria—may be correct. A more detailed examination, in light of recent investigations into nervous diseases through hypnotism, might reach more certain results. There are striking parallels with the descriptions of Mrs. Piper's appearance on entering and leaving a trance, which are scattered through the *Proceedings of the Society for Psychical Research*.

The narratives generally seem to imply that Muḥammad communicated his "revelations" only after he had come out of the trance condition. That may have been the case, but it must be regarded as

It reads, "Do not move your tongue in it <note>the revelation</note> to hurry it." This is exactly the caution that the conscious automatic writer has to observe, namely, that he does not consciously move his hand in order to hurry the process. That the words apply to Muḥammad's action in receiving revelation is a very old traditional exegesis.<gloss>1</gloss>

The second characteristic of the prophetic class, which Ibn Khaldūn gives, need not detain us so long. Even before inspiration, prophets have a good and pure disposition; they turn away from blameworthy things and uncleanness generally. This is what is meant when they are said to be protected against sin and error; it is as though they were created with a tendency to flee these things, which are incongruous with their nature. Stories follow describing how Muḥammad, as a child, fell into a faint when his body was exposed; how God cast a miraculous sleep upon him as a youth so that he would not go to a wedding and take part in unseemly sports; how he avoided garlic, etc., for the sake of his angelic visitors.

The third characteristic is that prophets call people to religion and devotion—that is, prayer, the giving of the poor-rate, and chaste conduct. Possessing the above three characteristics alone is enough to prove prophecy. Ibn Khaldūn recounts here one of those absurd stories of which *Islām* is so fond, and which seem to have sprouted up just as the apocryphal gospels did in early Christianity; or they are like our religious novels, with the difference that they were and are fully believed. Muhammad’s letter, summoning him to *Islām*, reached the Emperor Heraclius. So he summoned those of the Prophet’s tribe whom he could find into his presence, and among them was Abū Sufyān, the great enemy of Muhammad, and asked them about him, especially concerning what he commanded them. Abū Sufyān replied, “Prayer, the poor-rate, charity, and chastity.” Then Heraclius said, “If what you say is true, he is truly a prophet, and will rule all that is beneath these two feet of mine.” “This was enough proof for Heraclius,” comments Ibn Khaldūn; “he had no need of a miracle as evidence.”

The fourth characteristic is that the prophet must be a person of distinction among his people. No prophet has been sent, says a tradition, except those in a secure position among their people and in wealth. This is so that he may have a following and the power to defend himself against unbelievers until he delivers the message

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Ibn Khaldūn's characteristics of prophets, specifically addressing the prophet's role as a communal leader and the nature of miracles as divine interventions.</meta>

of his Lord, and finish the will of God in perfecting his religion and forming a community. This characteristic is clearly derived from the career of Muḥammad himself; it could in no way be obtained from either the Old or the New Testament. Also, the *kāhin* of pagan Arabia seldom held an assured position. But Ibn Khaldūn is perfectly right. This concept is an essential element in the Muslim idea of the prophet. He is not merely a voice preaching righteousness and proclaiming God, but the militant leader of a community claiming absolute sovereignty as a right.

The fifth characteristic of prophets is the occurrence through them of interruptions to the natural order, which bear witness to their truthfulness. These are actions that ordinary mankind cannot perform; they fall outside the range of a created being's power. There is a dispute, however, as to how they occur and how they prove the truthfulness of a prophet. The scholastic theologians generally maintain that they occur by the power of God, not by the action of the prophet. Even the heretical Muʿtaṭilites, although they generally say that the actions of a created being originate from the person themselves, say of the evidentiary miracle that it is not the action of a creature—in this case, the prophet. Furthermore, the scholastics maintain that the prophet has absolutely no part in it; he only uses it as a weapon, by the permission of God; that is, before it occurs, the prophet

--- Page 74 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Ibn Khaldūn's views on the evidentiary miracle, transitioning from how God permits the miracle to the specific criteria that define it as proof of prophecy.</meta>

<page-num>50</page-num>
<header>RELIGIOUS ATTITUDE AND LIFE IN ISLĀM</header>

states it as proof of his truthfulness in his claim; then, when it has occurred, it takes the place of a clear statement from God that the prophet is truthful; thus, its proof is absolute. So, an evidentiary miracle proves the truthfulness of a prophet by (1) being a disruption of the natural order and (2) being used in a challenge; this use is a part of it, or as the scholastics say, an essential quality. Further, this use in a challenge is what distinguishes the evidentiary miracle (*muʿjiza*) of the prophet from the miracle performed by a saint (*karāma*) and from magic. In the latter two, there is no need to prove truthfulness, nor is there any intention of a challenge, except by accident. If, by chance, a challenge occurs in a saint's miracle on the part of the performer of the miracle and has the weight of proof, it proves saintship only and not prophecy. Some have denied the possibility of miracles by saints, fearing this confusion, but Ibn Khaldūn thinks the distinction is clear. The Muʿtazilites, however, reject the miracles of saints because disruptions of the natural order cannot be the actions of a created being, since the actions of created beings are limited to what is customary. That the evidentiary miracle cannot be performed by a liar is proven as follows: The Ashʿarites, who are orthodox scholastics, say that the confirmation of truthfulness and right guidance are both essential qualities in an evidentiary miracle. Then, if it occurs without these—as it would if performed by a liar—the proof is ambiguous, the guidance into right

<meta>This page continues the theological debate regarding the nature of miracles, contrasting the Muᶜtazilite view on divine morality with the philosophical view of the prophet's innate power.</meta>

becomes a source of leading others astray, and the claim of truthfulness becomes a lie. Thus, the fundamentals are changed; the essential qualities are transformed. Therefore, its occurrence is impossible. The Muᶜtazilites simply say that for proof to appear as ambiguity and for right guidance to lead people astray is offensive and cannot be attributed to God.

The philosophers, on the other hand—meaning the students of Greek philosophy, Aristotelians, and neo-Platonists—hold that the disruption of the natural order in a miracle is an act of the prophet, even though that disruption is strictly beyond his own power. They base this on their general position regarding inherent necessity. The occurrence of events, following one after another, depends on causes and conditions; ultimately, everything traces back to the Necessary Being, the agent *per se* (by its own nature), acting not by choice. For them, the soul of the prophet has inherent characteristics, including the ability to produce these disruptions of the natural order by his own power, and the obedience of the elements to him in the creation of things. In their view, the prophet is endowed with control over things whenever he directs his attention toward them and has reached the proper maturity for that purpose. This is given to him by God. Thus, the disruption of nature on the part of the prophet occurs whether or not there is an intent to challenge others; it serves as proof of his truthfulness by demonstrating that he possesses that control over things which is unique to the prophetic soul. For the philosophers, therefore, the

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how philosophers differentiate between prophets, saints, and magicians based on the nature and morality of their supernatural actions.</meta>

<page-num>52</page-num>
<header>RELIGIOUS ATTITUDE AND LIFE IN ISLĀM</header>

distinction made for the sake of argument between the evidentiary miracle, the saint’s miracle, and magic fails. They distinguish the prophet from the magician by the quality of his actions. The prophet’s actions are good, while the magician’s are bad. Saints, on the other hand, have a more limited range of wonders. The prophet ascends to heaven, penetrates solid bodies, restores the dead to life, speaks with angels, and flies through the air. However, the saint can only multiply objects, foretell something of the future, and perform similar feats. You will notice that these philosophers were clearly working very hard to develop a scientific explanation for the beliefs of their time. We recognize this same phenomenon. According to Ibn Khaldūn, all of this—apparently meaning the entire doctrine of the miracles of prophets and saints—has been confirmed by the Ṣūfīs, the professional Muslim mystics, in the books concerning their discipline. For him, as we shall see, the mystical experience is the ultimate proof. Finally, the greatest and clearest of all evidentiary miracles is the *Qur’ān* itself. It combines both the thing to be proved—that is, the inspiration—and the proof; in this lies its uniqueness, the very self-evident soul of inspiration itself.

These five characteristics, then, are the outward signs of a prophet. But what is the nature of the prophet? What is this prophetic soul of which we have just heard? What is his role in the scheme of the universe? Here we enter deep waters, and I

<meta>This page continues the transition from the previous page's discussion on the nature of the prophetic soul, moving into the philosophical and cosmological framework used by Ibn Khaldūn.</meta>

<page-num>53</page-num>

## PROPHECY AND SOOTHSAYING

I must ask for your very close attention and also your patience with the strain I will place on the English language. Scholastic philosophy cannot be made very clear; and when it must be translated from Arabic, its complexity is doubled.

You will remember, of course, that for Ibn Khaldūn, the universe was the Ptolemaic universe—the universe of Dante and Milton—consisting of concentric spheres. He is also guided by a conception of the unity of nature and its processes derived from Neo-Platonism; all life is in a process of emanation, tending toward gradual specialization from the universal, yet always possessed by a longing to return to the one, perfect unity. This connects to the Aristotelian conception of nature as being filled with a vital impulse toward some higher manifestation, and of organic life as an ascending scale of complexity with humanity as the ultimate goal. While Aristotle regarded species as fixed, Ibn Khaldūn seems to have accepted the possibility and the reality of one species truly developing into another. He gained this from the Neo-Platonic influence, as well as the extension of that process into the spiritual world. A semi-pantheistic attitude is thus reached, which among many Muslims became absolute pantheism. For him, it is simply mysticism:

> "Know," says Ibn Khaldūn<note>Beirut edition, p. 95; Būlāq edition, p. 80; de Slane’s translation, Vol. I, p. 196.</note> "—may God guide us and—"

---

<summary>This page describes Ibn Khaldūn’s cosmological worldview, which blends Ptolemaic astronomy, Neo-Platonic emanation, and Aristotelian development to explain the unity of nature and the spiritual world. The author notes that Ibn Khaldūn, unlike Aristotle, believed species could develop into one another, leading toward a mystical understanding of the universe.</summary>

<keywords>Ibn Khaldūn, Ptolemaic universe, Neo-Platonism, emanation, Aristotelian philosophy, evolution of species, mysticism, pantheism, de Slane</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the translation of Ibn Khaldūn’s philosophical outlook, specifically his description of the "Great Chain of Being" or the hierarchical structure of the universe, moving from the physical elements up to humanity.</meta>

<page-num>54</page-num>
<header>RELIGIOUS ATTITUDE AND LIFE IN ISLĀM</header>

...you!—that we observe this world, with all the created things within it, to be in a structured arrangement and ordinance, a linking of causes to results, a joining of things to things, and a transformation of some existences into others; its marvels are unending, and its limits are unbounded. I begin, then, with the physical world, which is perceptible to the senses.

First, consider the world of the elements. We can observe how it ascends upward by steps, from earth to water, then to air, then to fire. Each is joined to the other, and each is prepared to change into that which is beyond it, both upward and downward, and they actually do change on certain occasions. The higher of these is always more refined than the one that precedes it, until the world of the spheres is reached. This is the most refined of all and exists in stages, one joining to another according to a scheme in which the senses can perceive nothing except their motions. Yet, by these motions, some people have been guided to a knowledge of their measurements and positions, and to the essences that exist beyond them, which are responsible for the effects seen in the spheres. 

Next, consider the "world of becoming"—this changing world of ours—and how it begins with minerals; then come plants; then animals, following a wondrous scheme of upward progress. The end of the mineral realm joins the beginning of the plant realm, such as grass and plants that have no seed; and the end of the plant realm, such as the palm tree and the vine, joins the beginning of the animal realm, such as the snail and shellfish, both of which possess only the power of touch. In the case of these created things, "joining" means that the final stage of one realm is curiously fitted to become the beginning of the realm that follows it.

The world of animals is wide and its species are numerous, and it extends in the development of this changing world up to man, the possessor of reflection and thought. The species mount up to him from this world which we perceive with our

senses, which include sensation and perception, but do not extend to actual thought and reflection. That marks the beginning of a region that extends from humanity onward, and here ends what we can directly observe.

Then we find differing effects within the various worlds: in the world of the senses, there are effects from the movements of the celestial spheres and the elements; in the world of change where we live, there are effects from the movement of growth and perception. All of these testify that there is a cause of these effects which is separate from physical bodies. This cause is spiritual and is in contact with the things of this world, because this world and everything in it are connected throughout. It is, therefore, the soul which perceives and initiates motion; and above the soul, without question, there must be another being that grants it the power to perceive and move. This being is also in contact with the soul, and its essence is pure perception and absolute rationality. This is the world of angels.

Therefore, the soul must necessarily possess the potential to be stripped of human nature and to take on an angelic nature, so as to actually become part of the genus of angels for a moment, on certain occasions. However, this can only happen after the soul's spiritual essence has been fully perfected, as we shall mention hereafter.

The soul also has contact with the regions beyond it, just like the other ordered existences mentioned above. In these contacts, there are two directions: upward and downward. Downward, the soul connects with the body and acquires through it sensory perceptions which prepare it to achieve the actual operation of reason. Upward, it connects with the region of the angels; through this, it gains the perception of divine knowledge and of the Unseen. For the world of events <note>this world in which things happen</note> exists within the intellectual operations of the angels, independent of time. All this, according to what we have said above, proceeds from the ordered arrangement which we
