Then, just as the powers of apprehension are arranged and ascend to their highest point, and to the height of the thinking power which is called the <term>logical soul</term>, so the <term>external senses</term> with their instruments of hearing, seeing, etc., ascend to the <term>internal senses</term>. of the wolf. Memory is for the storing up of all perceived and imagined things and is like a treasury for them, preserving them for times of need. The instrument for these two powers in their operation is the posterior hollow of the brain; the first part of it for the former power, and the latter part for the other.

Then they all ascend to the intellectual power whose instrument is the middle hollow of the brain. It is the power through which the process of meditation and the inclination toward intellectual processes occurs. Thus, the soul is constantly moved by this because of the longing within it for deliverance from the surveillance of the power that holds it back and from the constraints that belong to human nature. So, in its rational operation, the soul comes into action, making itself like the Heavenly Spiritual Host¹ and it enters the lowest of the ranks of the Spiritual beings when it perceives without bodily instruments. Toward that state, it is constantly moving and heading. Then, sometimes, it passes over completely from human nature and its form of spirituality to the angelic nature of the higher region—not by acquiring a new thing, but through the constitution and original creation in which God has made it.

And human souls are of three kinds. First, a kind too weak by nature to attain this degree. These souls are limited to motion in the lower direction, toward sensory and imaginary perceptions, and to the combining of ideas from the memory and to the power which forms opinions according to limited rules and specific arrangement. Through this, they acquire the sciences, both conceptual and affirmative, which belong to the intellect when it is in the body; all of these belong to the imagination and are of limited range, since, from its beginning, the soul extends to physical elements only and does not pass beyond them. If it is corrupt, then all beyond these is barred to it. This, for the most part, is the limit of human

<meta>This page continues the classification of human souls based on their capacity for spiritual perception, moving from the first kind (limited to bodily perception) to the second (saintly/intellectual) and third (prophetic) kinds.</meta>

<page-num>58</page-num>

apprehension in the body; to this, the apprehensions of the learned attain, and in it their feet are firm.

Souls of the second kind set out through that intellectual motion toward spiritual reason and toward the kind of apprehension which—on account of the equipment provided for them—has no need of bodily instruments. The range of their apprehension is wider than the elements that comprise the range of primary human apprehension, and they proceed freely to internal observations. These are a kind of ecstasy as a whole; they have a limit at their beginning but not at their end. They are the apprehensions of the learned saints, the people of the religious sciences and of divine knowledge; they are attained after death by the saved, in the state between death and the resurrection.

The third kind are created with the power of passing over from humanity—its flesh and its spirit—to the angels of the upper region, so that, for a moment, they actually become angels; in that moment, they witness the Heavenly Host in their region and hear spiritual speech and the divine allocution. These are prophets; God has created for them the power to momentarily pass over from humanity. And this is the state of inspiration—a constitution with which God has endowed them and a nature in which He has formed them. Through the character traits combined within them—consisting of the striving and uprightness by which they look toward God—and through the desire fixed in their natures for the service of God (unveiled in that looking and making its path easy), He has removed them from the hindrances and entanglements of the body, even while they remain joined with it through their human nature. Thus, whenever they wish, they set out for that region by means of that power of transition and that constitution with which they are endowed, not by any acquired skill or art.

Then, when they set out and pass beyond their humanity and encounter what they do within that Heavenly Host, they return to the channels of physical perception with that revelation to be sent down through these channels for the purpose of transmitting it to other creatures. So, at one time, one of them hears a humming sound, as though it were a hint of speech, from which he may grasp the meaning that has been brought to him; the humming sound does not cease until he has remembered and understood it. And, at another time, the angel who brings the revelation to him presents himself as a man and speaks to him, and he remembers what the angel says. The encounter with the angel, the return to the channels of physical perception, and his understanding of what is brought to him—all of that is as if it were a single flash, or faster than the blink of an eye. It does not happen within time; instead, it all happens together. Thus it appears to be swift, and therefore it is called inspiration (*<term>waḥy</term>*) because <term>waḥy</term>, in Arabic, means “hastening.” <note>But see page 252 below.</note>

Know, further, that the first state—the state of hearing a humming—is the stage of the prophets who are not messengers sent with books, as the distinction is made. The second state—when an angel appears like a man talking—is the stage of the prophets sent with books, and for that reason is more perfect than the first. This is the meaning of the tradition in which the Prophet explained inspiration when he was asked about it. He said, “At times it comes to me like the ringing of a bell, and that is the most difficult for me. Then it leaves me and I have remembered what was said. And at times the angel presents himself to me as a man and speaks to me; then I remember what he says.” The first of these was more difficult only because it was the initial step in passing from potentiality to actuality in reaching

the spiritual world. So he was under some degree of strain. Therefore, when he turned to the channels of physical perception in this state, they were limited to hearing; any other method would have been too difficult. But when the inspiration was repeated, and encounters with the angel occurred frequently, reaching the spiritual world became easy. Know, too, that in the state of full perception, there is a general difficulty and severity which the *Qur'ān* has pointed out. God has said, "Behold, we shall cast upon you a heavy word." She also said, "Inspiration would descend upon him on a day of bitter cold; then it would pass from him, and his brow would be dripping with sweat." For that reason, there used to happen to him, when in that state, such unconsciousness, roaring, and choking in the throat as is well known. The cause of this was that inspiration, as we have explained, is a separation from the physical nature for the sake of angelic perceptions and an encounter with the speech of the soul. Thus, a severity arises from the separation of the self from the self and its transition from its own region to that other region. This is the meaning of the choking that was said to occur at the beginning of the revelation, when he <note>Muḥammad</note> said, "Then he choked me until the pain reached its limit for me; then he let me go and said, 'Read!' I said, 'I cannot read'; and so it happened a second and a third time, as is recorded in the tradition."

<meta>This page continues the analysis of the prophetic state, specifically contrasting the circumstances and styles of divine revelation received in Mecca versus those received in Medina.</meta>

<header>PROPHECY AND SOOTHSAYING</header>

Mecca, were shorter than when he was in al-Madīna. Consider what has been handed down regarding the revelation of Chapter 9, which deals with the expedition of Tabūk: the whole or the greater part of it was revealed while he was traveling on his camel. In Mecca, on the other hand, only a part of a chapter—often a very short one—would be revealed at one time, and the rest would be revealed at another. Similarly, the last part of what was revealed in al-Madīna was the “Verse of the Religion”<note>¹ *Qur'an* 5:4.</note> and its length is well known, whereas in Mecca, very short verses used to be revealed. In this, there is a clue by which you may distinguish between Meccan and Medinan chapters and verses.

For Ibn Khaldūn, this then is the essence of the nature of prophecy. You will notice how careful he is to keep the concept of the unseen world vague. He undoubtedly felt our own reluctance toward an elaborately concrete heaven of the Miltonic and Dantean type. Beyond the veil, there is something from which ideas come in flashes to those whose natures are such that they can perceive them. The perceiver translates these elements of spiritual intuition into sensory terms—sight, hearing, and the like—because that is the only way he can make what he has reached intelligible. They do not come to the prophet through the senses, but he unconsciously renders them in sensory terms, and thus they are shared with the world. Ibn Khaldūn was compelled by the theology of the *Qur'ān* to speak of angels, but it is obvious that he much pre-

In no part of his major work does he deal with them at length, and his incidental references are always made only when necessary. There is a curious passage, however, found only in certain manuscripts in which he assigns the verses in the <term>Qur’ān</term> which mention revelation, angels, the Holy Spirit, and the <term>Jinn</term> to the technical category of “ambiguous verses” (*<term>mutashābihāt</term>*), those whose meaning we do not know with certainty. In this he stands alone among presumed orthodox <term>Muslims</term>; with others there is no trace of uncertainty as to the nature of the <term>Jinn</term>, as we shall see later.

He moves next to an analysis of the nature of <term>soothsaying</term>:¹

> That, also, [he says] belongs to the characteristics of the human soul. In all that has preceded we have seen that the human soul is equipped to pass from its humanity to the spiritual nature which is above it. A flash of insight comes to those of the prophetic class through their natural makeup; this clearly comes to them not through any learning, nor by seeking the aid of any sensory channels, nor through intellectual concepts, nor through physical actions in speech or movement, nor through anything else at all. It is simply a transition from the human to the angelic nature.

through innate constitution, in a flash, in less than the blink of an eye. Since that is so, and since that readiness exists in human nature in general, a logical subdivision follows. There must be another class of human beings who fall short of the first class to an extent that signifies a complete opposite; because failing to seek aid for that spiritual apprehension is the opposite of seeking aid for it, and how far apart these two are! Logical division, then, presents this other class of human beings, who have such a constitution that their reasoning power can be intellectually set in motion through a will aroused by desire, but who fall short because of a weakness in their nature. They, therefore, cling for help to specific things, perceived by the senses or imagined—such as transparent objects, animal bones, rhymes, words, birds, and beasts as they appear. So they wait for that sensory perception or imagination, seeking its help to bring about the transition to spiritual perception which is their goal; it serves them like a strengthener, and this force within these things that serves as a starting point for that spiritual apprehension is the essence of soothsaying. Yet because these souls have a defective constitution and fall short of completeness, their apprehension involves particulars rather than universals. For that reason, the supporting imagination in them is very strong, because it is the tool for dealing with particulars. It has free passage, then, through these particulars, whether waking or sleeping, and is present with them as a powerful helper, presenting them and acting like a mirror in which they are constantly seen.

But the soothsayer cannot completely attain the apprehension of rational things because his inspiration is Satanic. The highest state of inspiration that this class of men is capable of is reached by seeking help from rhymed prose and balanced speech, so that the soothsayer may be distracted from the senses and strengthened in some

Because of that mental agitation and the external factors that strengthen it, something enters his mind which his mind then conveys to his tongue. Sometimes he speaks the truth and aligns with the facts, and sometimes he lies, because he compensates for what he lacks within himself with something external to his perceiving self—something that is separate and unsuitable. The practitioners of this rhymed prose are specifically designated as *kāhins*, because they are the highest of this class. The Prophet said, in speaking of people like them, "This belongs to the rhymed prose of the *kāhins*." He thus identified rhymed prose as their specific characteristic. Also, he asked Ibn Șayyād <note>See more on page 66 below.</note>, investigating his condition, "How does this thing come to you?" Ibn Șayyād replied, "It comes to me in truth and in falsehood." The Prophet said, "The matter is confused for you," meaning that the defining quality of prophecy is truth, and it is never affected by lying. This is because prophecy is a union of the prophet's self with the Heavenly Host, without any helper and without seeking aid from external things. However, because the practitioner of soothsaying is driven to seek the aid of external perceptions, these enter into his awareness and confuse it; consequently, falsehood reaches him from that direction. Thus, his perception cannot be prophecy. We stated that the highest degree of soothsaying is the state of using rhymed prose. This is only because

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of soothsayers (*kāhins*) and their reliance on external stimuli and rhymed prose, contrasting their unreliable perceptions with the absolute truth of prophecy.</meta>

# PROPHECY AND SOOTHSAYING

The essential nature of rhymed prose is lighter than all the other methods seen and heard that are used to produce that effect. This essential lightness shows how close the attainment and apprehension of spiritual things are, and therefore, how comparatively slight the weakness is in that case.

<margin>¹ *Quran xv, 18.*</margin>
Some assert that this soothsaying has been stopped since the time of prophecy through the pelting of devils (*shayṭāns*) with shooting stars, which took place before the mission of Muḥammad. They argue that this pelting was intended to keep the devils away from knowledge of what was said in the heavens, as stated in the *Qur'ān*.¹ The soothsayers informed themselves about those matters only by means of the devils; therefore, soothsaying was nullified from that day forward. However, that argument is not valid as proof. The knowledge of the soothsayers came from themselves, as we have explained, just as much as it came from the devils. Also, the verse in the *Qur'ān* shows only that the devils were hindered from one specific kind of information said in the heavens—that which was connected with the mission of Muḥammad—and they were not hindered from anything else. Furthermore, this interruption took place only before the sending of the Prophet, and perhaps they returned afterward to what they had been doing. This is the plain meaning, because all these sources of knowledge were obscured during the Prophet's lifetime, just as the stars and lamps are obscured in the presence of the sun; for prophecy is the mighty light before which every other light is dim or fades away.

Some philosophers have asserted that soothsaying existed only before the coming of the Prophet and was then cut off, and that this happens with every prophet. They argued that the existence of a prophet involves a certain alignment of the spheres which requires a prophet's appearance. If that alignment of the spheres is complete, it requires the appearance of a prophet in his complete—

<summary>This page explores the debate over whether soothsaying was permanently abolished by the mission of Muhammad or merely overshadowed by the superior light of prophecy. It addresses the role of devils in gathering heavenly knowledge and mentions philosophical theories regarding the celestial spheres and the arrival of prophets.</summary>

<keywords>prophecy, soothsaying, devils, shayṭāns, Muḥammad, Qur'ān, shooting stars, celestial spheres, rhymed prose, Ibn Khaldūn</keywords>

--- Page 90 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the theories regarding soothsayers and their relation to celestial alignments and prophecy.</meta>

# RELIGIOUS ATTITUDE AND LIFE IN ISLĀM

ness. But if it falls short of completeness, it requires the existence of a nature of that same kind, only incomplete. That is the essential nature of a soothsayer, as we have explained. So, before that perfect alignment of the spheres is completed, the imperfect alignment occurs and requires the existence of one or more soothsayers. Then, whenever the alignment of the spheres is complete, the existence of the prophet in his perfection is complete, and the labors<gloss>1</gloss> which indicated such a nature are accomplished, for nothing of them is felt afterward. All this is based upon the idea that an incomplete celestial relation requires part of what would be its effect if complete; but that is not admitted. Perhaps, rather, the celestial relation requires such a thing only when its scheme is entire, and if any of its parts are lacking, it does not require anything; it does not require that effect imperfectly, as they say.

Furthermore, these soothsayers, when they are contemporaries of a prophet, recognize his truth, worthiness, and the evidence of his miracles, since they possess some of the ecstatic nature of the prophet, just as every man does in sleep. However, the rational nature of that relationship to the unseen world exists more strongly in the soothsayer than in the sleeper. The only thing that keeps them from admitting all that, and causes them to fall into denial, is the influence of their desire that this relationship might be prophecy on their own part. So they begin to oppose him, just as Umayya ibn Abī-ṣ-Ṣalt did; for he desired to be a prophet himself. The same happened to Ibn Ṣayyād, Musaylima, and others. Then, when the Faith triumphed and those hopes were cut off, they believed with the best kind of belief, as happened to Ṭulayḥa al-Asadī and Qārib ibn al-Aswad; both of these men, in the Muslim conquests, gave clear signs of belief.

<page-num>67</page-num>
<header>PROPHECY AND SOOTHSAYING</header>

This, then, is Ibn Khaldūn’s teaching on the *kāhins* and prophets. Their nature, in a broad sense, is the same. However, the *kāhin* required certain mechanical prompts to distract his ordinary consciousness and allow freedom to his subconscious self. If we account for the psychological influence that Aristotle had on Ibn Khaldūn—transmitted through the Muslim scholastics—we must admit that his theories align closely with modern doctrines regarding the functioning of the various aspects of the self. 

But you will also notice that Ibn Khaldūn does not address one specific point of contact between the *kāhins* and Muḥammad. He describes the use of *sajᶜ* (rhymed prose) as the “lightest” of the methods used by the *kāhins* to induce an ecstatic state, likely meaning that it was the simplest and least mechanical of those techniques. Yet, he gives no indication that the entire *Qur’ān* is composed in this exact same rhymed prose, or that in both form and spirit, Muḥammad belonged to the same category as the *kāhins*. 

In Ibn Khaldūn, this is a strange instance of conservative traditionalism. Earlier theologians had been extremely careful to hide the similarities between Muḥammad and the *kāhins*. Consequently, they had developed complex arguments to prove that the *Qur’ān* was not written in *sajᶜ*, and they claimed that applying the technical terms of *sajᶜ* to the holy book was an act of pure unbelief. Ibn Khaldūn clearly sees how closely Muḥammad and the *kāhins* resemble one another, but he has not yet reached

The author suggests this avoidance stems from traditional theological pressures to distinguish Muḥammad from the kāhins.</summary>

Another point, which we could hardly expect Ibn Khaldūn to notice, is the question of whether Muḥammad did, after all, have means of inducing the ecstatic condition, somewhat similar to those of the *kāhins*. The records are so sparse that we can only form hypotheses. Hypnotic parallels would indicate that he may have suggested the state to himself through this very use of rhymed prose. He seems to have disliked the use of it by others. It is also possible that while in the hypnotic condition—if it was hypnosis—he may have put himself under auto-suggestion for its return at a certain time. If we could trust the stories of his Meccan period in the slightest, this might explain the great break in his revelations when he feared that he had been deceived and deserted. The case may have been, simply, that he had not mastered the knack of suggesting a return to himself. His doubts, too, would give him a suggestion of the opposite kind. It is particularly unfortunate that the fullest descriptions of his state under inspiration belong to his latest period, and cannot be free from the suspicion that he was acting according to his accepted *role*. Still, however it may have been with the revelations which in many cases were too clearly manufactured or manipulated by himself, the ecstatic or hypnotic conditions in which he professed to receive them may

And it is always possible that what he passionately desired may have seemed to come to him during that state. The self-deceptions of Eastern ecstatics and mystics must be accepted as certain facts, though they are among the most puzzling problems in the history of religions. There will be much more on this later.

# LECTURE III

## THE MUSLIM CONCEPTION OF COMMUNICATION WITH THE UNSEEN WORLD IN SLEEP

Ibn Khaldūn now moves on to the doctrine of communication with the Unseen through vision or dreaming. This had been fully accepted by Muhammad, according to tradition, and here he was on more universally accepted ground than in his account of the *kāhins*. He develops his theory as follows:¹

The essential nature of Vision is that the rational soul, through its spiritual essence, briefly gains information about the forms of events. Since the soul is spiritual, the forms of events actually exist within it, as is the case with all spiritual essences; the soul becomes spiritual by being stripped of all material substance and the channels of physical perception. This happens to the rational soul from time to time for a moment because of sleep, as we shall mention. It thereby acquires knowledge of the future events for which it looks and returns with that knowledge to its channels of perception. Then, if that acquisition is weak and lacks clarity—due to the use of metaphor and imagery in the imagination to express it—it needs interpretation because of these metaphors. Sometimes the acquisition is strong and can do without metaphors; then it has no need for interpretation to clarify it from the imagery of the imagination.

The cause of the occurrence of this flash of perception in the soul is that the soul is potentially a spiritual essence, seeking

---

<note>¹ Beyrout edition, p. 102; Būlāq, p. 86; de Slane's translation, Vol. I, p. 211.</note>

<summary>Ibn Khaldūn explains that during sleep, the rational soul can briefly transcend physical limits to perceive future events. These visions may be literal and clear or symbolic and in need of interpretation.</summary>
<keywords>Ibn Khaldūn, Muhammad, visions, dreams, rational soul, spiritual essence, perception, imagination, prophecy, kāhins</keywords>

to fulfill itself through the body and its physical senses, until its essence becomes pure reason and it becomes actually perfect. In this way, it becomes a spiritual essence that perceives without any physical tools. Therefore, its category among spiritual beings is just below the angels—the People of the Upper Region—who do not need to fulfill their essential nature through any senses, physical or otherwise. The capacity leading to this perception belongs to the human soul while it resides in the body. There is a special kind of this capacity belonging to saints, and a general kind belonging to all of humanity. The latter is the basis of Vision.

Regarding the capacity of the prophets, it is the ability to cross over from human nature to pure angelic nature, which is the highest of spiritual things. This ability appears in them repeatedly during moments of inspiration. When the state of inspiration enters the realm of physical senses and perception occurs within them, it is clearly similar to the state of sleep, although sleep is a much lower state. Because of this similarity, Muḥammad said that a vision was one of forty-six parts of prophecy.

Ibn Khaldūn continues by explaining how some calculated this exact number by comparing the total twenty-three years of Muḥammad’s prophetic mission with the first half-year, during which his inspiration came only through visions. In his case, dreaming accounted for one-forty-sixth of the total time. However, Ibn Khaldūn disagrees with this. Other versions of the tradition provide different numbers, one even suggesting seventy. He clearly had our own objec-

<summary>Ibn Khaldūn describes how the human soul can ascend toward an angelic state of perception, noting that prophetic inspiration is similar to—though higher than—the state of dreaming. He also examines the traditional claim that visions constitute one-forty-sixth of prophecy based on the chronology of Muhammad's life.</summary>

What is meant is that dreaming and prophetic inspiration are essentially the same, though there is a vast distance between them.

> When this is clear to you [he goes on], you will know that the idea of proportion is the relationship between the primary capability, which encompasses all mankind, and the relative capability unique to the class of prophets, which belongs to their nature since it is a less common capability. And, although this capability is general to all mankind, many hindrances and restraints prevent its actual attainment. Among the strongest of these are the external senses. But God created mankind with the quality that the veil of the senses might be raised during natural sleep. Thus, at this raising, the soul encounters knowledge of that which it seeks—in the World of Reality (<term>ʿālam al-ḥaqq</term>)—and occasionally catches a flash in which the sought object is attained. Because of that, the Prophet called these moments of attainment “the Comforters” (<term>mubashshirāt</term>, “Givers of good tidings”). He said, “Nothing of prophecy remains but the ‘Comforters.’” They asked, “What are the ‘Comforters,’ O Apostle of God?” He said, “A true vision, which a righteous man sees, or which is shown to him.”

I will now describe to you the cause of this lifting of the veil in sleep. The perceptions and the actions of the rational soul occur only by means of the physical, animal spirit. It is a fine vapor whose seat is in the right cavity of the heart, as is stated in the anatomical books of Galen and others; it is sent out along with the blood through the arteries

<summary>Ibn Khaldūn argues that while prophetic inspiration and dreaming share a common spiritual root, the latter is a universal human capacity often blocked by the external senses. He introduces the physiological concept of the "animal spirit" in the heart as the medium through which the soul perceives and acts.</summary>

and veins, and gives sensation, motion, and all bodily actions. Its refined part rises to the brain; then the brain is warmed from its cold state, and the activities of the faculties within its chambers are carried out. Therefore, the rational soul perceives and reasons only through this vaporous spirit. It is joined to it only because of the law of nature, which requires that refined things cannot directly affect coarse things. Since this animal spirit is the most refined of all bodily substances, it becomes the site for the workings of an essence that is physically different from itself—in this case, the rational soul. Thus, the rational soul's activities are performed within the body by means of the animal spirit.

We have already stated that perception by the rational soul is of two kinds: perception through external means—namely, the five senses—and perception through internal means—namely, the powers of the brain. All of this tends to prevent the rational soul from perceiving the spiritual essences above it, even though it is naturally equipped to perceive them by its very constitution. Because the external senses are physical, they are subject to sleep and inactivity caused by fatigue; the soul becomes overwhelmed by too many tasks. Therefore, God created within it a need for rest so that its power of perception could be completely renewed. This occurs only when the animal spirit withdraws from all the external senses and returns to the internal sense. The cold of the night, which causes the body to grow weary, aids this process. Natural heat seeks the deeper parts of the body, moving from the exterior to the interior and accompanying its vehicle, the animal spirit. Because of this, sleep mostly comes to mankind during the night.

So, when the spirit withdraws from the external senses and returns to the internal powers, the obstacles and constraints upon the soul are lifted. It returns to the forms stored in the memory, and there present themselves—

themselves from the memory, by combination and dissolution, imaginary forms that are mostly familiar, because these forms are usually taken from frequent perceptions. Then the "common sense," which is the union of the external senses, brings them down and perceives them according to the different manners of the five external senses.

Even often, the soul turns aside for a moment to its spiritual essence, despite the resistance of the internal faculty, and perceives by means of its spiritual perception—as it was created to do—and acquires some of the forms of things [the ideas], which then become joined with its essence. Thereafter, the imagination takes these forms, thus perceived, and presents them either as they essentially are, or by metaphor in habitual patterns. The metaphors, in such cases, are what require interpretation.

On the other hand, the soul’s manipulating, by combination and dissolution, the forms given by the memory before it has perceived anything directly, produces the "confused dreams" <note>Literally "bundles of dreams."</note> of the Qur’ān.<note>Qur’ān 12:44; 21:5.</note> Muḥammad said, "Visions are of three types: a vision from God, a vision from the angels, and a vision from the devil." This division corresponds with what we have mentioned: the clear vision is from God; the metaphorical vision, which calls for interpretation, is from the angels; and the "confused dreams" are from the devil, because they are all futile, and the devil is the source of futility.

This is the essence of vision, of what causes it, and of the sleep which accompanies it. It is the unique property of the human soul and exists among people in general; no one is free from it. Every human being has seen in sleep what has occurred to them in waking hours, often more than once. And there has resulted for them the certainty that in sleep the soul perceives the unseen world. Then, since that takes place in the world of sleep, it must necessarily be

Most of what occurs to mankind in this regard is separate from their intention and outside of their control. The soul is merely looking for something; then the dream comes to it at that moment in sleep. It is not that the soul wills the vision and thereby sees it. In the books of those who have written about <term>ascetic</term> and <term>mystical exercises</term>, certain names are given. If they are pronounced at the time of going to sleep, a vision of what is sought will come from them. These are called *al-ḥalūmīya* <note>Apparently derived from the Hebrew *ḥălôm*, "dream".</note>. The author of a book of this kind has mentioned one of these, which he calls "the *ḥalūma* of the perfect nature." It is said that at the time of sleep, after the completion of <term>religious exercises</term> and with a fully focused mind, these foreign words should be pronounced <note>Here follow certain unintelligible combinations of letters, which are unpronounceable because the vowels are not given. They are probably of Hebrew or Syriac origin.</note>, and that the seeker should keep his need in mind; for in slumber, he will see the unveiling of the answer to his question. It is related that a certain man did this after preparing for several nights regarding his food and religious exercises. Then a form appeared to him, saying, "I am your <term>perfect nature</term>." The man then asked his question and was told what he had been seeking. To me, myself, strange appearances have come through these names, and through them, I have learned details about the circumstances I was investigating.

But that does not prove that seeking a dream produces it. Rather, it suggests that these names prepare the soul for a vision to occur. Therefore, the more prepared the soul is, the nearer it is to that for which it is prepared. It is up to the individual to make whatever preparation he pleases, but that will not guarantee the bringing about of that for which he is

<header>RELIGIOUS ATTITUDE AND LIFE IN ISLĀM</header>

prepared. Control of the preparation is not control of the thing itself.

Again, you will observe how modern Ibn Khaldūn’s position is. It is so modern that I have hesitated to use him as a representative of Islām and its attitudes. But precisely because he is so modern, his evidence is all the more overwhelming. In this case, for example, he has no shadow of doubt about the reality of true dreams. His classification of dreams, in general, is that of the whole Muslim world. But he feels the necessity of rationalizing it for himself. This is where his psychology—a physiological psychology as well—comes in. It is an innate quality of the soul that provides this power; a primary, unexplainable thing that must be taken for granted. Spells and exercises can only prepare the soul for the appearance of this power; they do not force the power out. In the same way, a hypnotic condition is induced, not caused, by mechanical means. One automatic writer, for example, begins invariably by writing the words, “Rome was not built in a day.” But the sentence is never completed; the suggestion is enough. With another, the first words written are, “Let me name your name,” or part of that sentence. Ibn Khaldūn would immediately recognize here the counterpart to his "barbarous gibberish" used at bedtime. It serves to put the rational soul in the state which enables it to free itself from the shackles of the senses and to exercise its own power of perception.

<summary>Ibn Khaldūn argues that while rituals and "gibberish" can prepare the soul for true dreams, the power itself is an innate, physiological quality of the rational soul. He compares these spiritual preparations to modern phenomena like hypnosis and automatic writing, where mechanical suggestions induce a state of perception beyond the physical senses.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Ibn Khaldūn’s psychological theories, transitioning from the preparation of the soul to his specific analysis of dream interpretation as a semi-sacred Islamic science.</meta>

<page-num>77</page-num>
<header>INTERCOURSE WITH THE UNSEEN IN SLEEP</header>

This becomes even clearer in the special section¹ which he has devoted to the science, or rather art, of the interpretation of dreams. People have always dreamed dreams, he says, and there have always been dream interpreters. But the Muslim <term>oneirocritic</term> science and art are unique to Islām and are not derived from any preceding system. This opinion is probably due to Ibn Khaldūn’s close connection of dreaming and prophecy; the interpretation of dreams must be semi-sacred and attributed entirely to Muslims. On general principles, however, we may be reasonably certain that their books are based on those of the Greek <term>oneirocritic</term> writers. And, in fact, we find in the *Fihrist*—a *catalogue raisonné* of Arabic literature from around 1000 AD—in the section (p. 316) on the interpretation of dreams, mention of translations of the works of Artemidorus and Porphyry the Neoplatonist.² The Prophet, he goes on, and his Companions all interpreted dreams. After morning prayer, for example, Muḥammad used to ask whether anyone had had a dream. Dreams, when properly interpreted, encouraged the hearts of his followers. And the amount of interpretation necessary would vary with the character of the dream. The rational soul, by its nature, has an absolute power of perception in the spiritual world.

<summary>Ibn Khaldūn argues that dream interpretation is a unique Islamic science linked to prophecy, though historical evidence suggests influence from Greek writers like Artemidorus. He notes that Muhammad used dreams to encourage his followers, asserting that the rational soul naturally possesses the power to perceive the spiritual world.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Ibn Khaldūn’s theories on the psychology of dreams, specifically detailing how the rational soul interacts with memory and imagination to create meaningful versus meaningless dreams.</meta>

<page-num>78</page-num>
<header>RELIGIOUS ATTITUDE AND LIFE IN ISLĀM</header>

The soul can exercise this perception during the sleep of the body and its senses. However, it only receives ideas in that way. Thus, it brings them back to the body through its apparatus of internal senses. Then the imagination takes these ideas and clothes them in images from the stores of memory that are suited to represent them. In this way, they are brought in sensory form to the “common-sense” of Aristotle and are thus perceived by the sleeper.

On the other hand, if the imagination brings images from the memory that are not informed by ideas from the rational soul, the sleeper sees dreams that have no meaning. Sometimes these are called Satanic dreams, as they are stirred up by Satan with the intention to mislead.

The task of the interpreter, then, is to work backward to the idea perceived by the rational soul in its own spiritual world through the images the imagination used to express it. This task is sometimes very simple, as the idea may be clothed in forms so direct that there can be no question of the meaning. Sometimes it is more difficult, such as when the clothing consists of metaphors: a snake, for example, stands for enmity, or a sea for a <term>sulṭān</term>. The sensory clothing will always be derived from the dreamer’s range of experience; he can never see a thing in his sleep that he has not seen while awake, though new ideas may be conveyed to him under these old forms. Therefore, the interpretation will depend

<summary>Ibn Khaldūn explains that meaningful dreams occur when the rational soul transmits ideas to the imagination, which clothes them in sensory images for the "common-sense." Conversely, meaningless "Satanic" dreams arise from the imagination alone, requiring an interpreter to decode metaphorical symbols based on the dreamer's experience.</summary>

upon a knowledge of the dreamer's range of experience; for different people, the same thing may represent different ideas.

Furthermore, true dreams have signs that attest to their truth. One is that the sleeper wakes up immediately, hurrying, as it were, under the shock to return to the realm of the senses. Another is that the impression of a true dream does not fade; instead, it imprints itself with all its details on the memory and cannot be forgotten. The reason is that true dreams are flashes of perception by the rational soul; they are not conveyed within time, have nothing to do with the physical workings of the brain, and are therefore not subject to the accidents of time or memory. In this regard, there is also a connection between true dreams and prophecy.

But now, leaving Ibn Khaldūn for a while, I must use a collection of examples to show you how absolute the Muslim trust is in this minor form of revelation. It is part of the paradox of Islām. Viewed one way, Allāh is enthroned far from His creation in unattainable glory, and there can be no contact between Him and it except through miracles, and even then only in ways beyond reason. But viewed another way, Allāh and the spiritual world are very close to every human heart. Every person has enjoyed, during their hours of sleep, some measure of inspiration and some access to that world. In this way, all people are prophets to some extent; and every person—unless through an evil life they have given the devil power to deceive them—

<summary>Ibn Khaldūn identifies the characteristics of true dreams as immediate waking and lasting memory, attributing them to the rational soul's direct perception. This reflects a broader Islamic paradox where God is both transcendently distant and intimately accessible through dream-based inspiration.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the Islamic trust in dreams as a minor form of revelation, transitioning from the theories of Ibn Khaldūn to those of al-Ghazzālī regarding visions of the Prophet and God.</meta>

by night as well as by day, can learn of God’s truth. But the matter goes even further. There are dreams on record—and the truthfulness of those who narrated them cannot be doubted—in which God himself was personally seen; dream-books even provide sections for the interpretation of such appearances. This was too common to be considered an eccentricity; it was part of the normal range of possibility. Here is what al-Ghazzālī, to whom I have already referred, has to say regarding its nature and reality.<note>*Al-Maḍnūn*, p. 5, Cairo edition, A.H. 1303.</note>

> Whoever does not know the true nature of vision <note>or dreaming</note> does not know the true natures of the different kinds of vision; and whoever does not know the true nature of the vision of Muḥammad and the other prophets—indeed, even of the dead in general—does not know the vision of God in a dream. So the average man imagines that whoever sees Muḥammad in a dream has seen his actual physical person. But just as an idea which comes to the soul is expressed by the imagination through a word, so every impression made on the soul has a form assigned to it by the imagination. Furthermore, how could there be a vision of the physical person of the Prophet in a dream, when that person has been committed to his grave at al-Madīna and has not left it to go to the place where the sleeper saw him? And even if we set that aside, the Prophet is often seen by a thousand sleepers in a single night in a thousand different places and in different forms. Instinct supports reason in declaring that one person cannot be seen at one time in two places, nor in two different forms. Whoever does not grasp that has satisfied himself, in the realm of reason, with names and descriptions instead of realities and ideas. After that, we need neither rebuke him nor speak to him.

<summary>Al-Ghazzālī explains that seeing God or the Prophet Muḥammad in a dream is not a physical encounter but a symbolic representation created by the imagination. He argues that the soul assigns forms to spiritual impressions, allowing the Prophet to appear to many people simultaneously in various locations.</summary>

<keywords>al-Ghazzālī, dreams, visions, Muḥammad, al-Madīna, Al-Maḍnūn, imagination, Islamic philosophy, revelation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of al-Ghazzālī's theories regarding the nature of spiritual visions, specifically focusing on how one can "see" the Prophet Muhammad or God in a dream without seeing their physical person or divine essence.</meta>

<page-num>81</page-num>

<header>INTERCOURSE WITH THE UNSEEN IN SLEEP</header>

But perhaps he will say that what he sees is the image (*mithāl*) of the Prophet, not his person (*shakhs*). Then it is either the image of his person or the image of his actual sanctified spirit, which is apart from form or shape. If it is the image of his person—which consists of bones and flesh—what need have we of that person? His person, in itself, is an object of the imagination and the senses. Therefore, whoever saw it after death, separate from his spirit, would not see the Prophet, but rather a body which used to move only when the Prophet moved it. How then can he see the Prophet when he only sees the image of his person? The truth is that what he sees is the image of the Prophet's sanctified spirit, the site of prophecy; he does not see his spirit, substance, or person directly, but rather his image in actuality.

But it may be asked, "What then does his saying mean: 'He who has seen me in a dream has seen me; for the devil does not make himself like me'?" It simply means that what the dreamer sees is an image acting as a link between the Prophet and himself, instructing him as to the truth. Just as the prophetic substance—that is, the sanctified spirit of the Prophet which remains after death—is free from color, form, and shape, and yet knowledge from it reaches his people by means of a truthful image possessing color, form, and shape, even while the prophetic substance itself is free from those things; so, similarly, the essence of God is free from shape and form, but knowledge from Him reaches the creature by means of a sensory image of light or some other beautiful form, suited to be an image for that essential, intellectual beauty which has neither form nor color. That image, then, is truthful and real, and acts as a link in passing on knowledge. So the sleeper says, "I saw God in a dream," not meaning that he saw His essence, just as he says, "I saw the Prophet," not meaning that he saw his essence and his spirit, or the essence of his person, but rather that he saw his image.

But it may be said, "The Prophet has a likeness (*mithl*), but

<meta>This page continues the discussion of al-Ghazali's theories on how divine and prophetic truths are communicated to humans through symbolic images (mithāl) rather than direct physical equivalents.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>82</page-num>
<header>RELIGIOUS ATTITUDE AND LIFE IN ISLAM</header>

"God has no equal." That ignores the distinction between an "equal" and an "image." "Image" is not a term for an "equal," for an "equal" is a term for that which is identical in all its qualities, but an "image" does not require such equality.¹ The reason is that although there is nothing equal to God, we can use the sun as an image [symbol] for Him because of their relationship in one specific point. Sensory perceptions are revealed by the light of the sun, and intellectual perceptions by reason. This level of relationship is sufficient for an image. In fact, a sultan may be represented in sleep by the sun, and a wazīr by the moon. They are not equivalents in form or concept. However, only a sultan holds universal rule and affects everything, and to that extent, the sun is related to him. And just as a wazīr is an intermediary between the sultan and his people in conveying the effect of just decrees, so the moon is an intermediary between the sun and the earth in carrying the effect of light. But these are images and not equivalents.

Furthermore, many dreams were presented to the Prophet in which milk, a cord, and similar objects played a part. He explained that milk meant Islam; just as milk is the nourishment of the external life, so Islam is the nourishment of the internal. And the cord was the Quran, by which we are drawn to safety. Similarly, God has no equivalent, but He has images resembling intellectual relationships to His qualities. Whenever we wish to teach an inquirer how God creates, knows, and wills things, and how He speaks—and how that speech exists in its own person—we use man as an image for all of that. And if man did not know the quality from his own experience, he would not understand the image in the case of God. An image is possible in God’s case, but an equivalent is false; for an image explains, but an equivalent resembles.

But it may be said, “What you have mentioned does not

<page-num>83</page-num>
<header>INTERCOURSE WITH THE UNSEEN IN SLEEP</header>

...lead to the conclusion that God is seen, or even to the conclusion that even the Prophet is not seen—for seeing a symbol is not seeing the thing itself—how, then, did he say, “Whoever sees me in a dream sees me”? This must be an improper metaphor meaning “as though he saw me.” We reply that exactly the same thing is meant when anyone says that he saw God in a dream. He does not mean that he saw Him in His essence as He is. For it is generally admitted that the essence of God cannot be seen, but that an image which the sleeper believes to be the essence of God, or the essence of the Prophet, can be seen. The existence of such dreams cannot be denied; even if an individual has not experienced them, unanimous tradition must compel him to believe in them. However, the image is sometimes truthful and sometimes false. When it is truthful, it means that God makes the vision a link between the seer and the Prophet to teach him something; it is within the power of God to create such a link between a creature and his discovery of the truth.

You will notice that al-Ghazzālī is essentially in agreement with Ibn Khaldūn, though he does not work out the psychology of the situation in such detail. What occurs in the brain of the dreamer is not his primary focus, but rather the divine working behind the curtain. But in the idea that dreams consist of ideas conveyed to the sleeper and clothed by him in familiar forms and, so to speak, dramatized, he agrees with Ibn Khaldūn.

Here now are some stories of Muslim dreams. You will find them marvelously clear and coherent, more suggestive of Du Maurier’s “*dreaming true*” than of our usual jumbles. But, just as with us,

Al-Ghazzālī’s perspective is compared to Ibn Khaldūn’s, noting their shared belief that dreams dramatize abstract ideas through familiar forms.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the theoretical framework of dream interpretation to specific historical examples, continuing the discussion of how Islamic scholars documented visionary experiences.</meta>

<page-num>84</page-num>
<header>RELIGIOUS ATTITUDE AND LIFE IN ISLĀM</header>

There are individuals who dream rarely, but when they do, it is with extraordinary clarity and coherence; it may be that Eastern people, simply because of their belief in dreams, may frequently achieve that clarity. Furthermore, I have been careful to select, as my illustrations, cases that we have from firsthand accounts.

The following would be admitted even by the Society for Psychical Research. The dreamer himself, Ibn Khallikān—a theologian, a lawyer, a grammarian, and a man of letters who died in 1282 AD—recounted it in his *Biographical Dictionary*,¹ for which an autograph manuscript is preserved in the British Museum. He says:

> I once saw al-Mubarrad in a dream and had a very strange encounter with him; so I wish to recount it. I was in Alexandria in the year 636 [AH] and remained there for five months. I had al-Mubarrad's book, the *Kāmil*, and the *ʿIqd* of Ibn ʿAbd Rabbihi with me, and I used to read them.

He then tells of a contradiction in the *ʿIqd* regarding a statement made by al-Mubarrad in the *Rawḍa* (another of his books), which interested him, and continues:

> A few nights after I had come across this passage, I dreamed that I was in Aleppo in the college of the Qāḍī Bahā ad-Dīn, known as Ibn Shaddād, where I had been a student. And it was as if we had performed the afternoon prayer in the place where it was customary for prayer to take place. Then, when I had finished my prayer, I rose to leave; but I saw at the rear of the place a person

standing and praying. One of those present said to me, "That is al-Mubarrad." So I went to him and sat beside him, waiting until he finished. Then he said, "Come and I will show it to you." So I went with him, and he went up with me to his house, and we went in, and I saw a great many books there. He sat in front of them, searching for it, and I sat opposite him. Then he took out a volume and handed it to me. I opened it and placed it in my lap. After that I said, "They have found something against you here." "What have they found?" he asked. I said, "You have accused Abū Nuwās of an error in such and such a verse," and I quoted it to him. "Certainly," he said, "there is an error there." "No," I said, "he was right, and they say that you were wrong to blame him." "How is that?" he said. So I told him what the author of the *ʻIqd* had said, and he bit the end of his forefinger and kept staring at me absentmindedly as though confounded. Then I awoke from my dream while he was still in that state.

Al-Mubarrad, I should mention, died in 898 AD, almost 400 years before Ibn Khallikān.

Another dream, to which Ibn Khallikān mentions briefly,¹ was of a manuscript, a single gathering, containing traditions handed down orally and traced back to a certain Surayj. In both cases, he seems to have had no doubt that his dreams were veridical; that he had had a conversation with al-Mubarrad, and had seen and read an actual manuscript.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the significance of dreams in Islamic tradition, moving from the literary anecdotes of Ibn Khallikān to the scientific and religious experiences of figures like al-Bērūnī and Nāṣir ibn Khusraw.</meta>

<page-num>86</page-num>
<header>RELIGIOUS ATTITUDE AND LIFE IN ISLĀM</header>

Ibn Khallikān is a thoroughly representative Muslim figure. But if it should be thought that a theologian and lawyer might be affected by superstition, take the case of al-Bērūnī, who died in 1048 AD—probably, and almost certainly, the greatest scientist of his time. He was a man of a thoroughly critical, objective mind, an astronomer, a chronologist, and a calm-headed student of custom and religion. Yet he had his dream, which he himself narrates.¹ It was on the last night of his sixty-first year, and he dreamed that he looked for the new moon in the quarter where it should appear. Then he heard a voice, "Leave the new moon alone; you are its son, one hundred and ninety times." This he interpreted to mean that he would live for another one hundred and ninety lunar months. That his actual life fell short by one month testifies, if anything, to the historical truth of the story.

Frequently, to return to the religious world, a dream is given as a reason for going on a pilgrimage to Mecca, and even for dedicating oneself to the religious life. Even more than in Christendom, theological conversions have been brought about among Muslims by dreams.

By such means, as he himself tells in his *Travel-Book*,² was Nāṣir ibn Khusraw turned from the

world. He was a secretary in the service of the state at Merv, and devoted to wealth and the pleasures it brings. In October 1045 (A.D.), he confesses that he took the opportunity of a favorable astrological situation to address to Allāh a special prayer for wealth. Under such circumstances, he believed it would be heard. Then he went to a neighboring town, and gave himself up for a month to wine. He was plainly in a completely unregenerate condition, and jumbled together religion, astrology, his worldly ambitions, and his pleasures. But one night he saw in a dream a figure which addressed him thus: “How long will you drink the wine that deprives man of reason? It would be better that you should return to yourself.” He answered, “The wise have found nothing better than wine to dissipate the cares of this world.” “The loss of reason and of your self-possession,” the figure replied, “does not give peace to the spirit. The wise cannot recommend to anyone to allow himself to be guided by madness; there is rather a need to seek that which will increase wisdom and inheritance.” “How,” he replied, “can I get it for myself?” “He who seeks, finds,” added the form, and indicated with a gesture the direction of Mecca.

This dream changed his life. However his own psychological condition may have been prepared, there was no question in his mind of the suddenness with which his conversion came. With the morn-

morning, he determined to give up everything for which he had lived for forty years. He resigned his secretaryship; he abandoned his wealth, except for what was needed for the journey; and on March 6, 1046, he set out from Merv on a pilgrimage to Mecca. Thereafter, his life was that of a wandering religious person, and he died as a hermit in the mountains of Badakhshān in 1088. He is in many respects a perplexing personality, and legend has cast around him a halo of mingled miracle and heresy, but the great fact of this sudden conversion is certain.

Another sudden conversion by similar means happened to al-Ashʿarī, the founder of the Ashʿarite system of scholastic theology, which has now been dominant for 800 years in the Muslim church. He had been brought up as a Muʿtazilite—that is, in the heretical school which denied, on rationalistic principles, such doctrines of Islām as the belief that the *Qurʾān* was the Eternal Word of God, that God would be seen by the believers in Paradise, and that God created all the actions of His creatures. Generally, they applied logical argument to theology and did not, like the orthodox Muslims, content themselves with statements of the faith of the forefathers as derived from the *Qurʾān* and the personal words of Muḥammad.

As a Muʿtazilite, then, al-Ashʿarī lived, taught, and fought for thirty or forty years. But he came from the blood of the desert, and with the Semitic consciousness for direct and mandatory faith, he

His soul was yearning within for a direct “Thus says the Lord!”—a voice of authority and peace. He was on his way to a spiritual crisis, which came one Ramaḍān in his snatches of sleep, wearied with fasting and prayer. Then I saw the Prophet, and he said to me, “What have you done regarding that which I commanded you?” I said, “I have abandoned theological reasoning and applied myself to the Book of Allāh and to the record of your sayings and doings.” But he said to me, “Did I command you to abandon theological reasoning? I commanded you only to support the tenets handed down from me, for they are true.” Then I said, “O Apostle of God, how can I leave the tenets whose elements I have clearly understood and whose proof I have known these thirty years for a dream?” He said to me, “If I did not know that God will give you special aid from Himself, I would not rise from your side until I had explained those positions to you. And, since you consider this visit of mine to be a mere dream, was my seeing Gabriel a mere dream? You will not see me in this fashion hereafter; so apply yourself to these things, for God will give you special aid from Himself.” Then I awoke and said, “After the truth there is nothing but straying.” And I began to defend the traditions dealing with dreaming and the intercession of the Prophet.

That al-Ashᶜarī had some such dreams as these and with such consequences I have no doubt, although, unfortunately, the precise form is left uncertain to us. Beyond doubt, too, is the momentous character of these dreams; they marked a turning point in the religious history of Islām. This was what is called "the return of al-Ashᶜarī" from the Muᶜtazilites to the orthodox, bringing back with him the weapons of scholastic disputation which had before been found among the Muᶜtazilites only. From his time on, the orthodox defended their faith with syllogisms as well as traditions; this occurred in the year 300 of the Hijra, <term>A. D.</term> 913.

Thereupon the Prophet said, "Bring the whips." So the man was beaten because of al-Ghazālī. Then the man woke from sleep, and the marks of the whips remained on his back; and he used to weep and tell the story.

now sufficiently made. The means of access to the unseen world open to all—the universal crack in the shell of which I spoke—is the faculty of dreaming. All members of the Muslim world—orthodox, heretical, unbelieving, theologians, philosophers, and the common person—believed and continue to believe in dreams. There is a book, not nearly as well known as it should and could be for its varied interest and its vivid picture of its author, his adventures, and the world in which he lived. It is the account of his life and travels, dictated in 1355 A.D. at Fez by Ibn Baṭūṭa, after his return from twenty-eight years and more than 75,000 miles of wandering. He is a much more talkative, free-spoken, and more widely traveled version of Marco Polo, and he is almost as trustworthy an observer and describer. In certain ways, he has a marked kinship to Pepys <note>Samuel Pepys, the famous 17th-century English diarist</note>. I shall have to use his book hereafter, but, in the meantime, you will find in its pages—there is a good edition of the Arabic text, with a fairly adequate French translation by Defrémery and Sanguinetti—numerous cases of dreams very much relevant to our current point. For him, the crack in the shell was even wider than usual.

# LECTURE IV

## OTHER MEANS OF INTERCOURSE: WIZARDS, MAGIC, TALISMANS; UTILITARIANISM IN ISLAM

We can now return to Ibn Khaldūn’s philosophical reflections. In the following terms¹ he makes a fresh attempt to grapple with our connections with the Unseen.

We find among men certain individuals who provide information about events before they take place, due to a nature within them by which their kind is distinguished from other men. In this, they do not resort to an art; nor do they draw inferences from an influence exercised by the stars and the like; we simply find that they have channels of apprehension dealing with such matters which are necessarily involved in the constitution with which they have been endowed. Such are wizards and those who gaze into transparent bodies, like mirrors and cups of water, and those who gaze on the hearts, livers, and bones of animals, and those who divine by birds and wild beasts, or who cast pebbles, grains of wheat, and date stones. All these exist in the human world; it is not possible for anyone to deny them. Similarly, the insane have words from the Unseen cast upon their tongues, and they speak them. Similarly, a sleeper, when he has just fallen asleep, and a dying man, when he has just died, speak concerning the Unseen. So, too, it is with ascetics, that is, the <term>Ṣūfīs</term>. They have well-known channels of information regarding the Unseen, by way of grace from God (<term>karāma</term>). We will now speak about all these.

---

<note>¹ Beirut edition, p. 105; Būlāq, p. 89; de Slane's translation, Vol. I, p. 218.</note>

<summary>Ibn Khaldūn categorizes various ways humans access "the Unseen," ranging from innate divination and wizardry to the miraculous insights of Sufis. He argues that these phenomena are inherent to human nature and cannot be dismissed as mere superstition.</summary>
<keywords>Ibn Khaldūn, divination, wizards, Sufis, karāma, the Unseen, mysticism, Islamic philosophy, Beirut edition, Būlāq edition, de Slane</keywords>

--- Page 120 ---
<meta>This page continues Ibn Khaldūn’s philosophical exploration of how the human soul connects with the Unseen, moving from the types of people who possess this gift to the psychological and spiritual mechanics of the soul itself.</meta>

<page-num>96</page-num>
<header>RELIGIOUS ATTITUDE AND LIFE IN ISLĀM</header>
<page-type>text</page-type>

modes of perception, beginning with soothsaying and taking up the rest, one by one.

But first, an introduction on the way in which the human soul is equipped to perceive the Unseen in all these ways. The soul is the essence of a spirituality that exists potentially and is distinguished from other spiritualities as we have mentioned above; it becomes actual only within the body and its various states. Everyone perceives that. And everything that is potential has a substance and a form. The form of the soul, by which its existence is complete, is perception itself and rational thought. It exists, in the first instance, in potentiality—equipped to perceive and receive forms, both universal and particular. Then, its growth and existence become complete in actuality by being joined with the body and through the arrival of sensory perceptions to which the body accustoms it, and through the universal ideas which are drawn from these perceptions. So it rationally considers the forms, again and again, until it acquires in actuality a form consisting of perception and rational thought, and so its essence is completed. The soul, then, is like matter, and forms alternate upon it through perception, one after the other.

On that account, we find that a child, at the beginning of its growth, is not able to perceive that which belongs to the soul essentially—neither in sleep, nor by revelation, nor otherwise. That is because its form, which is its very essence—namely perception and rational thought—is not complete; indeed, even the extraction of universals cannot be completely carried out.

Then, whenever its essence is actually complete, it comes to have, so long as it is together with the body, two kinds of perception. One kind is through the instruments of the body, which the bodily channels of perception bring to it, and one kind is through its essence, without any intermediary.

<vocab>perception, soul, the Unseen, spirituality, potentiality, actuality, rational thought, universals, revelation</vocab>

<summary>Ibn Khaldūn describes the soul as a spiritual potential that achieves actuality through the body and sensory experiences. Once fully formed, the soul can perceive the world both through bodily senses and directly through its own essence.</summary>
<keywords>Ibn Khaldūn, soul, perception, potentiality, actuality, rational thought, sensory perception, the Unseen, universals</keywords>

--- Page 121 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of how the soul is equipped to perceive the Unseen, explaining the transition from physical sensory experience to spiritual apprehension.</meta>

<page-num>97</page-num>
<header>WIZARDS IN ISLĀM</header>

It is separated from the second kind by being immersed in the body, in the senses, and in their preoccupations. The senses are always drawing the soul toward what is outside through the physical perception that is their primary nature. But often it plunges from the external into the internal, and the veil of the body is lifted for a moment—either through a quality that belongs to humanity in general, like sleep; or through a quality that exists in certain men, like soothsaying and divining with pebbles; or through a discipline, such as that which gives *Ṣūfīs* their revelations. Then, the soul turns to the essences that are above it—the heavenly host—because of the connection that exists between its realm and theirs, as we have shown above. These essences are spiritual and consist of absolute perception and actual intelligence; within them are the forms and essentials of existence, as previously mentioned. Then, something of these forms shines out within them, and the soul acquires knowledge from them. Often, those perceived forms are carried back to the imagination, which shapes them into familiar forms. Then, these perceptions are brought to the senses, either in their simple state or in the shapes of the imagination, and so they are reported. This is an explanation of how the soul is equipped for the perception of the Unseen.

Let us return to the promised explanation of the various types of this perception. Those who gaze into transparent objects,¹ such as mirrors, cups of water, or the hearts, livers, and bones of animals, and those who divine with pebbles and seeds, are all of the same kind as the *kāhins*. However, they are weaker in their fundamental nature because the *kāhin* does not need much assistance to lift the veil of the senses, whereas these others seek assistance by narrowing all the channels of sensory perception down to only one. The noblest

one of these channels is sight, so sight is concentrated upon an object with a uniform surface (<term>mar'ī basīṭ</term>), until it achieves a perception, through sight, of that about which it must provide information. And often it is thought that those who observe see what they see in the surface of the mirror, but that is not so. They simply continue gazing at the surface of the mirror until it vanishes from their sight, and then there appears, between them and the surface of the mirror, a curtain, as though it were a cloud, on which forms show themselves. These are the things which they perceive, and thus they can indicate what is desired to be known, either negatively or affirmatively. So they report about it just as they have perceived it; but as for the mirror and the forms which were supposedly perceived in it, they did not really perceive these in that way. There only developed for them, through it, this other kind of perception, which belongs to the soul and is not really perception by sight. The spiritual perceiver forms his perception only as though it were according to the senses.

The case is the same with those who gaze on the hearts and livers of animals and water in cups and so on. Some of these, we have observed, distract the senses by means of vapors only, or by spells by way of preparation. Then they tell what they have perceived. They maintain that they see forms shaped in the air, telling them by symbols and indications the things a knowledge of which is desired. The detachment of these last from the influence of sense is less than in the case of the first. The world is full of marvels!

There follows a section on those who draw omens from the flight of birds, etc.; and then another, of more interest, on divination through the insane:

The rational souls of these individuals have a weak connection with the body, because their constitution is, for the most part, disordered, and the animal spirit is weaker in them. So the

<header>WIZARDS IN ISLĀM</header>

The soul of one of these individuals, because of the pain caused by what he lacks and the disease that distracts his senses, is not fully plunged and submerged in the senses. Often, another satanic spirit presses the soul to join it, clinging to it; and because that soul is too weak to drive it off, it is convulsed by this. Then, when this convulsion has taken place—either through an essential disorder in the physical constitution or by the pressure from satanic spirits that join it—the madman loses contact with his senses completely and perceives a flash from the world of his soul, and sensory forms are impressed upon it. The imagination then transforms these; and often, in that state, he speaks from his tongue alone, without intending to speak. <note>These phenomena would now be called technically “possession” and “automatic speech.”</note> The perception of all these people contains a mixture of truth and falsehood because their contact with the spiritual world occurs—even though they have lost contact with their senses—only after they have sought help from external things, as we have explained. This is the source of the false element in these appearances.

There follows his opinion of those whom he calls wizards (<term>ʿarrāf</term>). These people claim to have a connection with the Unseen, but they are actually guided by their intelligence and by free conjecture. They use, as a basis, the opinions they have gained from the first stages of this connection with the spiritual world and their understanding of it, and they claim by that a knowledge of the Unseen. He further refers by name to some of the most celebrated <term>kāhins</term> (soothsayers) of the pagan Arabs and to their wizards; then he goes on to speak of the approach to the Unseen at the beginning of sleep, and at the first moments of death;

--- Page 124 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the detachment of the soul from the senses, moving from involuntary states like automatic speech to intentional, often gruesome, methods used to perceive the future or the spiritual realm.</meta>

<page-num>100</page-num>
<header>RELIGIOUS ATTITUDE AND LIFE IN ISLĀM</header>

...how the tongue then speaks without intention—again, automatic speech—when the veil of sense has been lifted. Thus, some tyrannical rulers used to seize men and kill them so that they might learn from their speech, at the moment of death, what the future would bring. He further provides a gruesome recipe for how to dissolve a living man in a barrel of oil until nothing remains of him but the veins and sutures of his head. The head would then answer questions about the future. He condemns this as being among the actions of magicians, but accepts it as proof of the wonders of the human structure. This story of the head that answers questions, I should mention, is very widely spread in Islam, but mostly as a tale of ancient magic. It was apparently localized among the pagans of Ḥarrān in North Syria and connected with their star worship. Within Islam itself, it was probably never practiced.

Others sought to achieve the same results by bringing about an artificial death—the phrase is Ibn Khaldūn's. Through ascetic exercises and discipline, they sought to destroy all physical powers; then to erase their effects upon the soul; and then to nourish the soul through religious exercises and increase its internal power. When this death descended upon the body, then the senses and their veil were removed, and the soul could reach the Unseen. Others strove to reach the same end through magical disciplines; these were found especially in the more remote regions of the earth.

<summary>This page describes various methods mentioned by Ibn Khaldūn for accessing the Unseen, ranging from the gruesome use of deathbed speech and severed heads to the practice of "artificial death" through asceticism. These practices aim to remove the sensory veil to allow the soul to perceive spiritual realities.</summary>

<page-num>101</page-num>

and in India. Ibn Khaldūn had heard of the yogis and of the extensive Indian literature on this subject. Ibn ‘Arabī, a great mystical writer who died in 1240, revised a translation of one of these Indian texts with the help of a yogi.<note>¹ Brockelmann, *Arabische Litteratur*, Vol. I, p. 446.</note>

The traveler, Ibn Baṭūṭa, whom I have already mentioned, also had strange experiences with yogis in India and China. He observed them closely and seems to have had no doubt about the reality of their feats, but he shows a tendency to regard them as secret Muslims. Only a Muslim saint could perform such wonders. Some of their miracles affected him with heart palpitations, so that he fainted; but the performance was suspended until he recovered and could watch it through to the end. He identifies them as yogis (<term>jūkīya</term>) and is evidently a reliable witness.

Ibn Khaldūn next discusses the Ṣūfīs, or Muslim mystics. He treats them twice in his book: once here, where he specifically considers their interaction with the Unseen, and later, in a longer and more general article on their origins, history, and beliefs. In this section, he begins by saying that their system of discipline is religious and free from the shameful motives mentioned above. Their sole objective is to draw close to God, so that they may attain those pleasures that belong to the people who truly know God and enter into union with Him.

<summary>This page discusses Ibn Khaldūn's and Ibn Baṭūṭa's observations of Indian yogis and their supernatural feats, followed by an introduction to the Ṣūfīs' religious discipline. It distinguishes the Ṣūfīs' spiritual goal of union with God from the more questionable or magical practices previously described.</summary>

The essence of their discipline is starving the body and feeding the soul with meditation upon God; for when the soul develops in this way, it grows ever nearer to the knowledge of God; and when it is deprived of this meditation, it becomes satanic. Whatever comes to the Ṣūfīs by way of knowledge and control of the Unseen is only accidental; it is not a primary objective. If it were an objective, then their aim would be something other than God, and that would be equivalent to polytheism. One of them has said, “He who prefers (mystical) knowledge for the sake of (mystical) knowledge believes in the second”: that is, mystical knowledge and not God himself.

Their aim, then, is God and nothing else; when anything else occurs, it is by accident only; and many of them avoid such things when they present themselves and do not heed them. Still, the fact that such things come to them is well known and is disapproved of by only a few theologians, who fear that the miracles of the saints might be confused with the evidentiary miracles of the prophets.

Ibn Khaldūn proves the fact that the saints possess such miracles, however, by using narratives from the Companions of the Prophet. Thus the story goes that ‘Umar, the second Khalīfa, was preaching one day at al-Madīna when he suddenly stopped in his sermon and cried out, “O Sāriya, the hill! the hill!” Sāriya was a Muslim general, at that time, in
