"Many other such things happened," says Ibn Khaldūn, "in the time of the Companions, and, after them, among the pious. In the lifetime and especially in the presence of the Prophet, such things were few, and even now, when the student of Ṣūfism comes to al-Madīna, his ecstatic states cease, as long as he remains there." Apparently, saints are a substitute for a prophet; therefore, in the lifetime and environment of a prophet, the specific character of sainthood is not displayed. Further, according to many theologians, Muḥammad is not entirely dead, but lives, in a sense, in his tomb at al-Madīna. Immediately around it, therefore, the miracles and ecstatic states of saints do not appear.

What Ibn Khaldūn has to say in general about the Ṣūfīs we may leave until later; only one class of them requires notice now. These are "idiots" <note>A reference to the *majdhūb* or "holy fool," individuals who are mentally impaired but believed to possess spiritual insight.</note>, who are mentally deranged like the insane, but who show along with that clear proofs of sainthood. Ibn Khaldūn is of the opinion that whoever with sound understanding knows their manner of life will recognize this, although they do not fulfill the external duties of the law. In them, wonderful things occur by way of stories of the Unseen, for they are not limited by anything, and they give their speech full

course there. The canon lawyers sometimes deny that they are saints at all because of that neglect of the external rituals of the law which is seen in them, and because it is held that sainthood comes only through devotional exercises. But that is an error, for the grace of God comes to whomever God wills, and the attainment of sainthood does not consist of devotional exercises alone. Since the human soul is imperishable, God endows it with whatever of His gifts He wills. In idiots, the <term>logical soul</term> is not lacking; nor is it corrupt as in the case of the insane. It is only their reason which fails; and it is to that reason that the duty of the external observance of the law is attached. Ibn Khaldūn adds some scholastic reasoning on this point which we need not notice; his meaning is sufficiently clear. He warns, however, that these <term>idiot-saints</term> are to be carefully distinguished from the insane, whose logical souls have become corrupt, and who are, therefore, like the lower animals.

To distinguish them, however, there are certain signs. First, you will find in these idiots a distinct turn for religious meditation and devotion, although not according to legal conditions, because they are not under the law; the case is different with the insane. Secondly, they were created in idiocy, while insanity happens to the insane after a portion of their life has passed, because of bodily or physical accidents. When this has happened to them, their logical

### GEOMANCY IN ISLĀM

soul is corrupted, and they act without reason or sequence. And, thirdly, they often occupy themselves with people, for both good and evil; for they do not wait for permission, as there is no legal obligation in their case. The insane, however, have no such concern regarding others.

Ibn Khaldūn then discusses some ways of reaching the Unseen without discarding the veil of the senses. He briefly rejects the claims of astrologers, referring to his more detailed examination elsewhere. He describes the art of geomancy—that is, divining the future by arranging dots in sand or on paper—more fully. Like all such arts, it claimed a prophetic origin, and to prove its legality, a tradition from Muḥammad was cited that seems to be a distant echo of the pericope of the adulteress in the Gospel of John. We do not need to examine it here, and I will only refer you to a note in Mr. John Payne’s translation of *Alaeddin* (pages 199 and following), which contains the only description of this art that I know of in English.<note>1</note> In the end, Ibn Khaldūn concludes briefly that such arts cannot possibly reach the Unseen, and that these dots, for example, can only help toward that goal when they blur the sense perception of the person using them. The only basis for reaching the Unseen lies in the nature of the human soul itself. And here he adds a remark showing that many falsely claimed to possess

this power, and that he himself had a keen feeling of the need for some rational criterion:

The sign <note>he says</note> of the constitution which those have who can apprehend the Unseen, is that there comes upon them, when they turn to those things, a passing out from their natural state, such as yawning and stretching, and the beginnings of a loss of sense-perception. This varies in strength and weakness, just as this constitution varies among them. Anyone, however, in whom this sign is not found has no perception of the Unseen and is only trying to make money from lies.

Finally, Ibn Khaldūn mentions a category of efforts to reach the Unseen based neither on the—to him, certain—properties in the spiritual soul, nor on the hypothesis of the influence of the stars, nor on the conjectures and fancies of wizards, but on supposed powers residing in combinations of numbers and letters. Ibn Khaldūn rejects these absolutely, but he has found it necessary to state and expose them at considerable length. I do not need to follow him here. In part, they exhibit a curious lack of simple arithmetical power among the Muslim peoples, which has made them rely on Copts and Armenians as calculating machines. An arithmetical problem which involves nothing more than a simple proportion, or at the most requires a simple equation for its statement, appears to suggest to them the mysteries of the universe. This undoubtedly arises—apart from their lack of arithmetical ability—from their feeling, which I have already mentioned, that a very thin shell divides them from the Unseen. Pro-

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Ibn Khaldūn’s view on number-based divination and why the Muslim world of his time often viewed arithmetical proportions as mystical.</meta>

<page-num>107</page-num>
<header>DIVINATION BY NUMBERS IN ISLĀM</header>

portions of numbers lead them almost immediately to suppose proportions existing mysteriously in the very nature of things. On another side, this affected our own Middle Ages as the doctrine of signatures.

It was undoubtedly fostered, further, by the fact that the letters of the Arabic alphabet had numerical values, and that those values did not fit, in any way, the order of the letters in that alphabet. The values were derived from the quite different order of the Hebrew alphabet; but comparatively few Muslims knew that, and in consequence, there is the feeling that the number inheres, in some mysterious way, in the personality of the letter. It is as though we should always think of the number ten when we saw the letter *x*, and also have a vague feeling that whenever *x* occurred in a word, there must, in the scheme of things, be some working of the value ten.

Somewhat similar things are used in games and guesses by us. To a Muslim, that game, for example, of telling a person to take a number and perform on it various operations and from the result of them telling him his age, would seem to involve direct contact with the spiritual world. Ibn Khaldūn, naturally, has no patience with all this. He had Berber blood in him and could count and reckon; but the detail which he feels compelled to give to it and the elaborate simplicity of his explanation and example of proportion in arithmetic show how

<summary>Ibn Khaldūn critiques the mystical significance Muslims attributed to numbers and the Arabic alphabet's numerical values, which he views as simple arithmetic rather than spiritual insight. He suggests this fascination arises from a cultural lack of mathematical skill and a belief in a thin veil between the physical and spiritual worlds.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Ibn Khaldūn’s view on number-based divination and transitions into his analysis of the origins and nature of magic and talismans.</meta>

<page-num>108</page-num>

the minds for which he was writing were absolutely unmathematical.

It might now be appropriate to take up his doctrine regarding the saints and their miracles. However, as has been suggested already, it seems better to leave his more detailed discussion of that subject until we come to deal with the actual path and experience of the religious soul on its journey to God.

Therefore, I will next take up his doctrine of magic and talismans:¹

The sciences of magic and of talismans concern the nature of the faculties by which human souls are able to produce effects in the elemental world, either with or without the help of heavenly beings. If it is without a helper, it is magic; if it is with a helper, it is the science of talismans. Since these sciences are forbidden in various legal codes—both because of their harmfulness and because they involve looking toward someone other than God, whether a star or something else—books concerning them are almost entirely lacking, except for what is found in the books of the ancient peoples from before the time of Moses, such as the Nabataeans and the Chaldeans.

None of the prophets who preceded Moses established laws or brought down commands. Their books contained only exhortations, the doctrine of the unity of God, and warnings about heaven and hell. Thus, these sciences existed among the people of Babylon, the Syrians, and the Chaldeans, and among the people of Egypt, the Copts, and others. These peoples possessed writings and traditions; but only a small amount has been translated for us from their books on this subject, such as the *Nabataean Agriculture*, one of the books of the people of Babylon.

---

<note>¹ Beirut edition, p. 496; Būlāq, p. 414; de Slane's translation, Vol. III, p. 171.</note>

<summary>Ibn Khaldūn transitions to discussing magic and talismans, defining them by whether they involve celestial assistance and noting their historical origins in ancient pre-Mosaic civilizations. He explains that these practices were largely suppressed in religious law due to their potential for harm and their distraction from the worship of God.</summary>
<keywords>magic, talismans, Ibn Khaldūn, Nabataeans, Chaldeans, Moses, Babylon, Egypt, Copts, Nabataean Agriculture, religious law, miracles</keywords>

--- Page 133 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Ibn Khaldūn’s history of magic, specifically focusing on the lineage of practitioners from the East to Spain and the issue of forged historical sources.</meta>

# MAGIC IN ISLĀM

Then people adopted this science from them and became well-versed in it. Afterward, books were composed like the *Scrolls of the Seven Stars* and the *Book of Ṭimṭim, the Indian*, on the forms [or figures] of the Scale and the Stars, and so on. Then Geber appeared in the East, the greatest of the magicians in this community; he examined the books of the experts and extracted the art, testing it thoroughly and distilling its essence. He also composed other works and wrote extensively on this art and on the art of natural magic, because that art is one of the branches of magic. For the changing of specific bodies from one form to another can take place only by a power of the soul, not by mechanical skill, and that is part of the nature of magic, as we shall mention in its place. Then came Maslama ibn Aḥmad of Madrid, the leader of the people of Spain in mathematics and magical matters. He explained all these books and corrected them, and gathered their differing views in his own book. No one has written on this science since him.

I have translated this passage at length because of its historical significance. It was the fate of the entire science of history among Muslims—and Ibn Khaldūn himself does not rise above it—to seek to know too much; to dislike any gaps in knowledge, and not to be too critical toward books which claimed, on very little evidence, to be authorities on the most distant times. All divisions of Muslim literature have suffered from the pseudograph <note>falsely attributed writings</note>; and here Ibn Khaldūn makes mention of one of the most celebrated and disastrous of these books, which, after misleading all Muslim writers, misled even a European scholar in the nineteenth century. The *Nabataean Agriculture* was written at the beginning of the

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Ibn Khaldūn’s history of magic, specifically focusing on the influence of forged historical texts and the role of key figures like Geber and Maslama of Madrid in the development of alchemy and magic.</meta>

tenth century by a certain Ibn al-Waḥshīya, a descendant of an Aramaic family. His book is no translation, but a frank forgery, in which he invents a complete ancient literature and exalts the old Babylonians over the conquering Arabs. It is upon his book and upon their own conception of the history of revelation through a series of prophets, that Muslims base their ideas of the ancient civilizations.

With Geber we touch more honest and more solid ground. He seems to have lived in the latter part of the eighth century, and to have written much upon chemistry. He is the greatest name, of course, in the history of alchemy, but whether all the books attributed to him, and, if so, which ones, are really his, we are completely in the dark. The difficulty is that they, in their European translations at least, show a scientific knowledge which chemists find almost unbelievable for his time. Maslama of Madrid, on the other hand, is a perfectly historical character, who died in 1007 A.D. Ibn Khaldūn often refers to him.<note>See de Slane’s long note about him, Vol. III, p. 172, and on the whole subject, the article on Muhammadan alchemy in Hastings’ *Encyclopaedia of Religions and Ethics*, Vol. I, pp. 289 ff.</note>

But though Ibn Khaldūn’s ancient history was led astray by these forgeries, and his criticism weakened by these assumptions, yet, when he comes to philosophize about the whole question, he shows the same grasp of the possibilities of the mind. We must

### MAGIC IN ISLĀM

remember here, as everywhere, that he takes a great deal for granted which, not many years ago, we would have refused to think about at all, and only within the last few years have accepted as worthy of any consideration. The limits of what science is willing to discuss, which are so likely to hamper us, did not exist for him. The occult phenomena to which we are now turning again, because they have simply been forced upon our notice, had met him in even richer abundance. However, he felt no need to turn away from them. He accepted them and set to work to rationalize them.

    This is how he did it:

    Although human souls are one as a species, they divide into a great many kinds, each with different properties. These properties are inborn constitutions; thus, the souls of the prophets have a property which prepares them for the divine and for communication with angels, and for the influence on the things of this world which necessarily follows. In magicians, on the other hand, there is a particular psychic power by which they influence these things and draw down and apply the spiritual force of the stars, and thus exercise an influence which is either psychic or satanic. The influence, then, of the prophets is by the help of God and by a divine characteristic, while the souls of the diviners have a unique ability to learn about hidden things through satanic forces. Similarly, each kind of human being is distinguished by a distinctive quality which is not found in any other.

    The souls of magicians, furthermore, are of three kinds. The first of these exerts its influence through an effort of the will only, without using an instrument or a helper. This is what the philosophers call magic. The second uses as a helper

the intervention of the spheres, or of the elements, or of the peculiarities of numbers. This they call the talismanic art, and this kind is weaker than the first. The third kind has an influence on the imaginative powers. The user of this influence can control the imagination to a certain extent, and impress upon it different kinds of appearances, resemblances, and forms, according to his purpose; he affects the senses of those who see those things by the force of his soul influencing them. Then it appears to the observer as though he saw those things externally; and yet there is nothing of the kind there. So it is related that some have been made to see gardens, rivers, and castles that had no external reality. This the philosophers call conjuring. These, then, are the three divisions.

Ibn Khaldūn then goes on to discuss the theological and ethical implications of this. He points out that this peculiar power exists in the magician potentially, just as all human powers do. It can be brought to actuality only by practice. Practice, in the case of magic, consists of turning the attention toward the celestial spheres, the stars, the upper worlds, and the evil spirits, and magnifying, worshiping, and submitting oneself to them. But this is turning toward something other than God, which is unbelief. Magic, then, must be largely reckoned as a form of unbelief. On the ethical side, there can be no question of its corrupting influence.

Again, it is necessary to distinguish between these three kinds of magic when we consider whether there is any external reality in magic. In the third kind, it is plain that there is none. But as to the

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Ibn Khaldūn’s views on magic, specifically confirming the reality of the first two types of magic through scriptural evidence and historical reputation.</meta>

Regarding the first two kinds, their reality is certain. On that point, Ibn Khaldūn has no doubt; all reasonable people admit it, and the *Qur'ān* speaks of it with perfect clarity. There we find the case of the two angels at Babel, Hārūt and Mārūt, who taught magic to mankind. Spells were also cast upon the Prophet. A chapter of the *Qur'ān* speaks of "the evil of those who blow upon knots," and ‘Ā’isha relates how those magic knots untied themselves when this chapter was recited over them. The people of Babel—Chaldeans, Nabataeans, and Syrians (according to Ibn Khaldūn's ethnography)—were renowned as magicians, and the *Qur'ān* tells us how the magicians of Egypt competed with Moses. Traces of them still remain in the temples of Upper Egypt. This reference by Ibn Khaldūn is expressed somewhat obscurely, but it seems to point to the paintings and sculptures in Egyptian tombs, and to the mummies and figures found there.

For all Muslims, Egypt has been a land of mystery, ancient stories, hidden treasure, and enchanters. The Egyptians of Ibn Khaldūn's own day were believed to be particularly expert magicians. He provides a very obscure account of one whom he had seen working. I must admit that I cannot translate this passage with any certainty, and de Slane, in his French translation,<note>Vol. III, p. 177.</note> makes the same confession. Then he uttered sounds over that figure, and after gathering saliva in his mouth, he blew it repeatedly at the figure, apparently mixing words of enchantment with it at the same time. The idea was that an evil spirit left him when he blew, attached itself to his saliva, and fell upon his victim.

I confess this is not very clear, yet one can easily recognize in it several permanent elements of magical operations that appear in all countries and eras.

But Ibn Khaldūn had other experiences that are easier to understand. He had met masters of magic who could point to a garment or to a skin and whisper words at it, and it would be cut or torn. They could also point with a slicing gesture at grazing sheep, and their entrails would fall out of them onto the ground. Turning to talismans, Ibn Khaldūn had observed wonders performed through the use of what we call “amicable numbers.” For example, if you take the numbers 284 and 220, each is equal to the sum of the aliquot parts <note>The aliquot parts, or proper divisors, are all the divisors of a number excluding the number itself.</note> of the other. This unique property seems to have captured the Eastern imagination and led to the belief that this relationship could be used to promote love and friendship. These numbers were placed upon two symbolic images, constructed according to astrological rules—one number on each image. Their owners would then become friends. Ibn Khaldūn actually seems to say that experience had proven this true. He tells of other kinds of talismans consisting partly of figures—such as a lion, a snake, or a scorpion imprinted under specific conditions onto certain types of steel—and partly of what we call “magic squares.”

He then returns to the very remarkable “slitting” magic, which is of greater interest to us because he had personal experience with it. It flourished specifically in western North Africa, and the people who practiced it terrorized their neighbors by threatening to use it against their cattle and sheep. Naturally, both parties concealed the matter because of their fear of the authorities, but Ibn Khal-

<page-num>116</page-num>

### RELIGIOUS ATTITUDE AND LIFE IN ISLĀM

Khaldūn had met a company of these "slitters" and had witnessed their operations; they had told him that they possessed a special art and practice through heathen prayers and by drawing on the assistance of the Jinn and the stars. This was set out for them in a book which they owned and studied. They could exercise this art upon anything except a free man—that is, upon goods, cattle, and slaves. As they put it, "We can only work upon that in which money walks."

This <note>says Ibn Khaldūn</note> is what they assert: I questioned one of them, and he told me about it. As for their actions, those are plain and evident. I have been present for much of it and have witnessed it beyond any doubt.

The philosophers distinguished between magic and talismans, but they maintain that both together are due to an influence belonging to the human soul. They provide as proof of this influence how the soul affects the body apart from the ordinary operations of nature or physical causes. Indeed, there are effects which arise from spiritual conditions, such as heat caused by joy, or from ideas, such as those which result from fear. A person walking upon the edge of a wall, or upon a tightrope, will most certainly fall when the fear of falling comes strongly upon them. Only through long practice can the fear of falling be removed, and then such people walk safely. If, then, the soul has this influence upon its own body without physical natural causes, it is possible that it can have a similar influence upon another body, since its relationship to bodies in this kind of influence is uniform; for it is not enfolded in the body or shut up within it. So it follows that it can exert an influence upon all material objects.

This, you will notice, is precisely the theory which

This lies behind the “mental science” and “Christian science” of our own day. It is also practically involved in the infinitely more scientific “metapsychical” investigations—to use Dr. Maxwell's term—which are currently taking place. There is an indubitable element of truth within it. But the philosophers further distinguish between magic and talismans:

> In magic, the magician has no need of a helper; however, the user of a talisman seeks help from the spiritual powers of the stars, the secrets of numbers, the unique properties of things, and the positions of the celestial spheres, which act upon the elemental world, as astrologers claim. Magic, they say, is the union of one spirit with another spirit. But in the use of a talisman, there is the union of a spirit with a material object; conceptually, it is a joining of the higher, heavenly natures with the lower natures. The higher natures are the spiritual powers of the stars, and for that reason, the owner of a talisman mostly seeks aid through astrology. Furthermore, according to the philosophers, the magician cannot acquire the art of magic but must possess it as part of his innate constitution. He has the unique ability to exercise this kind of influence.

Their distinction between magic and miracle, then, is that a miracle is a divine ability that gives the soul this power of influence; the prophet is aided by the Spirit of God. But the magician performs it by himself, through his own psychic power or, under certain conditions, through satanic aid.

The philosophers' distinction, then, reached the very nature of the thing. But Ibn Khaldūn himself is inclined to follow the external signs of the difference:

> A miracle is what is worked by a good man, for good

I do not believe that Ibn Khaldūn would entirely reject the view of the philosophers given above, but only that he regards his own distinction—here he evidently counts himself among the philosophical theologians—as simpler, and as also providing the essentials of the case. The fact that a distinction between miracles and magic was felt to be necessary explains why I am devoting so much time to magic now. Just as with an Oriental person, it is impossible to separate his philosophy from his theology, so it is impossible to separate his religion from what we, in a somewhat narrow spirit, have fallen into the habit of calling “superstition,” or, more liberally, “folklore.”

But it is not only prophets and magicians who can thus bend the order of the world. The Sufis, too, or saints, have their miracles and exercise a similar influence. This is actually a far more important distinction. Muhammad was the last prophet, and so a consideration of the nature of prophetic miracles is largely a subject for academic study. But the miracles of the saints are happening every day, as we shall see, and a knowledge of them is a most practical matter. # THE EVIL EYE IN ISLĀM

saint and the closeness of his communion with God. Thus, by the very nature of the case, it is impossible for him to devote himself to evil; rather, he is held closely by the command and the permission of God. Magic cannot oppose him, either; it collapses and vanishes at his touch. So the enchanted flag of Persia—marked with magic numbers during a <term>planetary hour</term> to ensure victory—fell before the Companions of the Prophet at al-Qādisīya, where the empire of the Chosroes fell forever.

Last among the influences exerted by the soul, Ibn Khaldūn mentions the evil eye—or simply "the Eye," as it is called in the East. Whoever possesses it sees a thing, admires it, envies the owner, and strikes them with their eye. There is no doubt about this; but it differs from all other magic because it does not require the will of the person who has it. It works automatically, and they cannot control it. Therefore, if anyone kills by magic or a <term>talisman</term>, they are to be put to death; but not if they kill with the Eye.

Finally, regarding all of this, Ibn Khaldūn makes a very curious and illuminating statement. It is perfectly evident throughout his book that he is discussing these matters with intense intellectual curiosity and interest. Such subjects interested him, just as they interest many of us today. But that is the paradox of which he himself seems to have been unaware. According to Muslim law, he says, actions are allowable if they are important for

--- Page 144 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Ibn Khaldūn’s theories on the legal and spiritual status of human actions, specifically regarding their utility for salvation or worldly existence.</meta>

<page-num>120</page-num>

# RELIGIOUS ATTITUDE AND LIFE IN ISLĀM

us (*a*) religiously, that is for our final salvation, or (*b*) temporally, for our living in this world. If a thing does not concern us from either of these points of view, and if it is harmful—either actually, as in the case of magic, or supposedly, as in the case of astrology—it is legally forbidden. Furthermore, if it is not of importance to us, and is not harmful, it is still to be avoided; letting such things alone is a way of drawing closer to God, for part of the beauty of a man's *islām* (his resignation to God) is leaving alone what does not concern him.

This, you will see, sheds an astonishing amount of light on Muslim ideals. Contrary to his own clear, but evidently unconscious practice, Ibn Khaldūn teaches that the true Muslim must give up and avoid anything that is not of direct importance for his life in this world or the next. All that we would consider "interesting" is swept away; only the useful is relevant. And this is not Ibn Khaldūn's view alone; the theologians of Islam agree with him. If they include a section on the excellence of science (*fī faḍli-l-ʿilm*), it is certain to be followed by another on the praiseworthy and the blameworthy sciences (*al-ʿulūm al-maḥmūda wal-madhmūma*). Knowledge for its own sake has no place; it must be useful for this world or the next. And this is not simply a theological point; it is woven into the very fabric of the Muslim mind. We can say, "This is an interesting book"; in Arabic, you cannot express that exact idea.

--- Page 145 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous page regarding the Islamic theological perspective that knowledge and actions must serve a specific utility for this world or the afterlife, specifically focusing on the lack of a term for "disinterested interest" in the Arabic language.</meta>

<page-num>121</page-num>
<header>UTILITARIANISM IN ISLĀM</header>

Turn to Badger's *English-Arabic Lexicon* and you will find a large quarto page on "Interest" and its derivations. However, it only helps you to say that a book gives pleasure, or amuses, or is desirable, or useful, or touching, or surprising, or important, or that it sways or captivates you; it never suggests that the book arouses that disinterested intellectual curiosity which we so strangely call "interest." Even curiosity, in its highest and finest sense, cannot be expressed. It is regarded as either deep, devoted study and research, or intrusive spying.

Here, beyond question, we have one of the keys to the fatal defect in the Muslim mind. Of course, there have been exceptions, both conscious and unconscious, but the entire trend of usage and the weight of influence have served to limit and destroy free intellectual activity. The objective must be plain from the start and must belong to certain classified kinds. Investigation that does not know where it is going to end, or what it may produce—and does not care—is forbidden under the Muslim worldview. Even amusement must justify its existence by its usefulness; recreation must seek protection behind proverbs about how "all work and no play makes Jack a dull boy," and tales about the surprising humor and relaxation of saints. The free, self-determining, and self-developing soul may not walk its own path, however innocently, but must fit itself into the schemes and patterns of established schools of thought.

And this does not apply to the Arabic world alone.

<page-num>122</page-num>
<header>RELIGIOUS ATTITUDE AND LIFE IN ISLĀM</header>

Here is what one of the most insightful observers of Islam in recent years has to say on the matter:

> Few things shed a more instructive light on the character of a nation than an examination of the ideas that cannot be expressed in its language. Now the Turkish language, as extensive as it is, contains no equivalent for “interesting.” You can say this is a useful book, a funny book, a scholarly book, or a book that attracts attention, but you cannot precisely translate our expression, “This is an interesting book.” Similarly, you cannot convey in Turkish the precise shade of meaning expressed by the phrase, “I take an interest in the Eastern question, or the Islamic religion.” The various approximate equivalents imply either a more active and less intellectual participation than that denoted by interest, or else suggest that these serious subjects are something strange and funny which it is amusing to hear about. This gap in the language has its counterpart in the mind. The ordinary Turk does not take an interest in anything, and his intelligence seems incapable of grappling with any problem more complex than his immediate daily needs. A natural lack of curiosity, and a conviction that their own religion contains all that man knows or needs to know, keep the provincial population in a state of ignorance that seems incredible and fantastic.<note><sup>1</sup> *Turkey in Europe*, by “Odysseus,” p. 98.</note>

This is certainly expressed too strongly to apply to the much sharper-witted Syrians, Arabs, or Egyptians. But the lack of the idea of free, unrestricted interest, and the rejection of everything that may—in some remote development—weaken the sense of dependence on God characterize them all; which, to return to our starting point, makes it only more sur-

<summary>The author quotes a source claiming that the Turkish language lacks an equivalent for the word "interesting," suggesting a cultural deficiency in disinterested intellectual curiosity. <meta>This page continues the discussion on the absence of "disinterested interest" in Islamic thought, moving from the previous page's linguistic analysis to Ibn Khaldūn’s observations and the utilitarian nature of Islamic mysticism.</meta>

<page-num>123</page-num>
# UTILITARIANISM IN ISLĀM

comprising Ibn Khaldūn’s fresh, open-eyed attention to the phenomena of life.

I have spent so much time on this because it is not alien; indeed, it is very pertinent to our present subject. One of the most astonishing things in Muslim religious feeling is that even its mystical attitudes are utilitarian. Normally, among all peoples, the mystic is so immersed in the experiences of the moment that the future fades out of reality. When he is struggling to find peace, he is not concerned with his salvation from hellfire, but only that the choking burden of the present may be lifted from him. When he has reached peace, the vision and the light of God are on his daily path, and in them he walks. God is in the world and he is with God. I would be reluctant to say that this mystical disinterestedness is not also found in Islām, but far more frequent and always possible is the coarser, harder fear of the Fire—the sense of God as the relentless Watcher in whose presence no soul can stand.

We have seen already how the conversion even of such a saint as al-Ghazzālī was such simple fear: fear as if of an earthly sovereign who might doom one to death if unrecognized; such fear as our religious revivals too often have known. Certainly, there mingled in it the baffled struggles of his intellect, snared in the net of this most unintelligible world, overwhelmed by the burden of its travail and mystery. But once he had reached the sense of a God behind the veil—the

<summary>The author argues that Islamic mysticism often possesses a utilitarian character, driven more by a palpable fear of hellfire and divine judgment than by purely disinterested spiritual experience. This is exemplified by the conversion of al-Ghazzālī, which was motivated by a profound fear of God as a relentless judge.</summary>

<page-num>124</page-num>

The Allāh of the theologies of his day—the fear of that Allāh overwhelmed him. Afterward, he might have had moments of reconciliation and communion, but the dread of a quickly offended, possibly implacable deity was always watching. “Only by the mercy of Allāh,” the Prophet said, “can I hope to enter the Garden”; and so all Muslims have said. The love of God is an afterthought.

So, too, Ibn Khaldūn¹ warns against plunging into speculation on the mysteries of the divine Unity. It is sufficient for eternal salvation simply to confess that Unity in general terms. To go further will lead to nothing but disappointment and, perhaps, unbelief. The Prophet said, “Whoever dies testifying that there is no God but Allāh, will enter the Garden.”

Still more curious is the position of Averroes. He led a double life with two sharply distinguished sets of views. Publicly, he was a broad-school Muslim who, while he admitted that the masses should be taught the simple statements of the *Qur'ān* without explanation or discussion, claimed for himself and all educated men the right to speculate on and explain these statements so as to meet the requirements of philosophy. But this was simply for protection. On the other hand, he was a neo-Platonic-Aristotelian philosopher, separated by

---

¹ <note>Beyrout edition, p. 459; Būlāq, p. 383; de Slane’s translation, Vol. III, p. 42.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Averroes' "double life," explaining how his private neo-Platonic-Aristotelian philosophy diverged sharply from the public orthodox stance he maintained for protection.</meta>

# UTILITARIANISM IN ISLĀM

three great heresies from the Islām of his time. He held the eternity of the material world; he held that God cannot know individuals, cannot exercise providence in any usual sense, and can only produce and embrace the Whole; he held that the human race alone was eternal, but all individuals must pass away. Naturally, then, he was driven to two positions on the matter of salvation. In his books intended for the reading of ordinary, educated men, he followed the regular Muslim doctrine and taught that true knowledge is the knowledge of God, and especially of the religious law, and of happiness and unhappiness in the world to come.¹ In his philosophical writings, on the other hand, intended for students of Aristotle, he denounces all popular myths about the future life. Among dangerous fictions, he says, we must count those which tend to present virtue as simply a means of arriving at happiness. If virtue is nothing more, no one will abstain from pleasure except in the hope of being repaid with usury; a brave man will not seek death, except to avoid a greater evil; a just man will not respect the goods of another, except to acquire double.² Nothing could show the normal Muslim position and its results more clearly.

Before leaving the subject of magic and talismans,

one or two general considerations will be in place. First, you must not think that such things belong to a past age of Islam and have now lost their hold except upon the most ignorant people. That is not so. From one end of the Muslim world to the other, an unquestioning faith in the magician still reigns. It is true that among the educated classes you will encounter a good deal of absolute Voltairean unbelief, but even these individuals are liable to revert at any time. The shell that separates the Oriental from the Unseen is still very thin, and the magician's charm or amulet may easily break it. The world of the *Arabian Nights* is still his world, and for him these stories are not tales from a wonderland; rather, they should be compared to our stories about the wonders and possibilities of science, such as those Jules Verne used to write and which we now owe to H. G. Wells. Similarly, Lane, in his time, found the magic mirror in Cairo, and he and others had some most interesting experiences. You will find these gathered together in N. W. Thomas’s book on *Crystal Gazing*. I would only add that when Thomas says (on page 94) that Lane was eventually inclined to attribute the magician’s success to a certain renegade Scotsman, he goes too far. Scots have been responsible for a great deal, but in Lane’s final opinion, they were not responsible for this. A note by his nephew in a later edition of his *Arabian Nights* (Volume I, page 60) says that there were cases which remained to him

# MAGIC IN ISLĀM

inexplicable. The magic mirror, I may add, is still in popular use in Cairo. Only now, no professional magician is needed. Books have been printed describing the method of using it, and all classes experiment for themselves. Very few doubt the truth of its revelations.

More recent evidence is provided in Professor E. G. Browne’s *Year Among the Persians*. There he met a magician who produced in his presence some most interesting telekinetic phenomena, similar to those which Dr. Maxwell has described in his *Metapsychical Phenomena* (pp. 318 ff.) as performed by a medium whom he calls Meurice. These included moving a comb and a watch lying about three feet from the magician. Unfortunately, Professor Browne did not follow the matter up, apparently being disgusted by some lies which he found the magician was telling about him. He had also heard stories of the magic mirror but had no experiences himself. I shall take up a tale told to him about experiences in learning to control the Jinn later.

But, secondly, while belief in the power of magic is spread generally throughout Islām, and few doubt that the magician, by his spells, can break the thin shell of custom and law and work what only the results can distinguish from a God-given miracle, this does not affect the Muslim religious attitude as much as might be thought. The overwhelming fact of the personality, the will, and the power of God is over

At the opposite extreme from Islam, in this respect, are those lowest religions in which the gods, being harmless, are ignored, and demons—practically magic and witchcraft—are feared and appeased. But in Islam, the puzzle is, rather, how any forms of black magic can survive and how any magician would dare to set himself against Allah. It is certain that the art was and is practiced; but the student probably soothes his conscience and calms his fears by having doubts about the precise nature of the spirits he invokes, much as a person in medieval Europe felt sure that in the end he could cheat the devil, who was notoriously stupid.

Finally, if you want to appreciate the tremendous difference in atmosphere that this distinction involves, compare the *Arabian Nights* with the *Golden Ass* of Apuleius. Both books are filled with a kind of piety; in each case, set in a context that certainly seems strange to us. It has been said that the *Golden Ass* is the first book in European literature to show piety in the modern sense, and it is true that the most disreputable adventures of Lucius lead, in the end, to a religious climax. The *Arabian Nights*, on the other hand, is, despite everything, so pious that the sense of the all-seeing eye and the need to submit to the all-guiding hand become oppressive. But how different the feeling toward the Unseen is in each! Despite fantastic gleams of color and light, few books move under such leaden-weighted

But the world of the *Arabian Nights* is God's world. There is sun and air and the sense of an ultimate justice. Joy comes with the morning there. And so, despite all his belief in magic and his sense of the power of enchanters, the Muslim is a man. He stands on God's earth, beneath His sky, and at any time can enter that presence and carry his grievances to the highest court. Between him and Allah there stands nothing, and he is absolutely sure of Allah.

# LECTURE V

## INTERCOURSE THROUGH THE JINN; SPIRITS, DEMONS, GHOSTS IN ISLĀM.

The next point of contact with the Unseen to which I turn has a much more immediate connection with religion, as we understand that word. Although Ibn Khaldūn has, from time to time, been compelled to mention the Jinn, he has no section dealing explicitly with them; he never fully unburdens his mind on the subject. The simple reason is that he could not; his views on them were too far from those of the Muslim world to be stated in such a book as he was writing. He accepted the great fact of the institution of prophecy; he accepted the personal mission of Muḥammad and the authority of the book revealed through him, because he also felt compelled to accept man’s absolute dependence on God, and to admit that the research, the reasoning, and the systems of the philosophers had been a failure. Viewing life from the perspective of reason, he was an agnostic; by that path, the ultimate realities could not be reached. But reason is not the only pathway to reality, and it is only one side of man’s nature. On another side—that of the life of the soul—man came forth from God and can still have contact with God. This has already been made plain, again and again. Nor is it in the slightest peculiar to Ibn

<summary>This page introduces a lecture on the Jinn and spirits in Islam, focusing on Ibn Khaldūn's cautious treatment of the subject and his belief that the soul, rather than reason, provides a connection to ultimate reality.</summary>
<keywords>Ibn Khaldūn, Jinn, Islam, Muhammad, prophecy, agnosticism, reason, soul, the Unseen</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Ibn Khaldūn's philosophical and religious framework, specifically his debt to al-Ghazzālī’s skepticism regarding pure reason and his reliance on the soul's direct experience of the divine.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>131</page-num>
# INTERCOURSE THROUGH THE JINN

Khaldūn. He derived it from al-Ghazzālī; he was a convinced Ghazzālian.

And so, too, was the rest of Islam. This position, which some might compare with the pragmatic or humanistic stance that many of us have gravitated toward in recent years, is the standard attitude of Islam toward the problem of religion and metaphysics. All metaphysical systems have failed and must fail. The thinkers of Islam had been through them all and had come away empty-handed. Reason, no matter how subtle, could find no means of passing from "me" to "you"—from the effect to the cause. But the human soul could leave the body in many ways; it could meet the outstretched help of God and find peace and rest there. It is true that when the soul returned, it had to translate its message into terms of human experience; the veil of the senses, in which the body clothed it, required that. But the message was delivered, regardless of how its appearance might vary; a person could know this much with absolute certainty.

Starting from this position, then, Ibn Khaldūn looked out at the world with all its varied, changing phenomena and tried to interpret and understand it through these ideas. It seemed to him that the pieces of the puzzle fell together on their own. Throughout the world, he found this reaching and yearning of the soul for its source. Just as the Christian church speaks of the fullness of time, he felt

This spiritual framework allowed Ibn Khaldūn to interpret the world as a place where the soul constantly seeks its divine origin.</summary>

that all these yearnings led up to the final revelation in Muḥammad. That revelation in the Qur'ān, then, he had to interpret for himself in terms of the phenomena around him.

And he succeeded for the most part. He found corresponding phenomena in life for everything in the Qur'ān except for individual personal spirits—the angels and the Jinn. He had no experience with such things and, therefore, could attach no concepts to these words. He knew the spiritual world in a broad sense, but not the personalities within it. In all this which we have now reached, you will remember that Ibn Khaldūn stands alone. No other Muslim ever looked at the facts of life with such clear, untroubled vision, accounted for them all, and tried to rationalize them all as he did. Thus, he had never known angels and, clearly, had had no personal experience of the Jinn. He had known, tested, and accepted soothsayers and magicians; he had experienced dreams and found them valid; he was firmly convinced of the miracles of the saints; but he had never seen any of the Jinn, and so he excluded them from his calculations.

He speaks of them in only one passage in his book—and even that, as we have already seen, occurs only in a few manuscripts and was apparently added as an afterthought. There,¹ he places the verses of

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Ibn Khaldūn’s skepticism regarding the Jinn, explaining how he categorized mentions of them in the Qur'ān as "obscure" to reconcile his lack of personal experience with the text.</meta>

the Qur'ān that mention them in the "obscure" (*mutashābih*) class. All Qur'anic verses are divided into "clear" (*muḥkam*) and "obscure"; this is a division that delivers Muslims from the difficulties of the doctrine of inspiration, much as the human and divine elements do in our own Scriptures. Naturally, theologians agree very little as to what the true "obscure" verses are, and they include in 그that category those which their systems find difficult to digest.

But Ibn Khaldūn, by disregarding the Jinn entirely out of his respect for facts, was really ignoring one of the most primitive sources of ancient Arabian religion. The Jinn were the nymphs and satyrs of the desert; all wild, solitary nature was full of them. In a sense, they typified that side of the life of nature which was still unsubdued and hostile to man. They were in constant connection with wild animals and often appeared in animal forms. Whether they were originally animal fetishes and what their relation was to totemism, we need not consider here. Our subject does not reach so far back. But the difference between them and the primitive Semitic gods, as Robertson Smith so well puts it,¹ is simply that the gods have worshipers, and the Jinn do not. This means that the gods have entered into fixed, personal relations with men, are no longer hostile, and dwell in sanctuaries that are no longer dangerous, though they may still be awe-inspiring.

Robertson Smith thus goes on, in what is a *locus classicus* for our subject:

> In fact, the earth may be said to be divided between demons and wild beasts on one hand, and gods and men on the other. To the former belong the untrodden wilderness with all its unknown perils, the wastes and jungles that lie outside the familiar tracks and pasture grounds of the tribe, and which only the boldest men venture upon without terror; to the latter belong the regions that man knows and habitually frequents, and within which he has established relations, not only with his human neighbors, but with the supernatural beings that have their haunts side by side with him. And as man gradually encroaches on the wilderness and drives back the wild beasts before him, so the gods in like manner drive out the demons, and spots that were once feared as the habitation of mysterious and presumably malignant powers lose their terrors and either become common ground or are transformed into the seats of friendly deities. From this point of view, the recognition of certain spots as haunts of the gods is the religious expression of the gradual subjugation of nature by man.

But when we reach Muḥammad’s time, the situation has greatly cleared and simplified. No essential connection remained between the Jinn and wild beasts. They had become spirits with some curious animal associations. For example, they appeared riding upon animals, just as, in another connection, they were accompanied by manifestations of light. The pagan Meccans associated them with Allāh as his sons and daughters, or they were made partners with Allāh.<note>¹*Qur'an* vi, 100.</note> They also, as we have seen, inspired

By the time of Muhammad, the Jinn were viewed as spirits associated with light and animals, and were sometimes erroneously linked to Allah in Meccan paganism.</summary>

the *kāhins* <note>soothsayers or priests</note> and poets, and Muḥammad was said to be possessed by one. Muḥammad accepted Allāh and established Him as the one and only God. To him, the Jinn remained real, rational beings, but they were the creation of Allāh and under His rule. How he conceived their relations to the angels (the messengers of Allāh) on one hand, and to the devils (especially to Iblīs, the devil <note>Ancient Greek: ὁ διάβολος</note>—an influence from Judaism and Christianity) on the other, is unclear because of his own uncertainty and indecision. It is certain that, for him, the two races on earth capable of salvation were the Jinn and mankind; before Allāh, these two were on exactly the same footing. Therefore, he had to proclaim Islam to the Jinn just as he did to mankind. And that was done. In chapter 72 of the *Qur'ān*, we read the words of Allāh to Muḥammad, revealing that this had happened and telling him to inform the people about it:

> Say <note>O Muḥammad</note>, “It has been revealed to me that a small group of the Jinn listened, then said, ‘We have heard a wondrous *Qur’ān* <note>or recitation</note>, guiding to what is right; so we believe in it, and we certainly will not join anyone as a companion to our Lord.’ ”

The Jinn used to ascend to heaven and listen there in order to learn what was decreed by God. “Now,” they said, “whoever listens finds a shooting star waiting for him there.” The angels hurled these at them to drive them away.

In chapter 46, verse 28 and following, mention is again made of how a small company of the Jinn gathered to hear the Prophet and then dispersed to carry the message to their brethren. There are many other references in the Qur'ān to the Jinn, all accepting their existence quite simply as a race on earth alongside the Sons of Adam; the phrase, “the Jinn and mankind,” occurs again and again. With them, as I have already said, Iblīs <note>Ancient Greek: ὁ διάβολος, the devil</note> is curiously confused; sometimes being reckoned a fallen angel, and sometimes one of them. Several times we are told that he refused to prostrate himself to Adam when the other angels did so. In one of these passages (chapter 38) he is explicitly said to be one of the Jinn, and mankind is asked, “Do you then take him and his offspring as patrons <note>*awliyā*</note> instead of me?” This is an allusion to the semi-worship of the Jinn by the pagan Arabs.

So much for the Qur'ān, then. But these references, though clear, do not take us very far. Muḥammad

is either artistically or genuinely modest in his claims. The great controversy among Muslim theologians, as to whether Muḥammad ever actually saw the Jinn, must be decided in the negative. The *Qur’ān* is explicit that all this was a revelation to him from Allāh. But tradition has not been content with that, and the fixed belief of the vast majority of the Islamic community is that he had various direct interviews, face to face, with these spirits. Some are most vividly told, with details suggesting Western magic.

I choose one, not because of its superior historical accuracy—for all are equally unhistorical—but because of its detail, which appealed to the later Muslim imagination and makes it more representative for us. It is attributed to az-Zubayr ibn al-‘Awwām, one of the earliest of the believers, and also one of the ten who were personally promised by the Prophet that they would enter the Garden:

toe a line for me on the ground and said, "Sit in the middle of that." Then when I had sat down, all the fear I had felt left me. And the Prophet passed between me and them and recited the Qur’ān in a loud voice until dawn broke. Then he came past me and said, "Take hold of me." So I walked with him, and we went a short distance. Then he said to me, "Turn and look; do you see anyone where they were?" I turned and said, "O Apostle of God, I see a great blackness!" He bent his head to the ground, looked at a bone and a piece of dung, and threw them to them. Afterward he said, "These are a delegation of the Jinn of Nasībīn; they asked me for travel provisions; so I assigned to them all bones and pieces of dung."

<margin>¹ See al-Bājūrī on Ibn Qāsim, Vol. I, p. 63, *on ritual purification (istinjā)*; edition of Cairo, 1307 AH.</margin>

This ending is rather puzzling, but it seems to occur in all the stories of this kind. I take it that it is an attempt to explain a part of the ritual law dealing with purification.¹ In one form it runs:

> The Prophet said to them [the Jinn], "Every bone over which the name of God has been spoken belongs to you; you shall take it and it shall be in your hands as full of meat as possible; and dung shall be food for your animals." Then he said [to his followers], "So do not use these two things for purification; they are the food of your brothers."

Of these legends there are curious later echoes. Stories were passed down of Muslims who saw Jinn or heard their voices, and learned from them that they had taken part in these famous delegations to Muḥammad. There is also a long tale of one aged Jinnī who met Muḥammad and converted to Islām.

<summary>This page concludes the story of Muḥammad’s encounter with the Jinn of Nasībīn, detailing how he provided them with bones and dung as food. This legend serves to explain Islamic ritual laws that forbid using those same items for purification, as they are considered the provisions of the Jinn.</summary>

That the Muslim law in its entirety is binding on the believing Jinn is accepted as certain. Whether they have had prophets of their own kind is uncertain, but it is certain that Muhammad was a prophet to them. That they will enter the Garden and be rewarded there is almost unanimously accepted. Iblis himself, of course, is an unbeliever, but different reasons are given for why he is considered so. He is also the supreme tempter of men and is conceived of as pitting his wits against Allah to seduce His creation away from Him. He brought about the Fall, but in Islam, it is only a historical event without theological consequences. Still, traces remain of a doctrine of original sin. The following story is strikingly to that

From the time when Satan was cursed and driven from Paradise because of Adam (peace be upon him!), he pursued him with hatred and sought to take vengeance. He had a son named Khannās; he made him take the form of a kid, and took him before our mother Eve, and said, "Let this kid remain with you; I shall come soon and fetch it." Saint Adam came and saw the kid, and he said, "Whose kid is this?" Eve answered, "Satan has left it, and has gone off." He said, "I will come now and fetch it." Saint Adam (peace be upon him!) was angry, and he killed the kid, and threw it into the desert and went away. Satan came and said, "Where is the kid?" But in one respect, the Muslim Iblīs differed markedly from the Devil of medieval Europe. He was hopelessly lost—that was accepted—but then he was also the father of all the Jinn, both believing and unbelieving. There was, therefore, despite all his strategies to mislead men, a kindly side to his nature. He was not simply stupid, as in European devil-lore; he was also humorous. Often in the *Arabian Nights*, he plays this double role, showing himself as most interested, friendly, and amusing, while the other characters in the story scrupulously refer to him as "Iblīs the Accursed." Outstanding examples are in the Story of *Sul and Shumūl*, recently published and translated by Seybold,² and in the "Story of Hārūn ar-Rashīd and Tūḥfat al-Qulūb."³

From many of these, as for example, “The Story of Abdullah and his Brothers,”¹ it is clear that the popular imagination had brought Hārūn ar-Rashīd into close relationships with the Jinn. Through his strict piety and exact observance of his religious duties—this sounds very curious, but *Hārūn was pious in his own way*—combined with his position as successor of Muḥammad, commander of the faithful, and representative of Allāh on earth, he had complete control, both supernatural and natural, over both Jinn and mankind. The Jinn added to his wealth, taught songs and melodies to his court poets and musicians, and took the oath of loyalty to his proclaimed successor. Regarding the last point, we have better authority than the *Arabian Nights*. Ibn Khallikān tells us of it in connection with a certain poet, who was the intermediary.² Hārūn is reported to have said to this poet, “If you have seen what you describe, you have seen marvels; if not, you have composed a wonder.” This must not be interpreted as implying doubt about the existence of the Jinn; that would be heresy of the worst kind. The doubt was only regarding whether he had interacted with them; for it was a highly contested point whether any men except prophets could see them. Some few lawyers stated flatly that any man who claimed to have seen them was not fit to be a legal witness; he had shown himself

A vast amount of literature has gathered around the possibility of marriage between humans and the Jinn. The general position is that such marriages have frequently occurred and are lawful; however, a few canon lawyers deny their legality based on grounds found in the Quran.<note>² *Quran* 16:74; 40:9.</note> According to the current code of Ottoman law, following the school of Abū Ḥanīfa, such marriages are illegal;<note>³ Young, *Body of Ottoman Law*, Vol. 2, pp. 210, 215.</note> one reason cited is that a Jinnī may appear as either sex. But the widespread belief of the Muslim people scorns these legal doubts. Probably every Muslim has heard of or had some connection to some man or another who was known to have married a female Jinnī. For instance, Lane, during his residence in Cairo, had a Persian acquaintance who told him of a friend of his own who had had such an experience.<note>⁴ *Arabian Nights*, Vol. 1, chapter 1, note 25.</note> This idea has also often served to cover up an intrigue. A good example of this, occurring in Alexandria in the middle of the last century, can be found in Bayle St. John’s *Two Years in a Levantine Family* (chapter 24). The book called *Al-Fihrist*, a *descriptive catalog* of Arabic literature from around 1000 A.D., gives a separate section to “Names of Those Among Mankind Who Loved the Jinn and Vice-versa.” It actually lists sixteen titles of books containing their love stories. Similarly, in the numerous collections of love stories, there are chapters dedicated to “Lovers of the Jinn.”

Another fertile aspect of this subject is the relationship between saints and the Jinn. As Muslim saints live more or less in contact with the unseen world all the time, that relationship is necessarily close. Of course, we must distinguish between stories that are clearly apocryphal and those which have at least some *verisimilitude*; although both are valuable for our purpose. A story with every appearance of truth is the one that al-Ghazzālī tells of his own attempt at spirit-seeing. I may say of him that one of his characteristics is extreme modesty in his claims of contact with the Unseen. He had visions that provided insight into spiritual truth, but he never felt that he had reached the same degree of closeness to the divine as some of his contemporaries, and he always declared that he had never been able to work miracles. This story, then, bears these characteristic marks of modesty. He applied to a celebrated evoker of the Jinn, Muḥammad ibn Aḥmad aṭ-Ṭabasī—an older contemporary of his who died in <term>A. I do not mean to suggest that all the marvels of 'Abd al-Qādir's life are necessarily mythical. One of the people of Baghdad came to him and told him that his young daughter had been snatched away from the roof of the house. Another saint who had significant dealings with the <term>Jinn</term> was <term>ash-Sha‘rānī</term>, a Cairene mystic who died in 1565. He was a very remarkable man, a combination of the most opposite characteristics. He was a canon lawyer of originality and insight—one of the very few creative minds in law after the first three centuries of <term>Islām</term>. He was a moralist, filled with strong ethical indignation. Unlike most of the scholars of <term>Islām</term>, he sought and found his place among the oppressed common people. He was a mystic who lived from day to day in constant touch with the Unseen; the spirit world was as near and real to him as the walls of the classroom in which he taught or of the mosque in which he worshiped. In the night, dreams would come to him, or else, when he woke, a voice would sound in his ears; a <term>hātif</term>, as they called such wandering voices, would warn or advise him. The records of <term>Islām</term> are full of these, but in no case so much as in his. Naturally, interactions with the <term>Jinn</term> were also present. They

It should be understood, then, that just as among men there are ascetics and devotees, so, too, are there among the <term>Jinn</term>. In deserts and solitary places, men have often heard their voices in pious exclamation or prayer; the records of the saints are full of such accounts. And just as they taught men, so men taught them. The great <term>shaykhs</term> had disciples among the <term>Jinn</term> just as they did among mankind. He had as followers more than seventy thousand of the <term>jinn</term>; and when he died, they were scattered to all quarters of the earth, but none of them ever found a teacher like him again. “I had made friends with four of these <term>jinn</term>,” he continued, “and once I asked one of the four, who was the most learned of all, which of the plants, in their opinion, provided the most useful drugs for medical purposes, so as to cure all illnesses. ‘There is not one among all plants,’ replied the <term>jinnī</term>, ‘more generally useful than the caper; for it combines within itself qualities that are found only separately in other plants; and if mankind but knew all that is in it, they would not wish for any other.’”¹

But for everyone, this matter was not so simple. The <term>Jinn</term> might be spoken of in the *<term>Qur'ān</term>*, and many might have seen them and spoken with them, but others had no such good fortune. <term>Al-Ghazzālī</term>, as we have seen, had only limited success in his attempt to reach them, and <term>Ibn Khaldūn</term> seems to have had none at all. These, however, were believing men, and either accepted the traditions and the testimony of others or maintained their position. But there were some who were not great believers and who had to determine the existence of the <term>Jinn</term> on grounds other than religious. Many of the <term>Muᶜtazilites</term> seem in general to have rejected them; how they dealt with the <term>Qur'ānic</term> passages, I do not know. They must have explained them away in some fashion, as

---

they were only heretics and not unbelievers. It is certain that they held varying opinions on the matter.

But the philosophers were in a different situation. <term>Al-Fārābī</term>, who died in 950, was a <term>Plotinian</term> and an <term>Aristotelian</term>, but he managed, by also being a mystic, to remain a devout Muslim. His only trouble, then, was to find a philosophical definition and so incorporate them into his system. The ordinary definition was, "Airy bodies capable of assuming different forms, possessed of reason and understanding, and able to perform difficult labors."<note>Damīrī, Vol. I, p. 177.</note> But there was a doubt regarding the point of reason (<term>ʿaql</term>). For example, in the "Story of the Fisherman and the <term>Jinnī</term>," in the *Arabian Nights*, the fisherman says to himself in some texts,<note>Galland; Breslau; I Calcutta.</note> "This is a <term>Jinnī</term>, and I am a human being, and <term>Allāh</term> has given me reason and made me more excellent than him; and lo! I plot against him with my reason, and he plots against me with his <term>Jinn</term>-mind (*bi-jinnihi?*)."

<term>Al-Fārābī</term> seized upon this distinction, and he constructed the following definitions: Man is a living being—rational and mortal; the angels are the same—rational and immortal; brute beasts are the same—irrational and mortal; the <term>Jinn</term>, then, to complete the analogy, are living beings—irrational and immortal. > first day, sure enough, a lion appeared and entered the circle. I was horribly frightened, but I stood my ground nonetheless, even though I nearly fainted with terror. When some time had passed after this and I had studied philosophy further, I concluded that I had been the victim of hallucinations, triggered by expectation, solitude, hunger, and long periods of staying awake. To test this hypothesis, I repeated the same process I had practiced before, this time in a spirit of philosophical skepticism. My expectations were justified; I saw absolutely nothing. There is another fact which proves to my mind that the phantoms I saw on the first occasion did not exist outside of my own brain. I had never seen a real lion then, and my ideas about the appearance of that animal were entirely derived from the pictures that may be seen over the doors of bathhouses in this country. Now, the lion which I saw in the magic circle looked exactly like those paintings in form and coloring; therefore, as I need hardly say, it differed considerably in appearance from a real lion.

This concludes the account of this <term>philosopher of Isfahán</term>, as reported by <term>Professor Browne</term>. But you already know, from <term>Ibn Khaldūn</term> and <term>al-Ghazzālī</term>, that the spirits which appear to an aspiring magician must take forms that he already knows. They are ideas—true ideas—which his memory and imagination dress in corresponding appearances. Thus, the idea of a "lion" would necessarily take the form of a lion from a bathhouse painting.

St. John could find no explanation for these two instances. Nor do I think you will be able to, if you read his careful narrative. I can make only one suggestion. ## LECTURE VI

### SAINTS AND THE ASCETIC-ECSTATIC LIFE IN ISLĀM

It may be helpful for us now to look back to our starting point, to see how far we have come and what journey still lies before us. You will remember that in our subject—the religious attitude and life as developed in Islām—I found three essential elements: (1) the reality of the Unseen, as a background to life that is unattainable to our physical senses; (2) man’s relation to this Unseen, concerning faith and insight into it: the whole emotional religious life; (3) the discipline of the traveler on his way to such direct knowledge of the divine and during his life within it.

The first of these—the tremendous reality and nearness of the unseen world for the Muslim—must now be reasonably clear to you. You will also have observed the different processes by which the Muslim endeavors to construct connections between his own life and that Unseen, his metaphysical scheme in the exact sense. Necessarily, that will appear to you very unmetaphysical in the ordinary sense, full of bizarre concrete details and materialisms. But that arises from the nature of the case. I should like to put before you an ontology full of the most beautiful abstractions, leaving you gasping in

But among these pathways to the Unseen, I have not yet formally put before you the path trodden by the saints—by those men who, in the beauty of holiness, have gained direct vision of divine things and whose lives, in consequence, are permeated with divine light and energy. Reference has been made to them again and again, and the Muslim conception of them as a class is already, in general terms, before you. Strictly speaking, I should now consider the phenomena of sainthood as one of the avenues of approach to the Unseen, one of the cracks in the shell, before passing on to our second element, the emotional religious life in general. I propose, however, to combine the two, and will now take up the psychological-religious element of man’s relation to the Unseen regarding faith and insight into it.

Practically, we come now to an investigation of sainthood as practiced in Islām. This does not limit our subject as the word "saint" might suggest. There are saints of all degrees in Islām, and sainthood is not the rare phenomenon of our usual associations. tude of brothers with varying spiritual illumination and powers, every pious Muslim looks up toward a path to God and knows his place in it. This institution, externally and internally, as an organization and as a way of life, is of the highest interest as a force in Islam; but I can only mention it most inadequately now. Our subject, strictly speaking, is the emotional life of the individual.

At least this much must be said. From the earliest times, there was an element in the Muslim church that was equally repelled by traditional teaching and by intellectual reasoning. It felt that the essence of religion lay elsewhere; that the seat and organ of religion was in the heart. Over time, all of Islam became permeated with this conception in different degrees and various forms. More extensively than Christianity ever did, Islam became and remains a mystical faith. Everyone—the simple believers, the theologians of the schools, and the philosophers—arrived in one form or another at the essential mystical positions.

The variation, of course, was wide; it stretched from absolute Plotinian <note>Relating to the Neoplatonic philosophy of Plotinus, which emphasizes emanation from "the One."</note> pantheism—the emergence of individuals from the One and their vanishing back into the One—through various phases of more or less disguised pantheism, to a simple acceptance of life in God and of God’s immanence in the world. In the strangest fashion, the absolute contrast found in the Qur’ān between Allah and His world—Allah and not-Allah—almost entirely vanished.

## RELIGIOUS ATTITUDE AND LIFE IN ISLĀM

Then, from this soil, developed the concept of saints in the precise sense: favorites of God, gifted by Him with enlightenment and wondrous powers, accepted and heard by Him, and therefore to be honored, petitioned, and practically worshiped by other men. These began as ascetics, fleeing from the coming wrath; they developed into ecstatics, reveling in the concept of the love for and of God, which they pictured in various sensory ways; and they often ended as antinomians <note>Those who believe that under the gospel dispensation of grace, the moral law is of no use or obligation because faith alone is necessary to salvation.</note> in a spiritual life above the moral law and ritual duty. Among certain classes, the worship of these individuals, both living and dead, by the common people has gone so far as to bring Islām back to a polytheistic or, at least, a poly-demonistic position. I cannot enter into the extremely interesting details of this here. You will find them at length in Dr. Goldziher's *Muhammedanische Studien* <note>Vol. II, pp. 277-378.</note>.

Furthermore, there was a tendency for schools of disciples to gather around such outstanding figures, exactly as the schools of the prophets did in the Old Testament. They were students surrounding their teacher, unconstrained by any vows except those of their love and devotion. He instructed them in theology, initiated them into the specific religious life, and guided them on that pathway. They did not necessarily remain with him always. They might go back into the world and live their lives there. And he, on his part, was not necessarily a cloistered monk

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the organization of early Muslim mystics, explaining how circles of disciples were centered around individual teachers and providing the etymology for terms such as Sufi and dhikr.</meta>

...or a solitary; he might hold what we would call a university position as a professor of theology or canon law.

This continued for centuries. Such teaching saints came and went, and with their deaths, their circles of disciples disbanded. The unit of organization was still the individual teacher, and only for the duration of his life. It is true that early on, there were communities of begging friars who wandered in the summer and settled in their monasteries in the winter, and their organization was reasonably continuous; however, these had not yet begun to call themselves by any specific name. The general words describing them are suggestive. The saints are *walīs* (*walī*, plural *awliyā*, "a relative," "friend," "associate," "favorite," and in this context, "of God"); the ascetics were *sāʾiḥs*, "wanderers"; *rāhibs*, "fearers"; and *zāhids*, "ascetics." Broadly, they were all called *Ṣūfīs*, because of their habit of wearing wool, *ṣūf*. Their exercises are called *dhikrs*, "remembrances," from the Qurʾānic injunction, "Remember Allāh often, and praise Him morning and evening."¹

The "remembering" consists of the repetition of the names of God and certain formulas.²
