<meta>This page continues the history of Islamic mysticism, describing the transition from individual disciples gathered around a teacher to the formal organization of self-perpetuating dervish fraternities, such as the Qādirites and Sanūsites.</meta>

# RELIGIOUS ATTITUDE AND LIFE IN ISLĀM

In the course of the twelfth century, these associations of personal followers and pupils began to transform into self-perpetuating corporations. What are now known as fraternities of *darwīshes* were well established. *Darwīsh* is derived from a Persian word meaning a beggar "seeking at doors," but it has come to be applied to all members of these fraternities or orders, whether they are begging friars or not. The strict begging friar is called a *faqīr* in Arabic, meaning "a poor man."

Among the earliest of these orders appears to be that of the Qādirites, founded either by ‘Abd al-Qādir al-Jīlānī—about whom I have already shared some legends—or by his immediate followers. However, their numbers soon grew, and today many of these brotherhoods are scattered across the Muslim world. Some of them are very widespread. The Qādirites, for example, have spread from the region of Baghdād as far as North Africa and are dominant even in the Fulani Emirates of northern Nigeria.<note>¹ Major Alder Burdon, in the *Geographical Journal*, December 1904, p. 650.</note> One of the youngest orders, and by far the most fanatical, the Sanūsites, is spread from the Atlantic to the Philippines.

These orders are independent of one another in governance; each has its own ritual and way of life; they differ in the wonders which they perform in ecstasy; and their theological attitudes,

<vocab>darwīsh, faqīr, Qādirites, ‘Abd al-Qādir al-Jīlānī, Muslim world, Baghdād, Fulani Emirates, Sanūsites</vocab>

<summary>During the twelfth century, Muslim mystic circles evolved into permanent religious fraternities known as dervish orders, such as the widespread Qādirites and the fanatical Sanūsites. These independent organizations established their own rituals and governance as they spread throughout the Islamic world from Africa to Southeast Asia.</summary>
<keywords>darwīsh, faqīr, Qādirites, ‘Abd al-Qādir al-Jīlānī, Sanūsites, Sufi orders, Islamic mysticism, religious fraternities</keywords>

--- Page 187 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of dervish orders, specifically focusing on their organizational diversity and their structural similarities to Christian monastic and Protestant groups. It also introduces the concept of the invisible Sufi hierarchy led by the Quṭb.</meta>

->THE ASCETIC-ECSTATIC LIFE IN ISLĀM 163<-

<margin>Faint pencil checkmarks appear on the right margin next to the first paragraph.</margin>

even, are different, varying in the strictness of their adherence to the ritual law and the precision of their acceptance of the traditional faith. In fact, they constitute the only ecclesiastical organization that Islam has ever had; however, it is a diverse organization, completely lacking unity, either internally or externally. Furthermore, each of the orders has a large number of affiliated lay members, who are in exactly the same position as the tertiaries of Christian monasticism. Thus, the influence of these orders is active in all ranks of life throughout the Islamic world. They are the closest equivalent to the various churches of Protestantism, just as there are striking parallels between their meetings and the early class-meetings of the Methodists.

But besides these great separate and visible organizations, there is one that is even greater, single, and invisible. This is the Sufi hierarchy. At its head is a great saint, presumably the greatest of the time, chosen by God for the office and granted miraculous power above all others. He is called the *Quṭb*, or *Axis*, and he wanders—often invisible and always unknown to the world—through many lands, performing the duties of his office. From him, the hierarchy descends in gradually widening numbers, and the whole system forms a saintly board of administration by which the invisible government of the world is carried out.<note>¹ Lane, *Modern Egyptians*, chapter 10; Lane, *Arabian Nights*, note 63 to chapter 3; von Kremer, *Dominant Ideas* (*Herrschende Ideen*), p. 172; Vollers, in *ZDMG.*, Vol. 43, p. 115.</note>

<summary>Dervish orders serve as the primary ecclesiastical structure in Islam, incorporating lay members similar to Christian tertiaries. Additionally, Sufism posits an invisible spiritual hierarchy governed by a supreme saint known as the Quṭb, or Axis.</summary>
<keywords>Sufi hierarchy, Quṭb, Axis, dervish orders, ecclesiastical organization, lay members, tertiaries, invisible government</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the comparison between Islamic dervish orders and Christian organizations, specifically contrasting the Islamic spiritual hierarchy with the structure of the Roman Catholic Church.</meta>

# RELIGIOUS ATTITUDE AND LIFE IN ISLĀM

It may, perhaps, make the points above clearer to contrast these orders and this hierarchy with the monastic orders and the organization of the Roman Catholic Church. In every respect, the advantage in universality and freedom lies with Islam. Regarding the hierarchy—I assume its existence and reality since our subject is the beliefs and attitudes of Muslims—its head is spiritual, invisible, and in direct connection with Allah. The organization as a whole is free from the bonds of locality, worldly matters, and the entanglements these bring. One might say the entire system is a beautiful dream. The orders, furthermore, are independent and self-developing. There is rivalry among them, but none rules over another. In faith and practice, each follows its own path, limited only by the universal conscience of Islam. Thus, unusual doctrines and serious moral defects easily develop unnoticed, but freedom is preserved. As for universality, the entire Muslim population is reached and can be included. The system of tertiaries—adherents who live in the secular world but are bound to certain religious practices—which the Franciscans first introduced to Christendom and the Dominicans adopted, has its broadest and earliest extension in Islam. In this respect, the Muslim fraternities might be said to resemble the lodges of Freemasons or other fraternal societies. Furthermore, everything said here regarding men also applies to women, even to the present day, including lay mem-

The distinction in Roman Christendom that a woman cannot be a priest does not exist in Islām, as there is neither priest nor layman there. Therefore, all the Arabic terms I provided above for ascetics, mendicants, and so on, can be used in the feminine form, and the lifestyle and spiritual exercises of men can be applied to women as well. Finally, a standard reference book for all these fraternities is Depont and Cappolani’s *Confréries religieuses Musulmanes*, Algiers, 1897.

But how did this movement and development appear to the Muslims themselves? That is the point of greater interest to us. Once again, I use Ibn Khaldūn to represent, quite fairly, the broad consensus of Islām toward the <term>Ṣūfīs</term>. As far as I can find, he has not discussed the fraternities; in his time, they apparently were not of outstanding importance. The following, then, is a summary of his section¹ on the <term>Ṣūfīs</term>. Their science is one of the religious sciences that emerged within the Muslim community. From the very beginning, the method of the <term>Ṣūfīs</term> was followed by the most eminent of the Companions and their immediate successors as a way to attain truth and guidance. Thus, I might add, dictionaries of <term>Ṣūfī</term> biography begin with the ten individuals to whom Muḥammad personally

promised Paradise, among them being the first four <term>khalifas</term>, the "rightly guided." The original form of the method consisted of (1) persistence in acts of worship; (2) living only for God; (3) turning away from the adornments of the world; (4) abstinence from the pursuits of the masses, such as pleasure, wealth, and ambition; and (5) separation from mankind in solitude for worship. All this, says <term>Ibn Khaldūn</term>—following the pious historical fiction that had developed—was common among the Companions and the early believers.

But in the second century and afterward, people turned to the world and lowered themselves to be involved with it. Then, those who turned instead to worship came to stand apart and were called, by distinction, <term>Ṣūfīs</term>. The derivation of this name he gives, as I have already done, is from <term>ṣūf</term> (wool); they wore woolen garments in contrast to the splendid clothing of the non-Ṣūfīs.<note>Cf. Nöldeke, in *ZDMG.*, Vol. XLVIII, p. 45.</note> But their turning to the devotional life and separating themselves from the mass of the people had spiritual consequences. Man has perceptions, either external or internal; the latter concern his own states of joy, sorrow, hope, fear, and so on. The intelligent spirit that governs the body grows through <note>reflective</note> perceptions, acts of will, and states; thus, consciousness is traditionally divided into knowing, feeling, and willing. By these, man is distinguished from other animals. They pro-

<meta>This page continues the explanation of the spiritual psychology of Sufism, beginning mid-word to describe how knowledge and emotion arise from perceptions, and how these lead the practitioner through progressive spiritual stages.</meta>

->THE ASCETIC-ECSTATIC LIFE IN ISLĀM<-

ceed, one from the other: knowledge from proofs, and joy or sorrow from painful or pleasant perceptions. So, when the Sufi beginner applies himself to his religious exercises, states<gloss>¹</gloss> (feelings) result within him, of one kind or another and with varying degrees of permanence. He then ascends as if on a ladder, rising above his former selves, until he arrives at that direct knowledge of the divine unity which is his goal. He must climb through these stages, which begin with obedience and purity, and which faith introduces and accompanies. He can be certain that if there are any interruptions in his progress, it is because he has failed in some way prior to that. He must, therefore, cautiously hold himself accountable for what he does and does not do, and be on guard against negligence. The method, then, involves this examination of the soul and a discussion of the experiences of bliss and the ecstasies that result from its influence. Furthermore, there are certain practices and technical terms unique to the Sufis that are not used by the general public.

When the time arrived that the various religious sciences were recorded in writing—and were no longer simply kept in memory—books were also written about this science. Thus, from being merely a practice, it became a science in the strict sense.

But again, through these exercises and ecstasies, the veil of the senses is often torn and the upper

### RELIGIOUS ATTITUDE AND LIFE IN ISLĀM

spiritual regions are directly perceived. The soul belongs to these worlds and is thus enabled to reach a state where it beholds them, rather than simply knowing about them. This is absolute perception, and in this state, the soul receives various divine gifts and favors directly from God. Thus, Ṣūfīs come to know the real nature of things, future events are revealed to them, and through their zeal and the power of their souls, they can influence the things of this lower world. Yet the more they advance, the less attention they pay to such matters, eventually regarding them as temptations and lower manifestations.

In later times, a group arose that focused more and more on this tearing of the veil of the senses. They had different methods for achieving this, but they disciplined the body and nourished the soul so that it could exercise its essential faculty of perception freely and fully. They maintained, then, that all existence in its true essence could be perceived by the soul, from the highest to the lowest. However, the soul must be guided by righteousness; otherwise, its perceptions would be distorted—as if in a convex or concave mirror—like those of magicians, Christians, etc.<note>¹</note> Apparently, the meaning is that anyone’s soul could be enabled to see the spiritual world through fasting and solitude, but only the righteous soul would see it perfectly.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Ṣūfī spiritual perception, detailing the transition from the psychological and ethical discipline of the soul to a metaphysical inquiry into the nature of existence and the relationship between God and His creation.</meta>

### THE ASCETIC-ECSTATIC LIFE IN ISLĀM

Then these Ṣūfīs passed from regarding their method as a discipline of the soul to treating it as a means of reaching a knowledge of the true nature of things. That is, from being psychological and ethical, it becomes metaphysical. In a later edition of his book,<note>¹ De Slane’s translation, Vol. III, p. 93.</note> Ibn Khaldūn inserted a long passage at this point regarding this, and especially regarding the relationship of God to His creatures. It is much more theological than psychological, so I shall give little of it here. The traditional attitude, that of the early scholars, was that God and His creation were absolutely separate. That, of course, introduced logical difficulties, and the scholastic theologians came in time to teach that God was neither separate from nor joined to His creation. The philosophers held that He was neither outside the world nor within it; and the later Ṣūfīs held that He was identified (*muttaḥid*) with created beings, either by fusion (*ḥulūl*) in them, or because these beings were He Himself and did not contain, either in whole or in part, anything except Him. He then goes on to discuss "separation" in its two aspects: separation in place and separateness as to essence, individuality, existence, and attributes. The opposite of the latter is unification (*ittiḥād*). This last view, in different phases, was the perspective of many Ṣūfīs.

Some of these explain—but their explanation, according to Ibn Khaldūn, is obscure to an extreme degree—

and the matter clearly cannot be expressed in ordinary language—that all things are appearances, which emanate in a specific order from God. They refer back to a saying of Allāh’s which they claim:

> “I was a hidden treasure, and I desired to be known; so I created the creatures so that they might know me.”

The scheme is clearly one of Plotinian emanation, beginning with the realities in the world of ideas; these gradually manifest themselves as the spheres, and so down to the elemental world where they become individuals and appearances. This is called the “self-revealing” (*tajallà*) of Allāh, and it is only truly understandable through mystical contemplation, which sees all things in God, just as it sees God in all things.<note>¹ See the article on ᶜAbd al-Razzāq in the *Encyclopaedia of Islām*, Vol. I, pp. 61 ff.</note>

But another school of Ṣūfīs emphasized the idea of absolute unity. The multiplicity which we see around us arises from man’s multiple senses. Just as there would be no color if there were no light, so the existence of all perceptible things depends on the existence of senses perceiving them—that is, on percipient beings. This, you will notice, is exactly Berkeley’s position: that the *esse* of a thing is its *percipi*. Eliminate all perceptions, and then all beings return to one unity. That is God, who is spread through all beings and unites them without direction, appearance, form, or substance. To

# THE ASCETIC-ECSTATIC LIFE IN ISLĀM 171

To this theory, Ibn Khaldūn raises some "common-sense" objections, but ultimately maintains that according to the Ṣūfīs themselves, the mystical perception of unity is only a stage. The neophyte must pass through this; and to go beyond it is difficult; but when he does, he reaches a further stage where he can again distinguish between beings and finds that they are no longer swallowed up in oneness.

But these concepts have undoubtedly had a great influence. So, too, has the Ismāʿīlite and Shīʿite belief that at all times in this world there is some representative of God whose right it is to rule, teach, and guide his people. To this Ibn Khaldūn traces the development of the doctrine of the hierarchy of saints, from the <term>Axis</term> downward, which I have already described; but as a good Sunnite, he, of course, rejects the Shīʿite view. All of this is comparatively recent; he cannot find that it was practiced by the older Ṣūfīs.

Ibn Khaldūn finally divides the general Ṣūfī position into four heads: (1) the discipline of the soul; holding it to a strict account for its actions; the tastes of bliss and the ecstasies which come upon it; its ascent from one spiritual stage to another; (2) the unveiling of the unseen world and the perception within it of spiritual things, of the real natures of things, and how they proceed from there; (3) the control of material things by the grace of God; (4) those wild, fanciful expressions which many of them utter

He also categorizes the Sufi experience into four parts: soul discipline, spiritual unveiling, divine grace over matter, and ecstatic speech.</summary>

To the first, the discipline of the soul, no one can take the slightest objection. That way lies the very essence of eternal salvation. Nor to the third, the miracles of the saints, can any objection be taken, provided they are carefully distinguished—which is easy—from the miracles of the prophets. The existence of these wonders is absolutely certain and was approved by the Companions. As to the second, the unveiling of the unseen world, the case is more complicated. Most of what they say is like the “obscure” verses in the *Qur’ān*. It is uttered in ecstasy, and he who is not in ecstasy cannot understand it. The words used give no clue to what they mean, for words are conventional signs for known things, and these things are not known. You will remember here Occam’s form of Nominalism. It is better, therefore, to leave these sayings alone, just as we leave alone the “obscure” passages in the *Qur’ān*. He to whom God has given knowledge of the meaning of any of these sayings, in a way which agrees with the plain sense of the faith, may say, “How noble they are for eternal salvation!” In this, you will not fail to observe precisely the phenomena of “speaking with tongues,” yet with one curious difference. Here there were no “tongues;” they spoke their own language, and hence the frequent scandal. It is very probable, however, that they used qur’ānic

But the matter went even further. Fourthly, the Sufis themselves recognized a class of utterances which they called <term>shaṭaḥāt</term>, or “overflowings in ecstasy as of drunkenness”—all of which is found in the etymology and usage of the root word. These were metaphorical expressions used by them when self-control had been lost. For these, they were held accountable and blamed by the canonists. Ibn Khaldun thinks that, in fairness, we should consider the following points. First, if they are people of known goodness and an overpowering ecstasy has fallen upon them—to express which there is no fixed language—then they should not be blamed for using language unsuitable for other people and situations. We may take for granted that such people do not intend it in a blasphemous sense. But secondly, if they are not people of known goodness, doubt may arise. Thirdly, if they use such language when they are not in a state of ecstasy, but are in control of themselves and aware of what they say, they are clearly to be held accountable. Finally, Ibn Khaldun agreed with the older Sufis in holding that all such attempts to look behind the veil, to comprehend the whole nature of things, and all talk regarding such perceptions should be suppressed. The knowledge of God is too vast, and His creation too

I now turn to some specific examples of this religious life, and I will begin with the case for which we have the most complete and reliable information. It is that of al-Ghazzālī, who experienced a distinct and lasting conversion in 1095 AD. He spent the rest of his life partly as a Sufi wanderer and partly as a teacher of Sufi theology, dying in 1111. He left behind an autobiography that is unique in Arabic for the intensity and thoroughness of its self-revelation. Unfortunately, he was influenced by the practical focus of Islam, and so he could not be satisfied with letting his book stand simply as a human document, nor even as a justification for his own life (*apologia pro vita sua*). He felt he had to turn it into a manual of religious defense suited to his time, and in doing so, he undoubtedly softened the personal tone. By doing this, he provided perhaps the most striking example of that persistent Muslim tendency toward instruction which does not allow an artist—whether conscious or unconscious—to present a living person to the reader and let them speak for themselves, but instead feels a need to systematize and explain everything. In this, Muslim writers differ significantly from the poets of pre-Islamic Arabia, who took a frank delight in the simple expression of themselves without considering their audience.

But, despite all this, al-Ghazzālī’s book is unique, and

I must now present to you significant extracts from it. As you may remember, it has already been used by Mr. William James in his *Varieties of Religious Experience* as the standard Muslim example of conversion. He wrote it late in life, when he was more than fifty (lunar) years old and only a few years before his early death. But from his earliest youth, the search to know reality had been a passion for him, and he recognized that this passion belonged to a nature implanted in him by God. It was not any choice of his own that made him continue seeking. He means, I think, that there is nothing sinful or unnatural in such an attitude. From his early youth, then, he could no longer believe simply because he was taught to do so. He looked around and saw that other children were taught differently—whether Jews or Christians—and they grew up as Jews or Christians. But there is a tradition that the Prophet said, "Every child is born a Muslim by nature; then his parents make him a Jew, a Christian, or a Magian." So he was moved to ask what the essence of this fundamental nature was, and what the essence was of the opposing traditional creeds taught by parents and teachers, and how he could distinguish between these traditional, accepted views, seeing that their origins were mere instructions and there was much contradiction in distinguishing the true from the false among them:

--- Page 200 ---
<meta>This page continues the translation of al-Ghazzālī’s spiritual autobiography, focusing on his philosophical quest for absolute certainty and his skepticism toward traditional and reasoned knowledge.</meta>

<page-num>176</page-num>
<header>RELIGIOUS ATTITUDE AND LIFE IN ISLĀM</header>

So I said to myself [he continues<note>Al-Munqidh min aḍ-ḍalāl, Cairo edition, 1303 A.H., p. 4.</note>], "What I want is knowledge of the real nature of things. Therefore, I must ask: what is the essence of knowledge?" It then seemed to me that certain knowledge is that which reveals the thing known in such a way that no doubt remains, nor is it accompanied by the possibility of error or illusion, nor can the mind even imagine such a thing. Security from error must accompany certainty to such a degree that if someone—for example, someone who could turn a stone into gold or a staff into a snake—claimed the ability to prove that knowledge false, it would not produce any doubt or denial. For when I know that ten is more than three, if someone says to me, "No, three is more than ten, and I will prove it by changing this staff into a snake," and he actually changes it, and I see him do it, I still do not doubt what I know because of that. The only result for me would be wonder at how he could do such a thing, but never any doubt regarding what I already know.

Then I realized that everything I did not know in this fashion, and of the certainty of which I was not assured in this way, was a kind of knowledge that could offer neither trust nor certainty; and knowledge that is not accompanied by trust is not certain knowledge. So I examined all the things I knew and found that I possessed no knowledge that could be described in this way, except for sense perceptions and necessary intuitive knowledge. Then, in my despair, I said, "There is no hope of resolving what is doubtful except through what is clear—that is, through sense perceptions and necessary intuitive knowledge. Therefore, I must test these first, so it may become clear to me whether my trust in objects of perception and my security from error in necessary knowledge is of the same kind as the trust I formerly had in traditional knowledge and the trust most men have in reasoned knowledge, or if it is a guaranteed trust without deception or limit."

<summary>Al-Ghazzālī defines certain knowledge as that which is immune to doubt, even in the face of miracles, and begins to question if sense perception and intuition are truly reliable.</summary>

<keywords>al-Ghazzālī, epistemology, certainty, sense perception, intuitive knowledge, skepticism, Al-Munqidh min aḍ-ḍalāl</keywords>

--- Page 201 ---
<meta>This page continues al-Ghazālī’s narrative of his intellectual crisis, specifically his investigation into whether sensory perception or basic logical axioms can provide the absolute certainty he seeks.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>177</page-num>
<header>THE ASCETIC-ECSTATIC LIFE IN ISLĀM</header>

So I turned zealously to consider sensory objects and necessary knowledge, to see whether I could bring myself to doubt them. Doubt reached such a point with me that I could not permit myself to trust even sensory objects. Doubt regarding them kept spreading, and I said, "How can you be sure of sensory objects? The strongest sense is sight, yet it looks at a shadow and sees the shadow staying still, judging that there is no motion. Then, through testing and observation after a time, it knows that the shadow does move—not suddenly, but gradually, bit by bit, never standing still. And it looks at a star and sees that star as small as a gold coin, but geometry proves that it is larger than the earth. In such cases, the senses judge in one way, but reason judges in another; reason proves the senses false and accuses them of deceit in a way that cannot be answered."

So I said, "My trust in sensory objects is also gone; perhaps there can be no trust except in those intellectual results that are axiomatic—such as our saying that ten is more than three, or that negation and affirmation cannot exist together in one thing, and that a thing cannot be both created and eternal *a parte ante*, existent and non-existent, necessary and impossible." But sensory objects said, "What assurance do you have that your trust in the conclusions of reason is not like your trust in sensory objects? You used to trust in me; then came the test of reason and proved me false, and if it had not been for the test of reason, you would have gone on believing me. Perhaps, then, beyond the perceptions of reason there is another judge; whenever it appears, reason will be proven false by it, just as reason appeared and proved the senses false. The fact that such a perception has not appeared does not prove it is impossible."¹

At an answer to this my soul somewhat paused and justified

---

<note>¹ The confusion here between singular and plural—the sense and its results—is also present in the original.</note>

<summary>Al-Ghazali details his rejection of sensory perception as a source of certain knowledge, using examples like the movement of shadows and the size of stars to show how reason contradicts the senses. He then extends this skepticism to reason itself, questioning whether a higher form of perception might eventually prove logical axioms false.</summary>

<meta>This page continues al-Ghazālī's account of his skepticism. Having established that reason can prove sensory perceptions false, he now considers the possibility that a higher faculty might similarly prove reason false, using the analogy of dreaming.</meta>

<page-num>178</page-num>
<header>RELIGIOUS ATTITUDE AND LIFE IN ISLĀM</header>

<margin>*</margin>
<margin>*</margin>
...her confusion by citing the phenomena of dreaming. She said, “Do you not see that in sleep you believe in certain things, and you imagine conditions and believe that they have reality and stability, and in that state you do not doubt them? Then you wake up, and you know that all your imaginations and beliefs had neither foundation nor use. How, then, are you sure that everything which in your waking time you believe in—because of either sense or reason—is not fact simply in relation to your condition at that time? But it is possible that a condition may overtake you, the relationship of which to your waking state is like the relationship of your waking state to your dreams; and your waking state is a sleep in relation to it. Then, whenever that condition comes upon you, you will be certain that all which you have vainly imagined through your reason consists of nothing but baseless phantoms.

Or, perhaps, that condition is what the <term>Sufis</term> claim to be their state; since they assert that in their heightened states—which occur when they immerse themselves in their souls and are apart from their physical senses—they have open <term>soul-perception</term> of certain conditions which do not agree with the results of reason. Or, perhaps that condition is death, since the <term>Prophet</term> said, ‘Mankind is asleep, and when they die, they are awakened.’ So, perhaps the life of this world is a sleep in relation to the next world; and when a man dies, things appear to him that are contrary to what he observes now. Then it will be said to him, ‘We have stripped your veil from you, and your sight, today, is sharp.’ ”¹

When these thoughts came to me, they made a deep impression on me, and I desired some remedy for them, but it was not easy. They could be refuted only by means of proof; and no proof can be constructed except by combining primary facts of knowledge; but when these are not granted, a proof cannot be assembled. This disease troubled me and

---

<note>¹ Qur'an 50:21.</note>

<summary>Al-Ghazālī explores the possibility that waking life is a dream-like state compared to a higher spiritual reality or the afterlife, casting doubt on the absolute certainty of reason. He finds himself in a deep intellectual crisis, unable to construct proofs because the very foundations of primary knowledge are in question.</summary>

<meta>This page continues al-Ghazālī's autobiographical account of his period of deep skepticism, following his realization that neither the senses nor reason provide an infallible foundation for certainty.</meta>

remained with me for almost two months. During that time, I was an absolute skeptic in my mind, if not in my speech.

At length, God healed me of that disease, and my soul returned to health and balance; the necessary intellectual truths returned, accepted and certain. This did not happen by means of a proof or by any form of words, but by a light which God cast into my breast. That light is the key to most knowledge; and whoever believes that the mystical unveiling is based upon abstract proofs narrows the vast mercy of God. When the Apostle was asked what the meaning of “opening” was in the saying of God, “Whom God wills to guide, He opens his breast to <term>Islām</term>,”<note>Qur'an 6:125.</note> he said, “It is light which God throws into the heart.” And when he was asked, “And what is its sign?” he said, “Withdrawing from the abode of deceit and returning to the abode of eternity.” About this same thing, the Prophet said, “God Most High created His creatures in darkness; then He sprinkled some of His light upon them.” It is from that light that the unveiling ought to be sought, and that light is cast forth suddenly out of the divine bounty on certain occasions and should be watched for; as the Prophet said, “Look, in the days of your earthly life, your Lord has outbreathings; be ready to meet them.” And the point of all these narratives is to show that every diligence should be used in seeking, until that is reached which was not sought. Fundamental conceptions cannot be sought, for they are already with us. And if that which is with us is sought, it cannot be found and vanishes. He who seeks what is not sought cannot be suspected of falling short in seeking what is sought.

When, then, God healed me of this disease by His grace and vast bounty, I observed that the different kinds of seekers around me divided into four classes. There were the scholastic theologians who claimed to be guided by judgment and

<summary>Al-Ghazali describes how God eventually cured his skepticism by casting a divine light into his heart, allowing him to accept fundamental truths without the need for rational proofs. He concludes by identifying four classes of seekers after truth whom he intends to study.</summary>

<meta>This page continues al-Ghazālī's autobiographical account following his recovery from deep skepticism. He now identifies the four specific groups of seekers who claim to possess the truth, marking the beginning of his systematic investigation into their doctrines.</meta>

discussion. There were the allegorists <note>holders of an inner meaning</note> who asserted that they possessed a doctrine of their own, and that they were distinguished from others in that they learned from an infallible Head. There were the philosophers, who asserted that they followed logic and absolute proof. And there were the <term>Ṣūfīs</term>, who claimed that they were distinguished by being in the presence of God, and that they possessed immediate soul-perception and unveiling.

> Then I said to myself, “The truth must necessarily lie with one of these four, for these are they who walk the paths of seeking the truth. If, then, the truth is hidden from them, there remains no hope of success in gaining it. For there is no hope in returning to a traditional faith after it has once been abandoned, since the essential condition for the holder of a traditional faith is that he should not know that he is a traditionalist. Whenever he knows that, the glass of his traditional faith is broken. That is a breaking that cannot be mended and a separation that cannot be reunited by any sewing or putting together, unless it is melted in the fire and given another new form.”

<term>Al-Ghazzālī</term> now turns to a discussion of these different pathways to truth, one after the other. It is here that his desire to write a manual of apologetics interferes most with the value of his book as an autobiographical revelation of himself. We can hardly believe that he worked through these different schools in the calm and orderly fashion which he now sets down. But in the putting together of this systematized search for truth, it is perfectly clear that he is simply arranging, in what he thinks is logical form, the experiences which had come to him, broken and irregularly, in the life of his soul.

<vocab>allegorists, philosophers, Ṣūfīs, Al-Ghazzālī, traditional faith, apologetics</vocab>

<summary>Al-Ghazzālī identifies four groups—allegorists, philosophers, and Sufis—as the only potential keepers of the truth, noting that once a person's uncritical traditional faith is broken, it can never be restored to its original state. The text suggests that Al-Ghazzālī's orderly intellectual journey was likely a retrospective systematization of a much more chaotic personal experience.</summary>

There can be no question, for example, that his strange descent into absolute skepticism and his recovery—not through argument but through the abundance of God’s mercy—are narrated exactly as they took place. Similarly, as we now turn to his account of his experiences on the path of the <term>Ṣūfīs</term>, I do not think we will have any doubt that here, too, is a genuine page from his inner life.

I do not think that this was his first acquaintance with <term>Ṣūfī</term> methods. Nor do I believe that he had not, himself, experimented with their practices earlier in life. As a young man, he had evidently experimented with everything, and later found it all to be in vain. You will remember, for example, the story of his dream, which I have already provided,¹ and the advice his <term>shaykh</term> gave him. I find it difficult to place that dream in his life after his conversion and, therefore, feel compelled to believe that he is now returning to ground he has already walked.

He describes his second trial of <term>Ṣūfīism</term> as follows:²

> Then I turned my attention to the path of the <term>Ṣūfīs</term>. I knew that their path could be completed only by means of both theory and practice. On the theoretical side, its result is climbing the steep ascent of the soul and purging the soul of its blameworthy characteristics and qualities, until one may reach the state of being alone in the mind with God and adorning the mind with the constant thought of God.

<meta>This page continues al-Ghazālī’s account of his transition from the theoretical study of Sufism to the realization that its essence lies in direct spiritual experience and transformation.</meta>

<page-num>182</page-num>

-> RELIGIOUS ATTITUDE AND LIFE IN ISLĀM <-

The theory of it was easier for me than its practice. So I began to acquire this knowledge by studying their books <note>Al-Ghazzālī here gives a list of several books.</note> until I had reached the peak of their theoretical goals and had learned as much of their path as could be gained by studying and hearing about it.

It was clear to me that it was impossible to attain the most essential elements through study; these called for experience (*<term>dhawq</term>*) and a spiritual state (*<term>ḥāl</term>*) and a transformation of one's qualities. What a great difference there is between someone who knows the definition of health and the definition of satiety, and their causes and conditions, and someone who is actually healthy and satisfied! Or between someone who knows the definition of drunkenness—that it is a term for a condition resulting from vapors that ascend from the stomach to the seat of thought, taking control of them—and someone who is actually drunk! The person who is drunk does not know the definition and science of drunkenness; he is simply drunk and has no theoretical knowledge at all. The sober man knows the definition of drunkenness and its elements, yet he possesses nothing of the actual experience of drunkenness. Similarly, a physician who is ill knows the definition of health, its causes, and its remedies, even though he lacks health himself. There is a similar distinction between knowing the nature, the conditions, and the causes of self-restraint, and actually possessing self-restraint as your personal state and keeping the soul detached from the world.

So I knew for certain that for the <term>Ṣūfīs</term>, spiritual states and not definitions were what mattered; and that I had acquired all that could be gained through learning. What remained could not be reached by hearing and studying, but only by experience and following a specific course of action.

From the sciences I had studied and the paths I had taken in searching out various kinds of knowledge, both religious and intellectual, I had attained a firm belief in God, in prophecy, and in the Last Day. These three funda-

<summary>Al-Ghazālī concludes that Sufism cannot be mastered through books alone, as it requires direct spiritual experience (dhawq) rather than theoretical definitions. He realizes that while he has mastered the theory and established his faith, the actual practice of the Sufi path requires a transformation of the soul.</summary>

<meta>This page continues al-Ghazālī’s account of his internal struggle between his worldly success as a scholar in Baghdad and his growing realization that true salvation requires spiritual asceticism and the abandonment of worldly fame.</meta>

<page-num>183</page-num>

### THE ASCETIC-ECSTATIC LIFE IN ISLĀM

The fundamentals of faith were established in my soul, not through abstract, formal proof, but through connections, associations, and experiences, all the elements of which cannot be expressed briefly. It had become clear to me that I had no hope of attaining salvation in the afterlife except through piety and by restraining the soul from its desires. I realized that the beginning of all this must be the severing of the heart’s ties to this world—the abode of deception—and a return to the abode of eternity, by striving toward God with an absolute purpose.

I also knew that this could not be fully achieved except by turning away from ambition and wealth, and by fleeing from entanglements and attachments. I looked at my circumstances, and behold, I was entangled in attachments that surrounded me on all sides. I examined my works—the best of which were my studies and teaching—and behold, in them I was pursuing knowledge that was insignificant and useless regarding the afterlife. Then I considered my purpose in studying, and behold, it was not purely for the sake of seeking the face of God; rather, its motivation and driving force were the pursuit of reputation and the spread of my fame.

I became certain that I was on the brink of a crumbling precipice and was looking down into the Fire, if I did not turn back and reform my condition. So I continued meditating on that for a time. While I still had freedom of choice, one day I would resolve to leave Baghdād and separate myself from those circumstances, and another day I would waver in that resolution. I would take one step forward and the other back. I could not maintain a pure desire to seek the afterlife in the morning without the army of passions attacking and breaking it by evening. The desires of the world kept dragging me by their chains to stay, while the herald of faith kept proclaiming, “Travel! Travel! There remains of life but a little."

<summary>Al-Ghazālī describes his spiritual crisis, realizing that his prestigious teaching career in Baghdad was motivated by fame rather than God, leading him to struggle with the decision to abandon his worldly life for the sake of his salvation.</summary>
<keywords>faith, piety, salvation, soul, desire, afterlife, Baghdād, reputation, asceticism, Islām, al-Ghazālī</keywords>

--- Page 208 ---
<meta>This page continues al-Ghazālī’s account of his intense spiritual struggle. After realizing that his scholarly fame was a distraction from God, he wavers between his worldly status in Baghdad and the necessity of asceticism for his salvation.</meta>

<page-num>184</page-num>

<header>RELIGIOUS ATTITUDE AND LIFE IN ISLĀM</header>

"The long journey is before you; and all that you are doing, of labor and of knowledge, is a vain phantom. If you do not prepare yourself now for the world to come, when will you prepare yourself? And if you will not cut your ties now, when will you cut them?" Thereupon my desire would be aroused and my purpose fixed. But the devil would return and say, "This is a temporary condition; beware lest you heed it, for it will pass swiftly; and if you obey it and abandon this great honor and secure position—free from disturbance and embitterment—and this recognized authority, clear of hostile strife, perhaps your soul would have adjusted itself to it and returning will not be easy for you."

<margin>1 *Qur'an* 27:63.</margin>
So I continued swaying between the attractions of the lusts of this world and the summons of the other world for almost six months, the first of which was Rajab, A. H. 488. In that month, the matter passed from the boundary of choice to compulsion, in that God locked my tongue until it was bound so that I could not teach. I would put pressure upon myself to teach for a single day, in order to satisfy certain persons, but I could not bring my tongue to utter a word. Then, this paralysis of my tongue brought a sorrow upon my mind; my digestion and desire for food and drink were destroyed; I could not swallow a drop nor digest a mouthful. My strength began to fail, and the physicians despaired of my cure. "This is a mental trouble," they said, "which has come to affect the physical organization, and it can be healed only by resting the mind from the anxiety which has befallen it." Then, feeling my weakness and giving up my own will entirely, I took refuge with God, as one in desperate need and with no resource left. And He, "Who answers the desperate when he calls,"<note>1 Quran 27:63.</note> answered me and made it easy for me to turn away from ambition, wealth, family, and companions.

...extricate himself from his impossible position in Baghdad at the court of the Caliph. He used the pretext of wishing to go on a pilgrimage to Mecca, although his real intention was to go to Syria and stay there, immersing himself in the ascetic and contemplative life of the Ṣūfīs. Apparently, his departure caused as much commotion as if a distinguished professor or perhaps a bishop among us were to announce that he had finally been converted and had determined to abandon the world. In the Roman Catholic Church, such a man would enter a monastic order; in Protestantism, I fear there would be no place for him. 

Al-Ghazzālī became a wandering Ṣūfī monk. He went to Syria and spent almost two years there in retirement and solitude. He practiced Ṣūfī religious exercises, purifying his mind and heart, working through his character flaws, and giving himself entirely to the thought of God. After a time, the desire to truly make the pilgrimage to Mecca rose within him; the clouds had lifted, and his religious life was once again normal. It was as if one of us had withdrawn from the communion of the Lord’s Supper and had again found his way back. So he went to Mecca and performed his religious duties there, and was gradually drawn back into the current of events. For ten years, his life was divided between cultivating his own soul and caring for others in the world. The cares

And [he said <note>Page 32 of the text cited above.</note>] there were revealed to me in the course of these periods of solitude things which cannot be numbered or exhausted. What I mention now, for the benefit of others, is that I know with certainty that the Ṣūfīs alone follow the path to God; that their mode of life is the best of all ways, their path the most sure of paths, and their character the purest of all. If intellectuals with their intellect, the learned with their wisdom, and students of the mysteries of <term>divine law</term> with their knowledge were to join together to alter anything of the Ṣūfī mode of life or its characteristics, or to exchange these for something better, they would find no way of doing so.

All actions of the Ṣūfīs, whether in movement or at rest, whether internal or external, are derived from the light of the lamp of prophecy. Aside from the light of prophecy, there is no other illumination on the face of the earth from which enlightenment can be sought. In short, whatever is said concerning their pure path is true. Its first condition is a total cleansing of the mind from everything except God. Its key of entrance—much like the first cry of prayer, which signifies that all else is now forbidden—is the complete immersion of the mind in God. But that is the end only regarding what falls under free will and personal effort from its start; it is truly only the beginning of the path, and what comes before it is like a foyer. From the beginning of the path, revelations and clear <term>soul-perceptions</term> begin; and the traveler on it, while awake, sees angels and the souls of prophets, and hears their voices,

and learns from them. Then his spiritual condition advances from witnessing forms and likenesses to stages where the limits of language are too narrow, and no expression in words is possible; for such an expression would contain manifest errors against which there could be no guarding. A point of nearness to God is reached which some have attempted to describe as <term>*ḥulūl*</term>, "fusion of being;" some as <term>*ittiḥād*</term>, "identification;" and some as <term>*wuṣūl*</term>, "union." But all these expressions contain error, and he who is in that state should say no more than the poet:

> And there happened what happened, of which I will not speak;
> So think well of it, and ask not about the Good.¹

He who has not been granted actual experience of anything of this can know only the name of the essence of prophecy. The miracles (<term>*karāmāt*</term>) of the saints are in reality the beginnings of the prophetic state. That was, also, the first condition of the Prophet when he went to <term>Mount Ḥirā</term> and was alone there with his Lord, engaged in religious exercises, until the Arabs said, "Muḥammad is passionately in love with his Lord." Whoever follows the <term>Ṣūfīs</term> in their path can verify this condition by experience (<term>*dhawq*</term>), and whoever is not granted actual experience can be assured of it by the test of listening. If he associates much with them, he will understand that as a certainty through their circumstances. He who keeps company with them will gain faith from them; for they are not the kind of people whose companion can be lost. But let him who cannot keep company with them know assuredly the possibility of what we have been speaking of by certain proofs, such as we have laid down in the *Book of the Marvels of the Heart.*² Verification, then, by proof is knowledge; having intimate contact with the essence of that condition is

<meta>This page continues the discussion of al-Ghazzālī’s three stages of spiritual certainty—direct experience, observation, and faith—as they relate to the proof of prophecy and the Sufi concept of "states."</meta>

<page-num>188</page-num>

experience; receiving by the test of listening, with approval, is faith; these are three stages.

On this basis, then, <term>al-Ghazzālī</term> would establish the proof of the reality of <term>prophecy</term>. The soul bears witness to it, either through absolute experience of essentially similar phenomena, through first-hand observation of the existence of such similar phenomena, or through certain proofs which I hope to address later. The basis of it all is, therefore, what the <term>Ṣūfīs</term> call “<term>states</term>.” The word is a puzzling one to translate and must be rendered differently in different contexts. Primarily, it means simply a psychological condition, arising without effort or apparent cause, as opposed to the results of reasoning. It is feeling, as opposed to reason; an immediate consciousness, as opposed to derivative knowledge.<sup>1</sup>

The data from such phenomena of the inner life were for <term>al-Ghazzālī</term> and his school—that is, practically for <term>al-Islām</term> after him—the only certain basis for religious faith and knowledge. He never really abandoned

---

<note>
<sup>1</sup> <term>Ibn ʿArabī</term>, the great western mystic, distinguishes them in this way:
> “Knowledge consists of three levels: (*a*) rational, meaning any knowledge that is self-evident or a consequence of considering a proof, provided one encounters what that proof signifies; (*b*) knowledge of ‘<term>states</term>’; these are reached only by ‘<term>tasting</term>’ and the purely intellectual person cannot know this or arrive at it through proof; such is the knowledge of the sweetness of honey, the bitterness of aloes, or of emotion and longing; only he who has experienced these can know them; (*c*) <term>knowledge of the secrets</term>; it is above the sphere of reason and is a knowledge breathed into the mind by the Holy Spirit; it is unique to prophets and saints; through it, they mysteriously take all knowledge to themselves” (Comm. on *<term>Ihyā</term>*, Vol. VII, p. 245).
</note>

<summary>Al-Ghazzālī argues that the reality of prophecy is proven through the soul's direct experience or observation of spiritual "states," which provide a certainty that reason alone cannot reach. These internal phenomena, described by Ibn ʿArabī as "tasting" or "knowledge of secrets," became the foundational basis for religious faith in post-Ghazzālian Islam.</summary>
<keywords>al-Ghazzālī, prophecy, Ṣūfīs, states, al-Islām, Ibn ʿArabī, tasting, knowledge of secrets, Ihyā, religious epistemology, mysticism</keywords>

--- Page 213 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of al-Ghazzālī’s skepticism toward reason and his elevation of "states" as the foundation of religious certainty in Islam.</meta>

<page-num>189</page-num>
<header>THE ASCETIC-ECSTATIC LIFE IN ISLĀM</header>

his skeptical position regarding the results of reason; and, in fact, he tended to attribute all human knowledge more or less directly to revelation, through either prophets or saints. For him, as for <term>Ibn Khaldūn</term>, philosophy was bankrupt, and he retained only enough trust in reason to enable him to dialectically destroy the possibility of a metaphysical system on one hand, and to establish the authority of psychological states on the other. In the revelation of man’s emotional nature, not in the results of his reason, lie fact and certainty; for, on that side, there is a spark of the divine nature in man. But reason is a mere utilitarian drudge, limited to a narrow scope, and to be distrusted beyond that. Further, the <term>Ṣūfīs</term> used the word “states” to also indicate those conditions of joy or sorrow, elation or depression, which descend upon the heart of the devotee in constant change. And as a final development, a “state,” in the highest sense, is a state of ecstasy when the devotee has passed out of himself, is unconscious of the world, and conscious only of God. But none of these could be controlled by the will; the spirit came and went. As with the <term>Scholar Gypsy</term>, “it took heaven-sent moments for that skill.” Yet this “skill” was man’s only guide. That the religion of <term>Islām</term> arrived at this final point, in spite of the blunt and, one might almost say, materialistic monotheism of <term>Muḥammad</term>, is one of the strangest developments in all the history of religion. The

<summary>This page explores how al-Ghazzālī and later Islamic thinkers prioritized emotional "states" and revelation over reason, which they viewed as a limited tool. It describes the development of Sufi ecstasy as a central, if surprising, element of Islamic religious life.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of al-Ghazzālī’s findings regarding the spiritual nature of man, specifically focusing on the concept of "tasting" (experience) and the dual nature of the body and heart.</meta>

<page-num>190</page-num>
<header>RELIGIOUS ATTITUDE AND LIFE IN ISLĀM</header>

the wheel came full circle and seems now nailed in its place.

Another word worthy of notice is that which I have rendered above as “experience.” Literally, it means “taste,” and is either the act of tasting or the taste itself. Then it has various derived applications: tasting in any way; taste in language and literature; tasting the savor of the divine truth or essence in the soul; and all such soul-experiences.

In accordance with the above, al-Ghazzālī gives in the following words his final results as to the nature of man and the character of man’s intercourse with the unseen world: <note>P. 38 of the *Munqidh*.</note>

> Then, after I had persevered in withdrawal from the world and in the solitary life for almost ten years, there became plain to me and certain, in the course of that, by innumerable causes—at one time by spiritual experience, at another time by demonstrative knowledge, and at another time by acceptance on faith—that man is created with a body and with a heart. I mean by “heart” his spiritual essence which is the center of the knowledge of God, as opposed to the flesh-and-blood organ in which dead bodies and the lower animals share. Further, it became plain to me that as the body has a health in which is its happiness and a sickness in which is its destruction, so the heart, similarly, has a health and a soundness (none is saved “except he who comes to God with a sound heart” <note>*Qur'an* xxvi, 89.</note>) and a sickness, in which is its eternal destruction for the world to come, as God has said, “In their hearts is a sickness.” <note>*Ibid*., ii, 9.</note> That ignorance with regard

<column-break/>

<summary>al-Ghazzālī defines the "heart" as a spiritual essence distinct from the physical organ and the center of divine knowledge. He explains that, like the body, the spiritual heart possesses its own states of health and sickness, which determine a person's salvation in the afterlife.</summary>
<keywords>al-Ghazzālī, Munqidh, spiritual experience, heart, soul, health of the heart, Quranic interpretation, mysticism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues al-Ghazzālī’s explanation of spiritual health, specifically comparing the cures for the "heart" to the medical treatments for the body.</meta>

<page-num>191</page-num>
<header>THE ASCETIC-ECSTATIC LIFE IN ISLĀM 191</header>

to God is a destructive poison; and that disobedience to God, through following one's desires, is the heart’s grievous ailment; that knowledge of God is its reviving remedy, and obedience to God in opposition to those desires is its healing medicine; that there is no way of treating it, to remove its disease and to restore it to health, except by medicines; just as there is no way of treating the body, except by the same.

And just as medicines for the body produce an effect in restoring health through a property within them which the intellect cannot reach, but concerning which physicians must be believed—who have learned that property from the prophets who, in turn, came to know the properties of things through the gift of prophecy—so it became clear to me that the intellect could not understand the way the medicines of the heart work. These are exercises of devotion, in their definitions and amounts, defined and prescribed by the prophets. Regarding this, the prophets must be followed, who attained these properties by the light of prophecy and not by means of reason. And just as medicines are composed of certain types and amounts, and some are double the weight and amount of others; and as a secret lies in the difference of these amounts belonging to their properties; so acts of devotion, which are the medicine for the heart's disease, are composed of actions differing in kind or amount. For example, “prostration” is double that of “bowing,” and the dawn prayer is half the afternoon prayer in amount; and in this, there is a certain secret regarding their properties, which cannot be learned except by the light of prophecy.

Therefore, those have been most foolish who have desired to discover a law for these things by means of reason, or have imagined that they were so given by accident—rather than because of a secret within them which required them to be so, by way of this property. And just as in medicines there are bases which are

<meta>This page continues al-Ghazzālī’s argument that reason serves only to validate prophecy and submit to it, just as a patient submits to a religious "physician."</meta>

fundamental elements and supplemental additions, every one of which has peculiar effects on the operation of the foundations; so supererogatory acts of devotion and usage are complements completing the effects of the basic acts of devotion.

In short, the prophets are the physicians for the diseases of hearts. The only use and authority for reason is that it should teach us this, and should bear witness to the truth of prophecy and to its own inability to reach what the eye of prophecy can perceive. It should take us by the hand and commit us to prophecy, just as the blind are committed to their guides and the sick to their physicians. This is the work and the limit of reason; beyond this, it may not go, except to make known what the physician has taught it. I learned these things with the assurance of absolute perception during my solitude and my retirement from the world.

Thus does al-Ghazzālī build an ingenious defense of the mechanical details of the Muslim ritual law based on his agnostic theory of knowledge. I shall return later to his *Science of the Heart*, but in the meantime, I must leave him with one remark. The exercises by which the ecstatic state was induced in his case seem to have been of a simply devotional and personal character. We have no details of him pursuing them at this time under the special guidance of a shaykh, although we have already seen him working under such influence during his earlier life. Thus, in his case, if there was any hypnotism involved, it must have been self-induced. It is significant that the course of preparation through which he went as a young man, under

There can be no question about the reality of the change in him. His own modesty regarding the rank of sainthood which he had reached is significant. Thus, the following story has great psychological probability, as it evidently describes his effort to free his mind from the burden of all his legal and theological studies, and to present it as a *tabula rasa* to new impressions. A later mystic tells it:

> Al-Ghazzālī used to say, “When I wished to plunge into following the people <note>the Sūfīs</note> and to drink of their drink, I looked at my soul, and I saw how much it was veiled”—at this time he had no shaykh—“so I retired into solitude and occupied myself with religious exercises for forty days, and I was granted knowledge that I had not possessed before, purer and finer than what I had known. Then I looked upon it, and behold, it contained a legal element. So I returned to solitude and occupied myself with religious exercises for forty days, and I was granted other knowledge, purer and finer than what had come to me at first, and I rejoiced in it. Then I looked upon it, and behold, it contained a speculative element. So I returned to solitude a third time for forty days, and I was granted other knowledge; it was finer and purer. Then I looked at it, and behold, it contained an element mixed with a knowledge that is known <note>that is, not simply perceived or felt</note>, and I did not reach the level of the people of the inward sciences. So I knew that writing on a surface from which something has been erased is not like writing on a surface in its original purity and cleanness, and I never separated myself from

<summary>Al-Ghazzālī recounts his attempts to achieve pure mystical insight by undergoing three forty-day periods of solitude to purge his mind of his previous legal and speculative training. He ultimately realizes that a mind once filled with intellectual studies cannot reach the same state of purity as a mind that was always clean.</summary>

And here, finally, is a remark by an intimate friend which shows how much he had changed from the arrogant, self-confident scholar of his earlier life:

> No matter how much opposition, attack, and slander he encountered, it made no impression on him, and he did not trouble himself to answer his critics. I visited him many times, and it was no mere guess of mine that he—despite what I had seen in him in the past of malice and rudeness toward people, and how he looked down on them, being led astray by the gifts of eloquence in word, thought, and expression God had granted him, and by his pursuit of rank and status—had become the very opposite and was purified from these stains. And I used to think that he was merely wrapping himself in a cloak of pretense, but I realized after investigation that the truth was the opposite of what I had thought, and that the man had recovered after having been insane."²

<header>
LECTURE VII
SAINTS AND THE ASCETIC-ECSTATIC LIFE IN ISLĀM.—Continued
</header>

Our next example of the mystical life takes us to India under the Mogul emperors, during the reigns of Akbar, Jahāngīr, Shāh-Jahān, and Aurangzīb. Living through these reigns, there resided at Balkh, Cashmere, and Lahore a saint of national celebrity whose life, sayings, and actions have been made public by von Kremer¹ based on the absolutely firsthand evidence of a book written by an immediate disciple. This disciple lived a curiously mixed life, partly as a Ṣūfī student and partly as a high-ranking official at the Mogul court. The saint was Mollā-Shāh, who was born in Badakhshān in <term>A. D.</term> 1584; was educated at Balkh and Lahore; and was received into the Qādirite order of darwīshes. He became a pantheistic Ṣūfī but performed the external rites of Islām in order not to offend the people. He developed great personal magnetism, through which he surrounded himself with many devoted disciples; he survived several attacks for heresy and managed to conciliate even the puritanical Aurangzīb; and he died in the odor of sanctity at Lahore in 1661. His disciple and biographer was Tawakkul Beg, whom he initiated into the mystical life as a young man and led to the

The interest in the account lies in the straightforward hypnotism employed and in the comparatively minor importance of devotional and ascetic exercises. It is true that Mollā-Shāh himself, according to this account, had reached his own unveiling through such exercises and without the hypnotic help of a teacher. But for his own pupils, he had discovered a simpler and shorter course, in which he used his will and personality to open—as the phrase goes—the knot of their hearts. That method is described by Tawakkul Beg as applied to himself and some others. His absolute faith in his master and evident devotion to his memory make his narrative a very trustworthy document.

For a long time he found it difficult to persuade Mollā-Shāh to work his method upon him. The master said that he recognized that the young man had a true vocation and that he was naturally saddened by seeing so many of his fellows admitted to the spiritual life. But if he were initiated, he would certainly abandon the public life for which his father, who had no idea of mysticism, intended him. What answer could Mollā-Shāh give if he made this old soldier’s son a <term>darwīsh</term>? Finally, Tawakkul Beg cut the knot when his father left Kashmīr and sought to take his son with him. He ran away, returned to

->Unhappy am I that without You, so much of my life has passed.<-

When I presented this poetic inspiration to Mollā-Shāh, he rejoiced that the idea of union with God had at last been revealed to my heart; and, addressing his friends, he said, “Tawakkul Beg has heard the words of the doctrine of the union with God from my mouth, and he will never betray its secret. His inner vision has been opened; the sphere of colors and of images has been shown to him; and thereafter the sphere where all color is erased has been revealed to him. Whoever has obtained the absolute reality, after having passed through all these phases of the union with God, does not allow himself to be led astray again, either by his own doubts or by those which skeptics can suggest.”

It goes without saying how different this is from al-Ghazzālī’s experience. There is no ethical element here; there is nothing but the stimulation of emotional religiosity with no suggestion for conduct, at least as far as this narrative goes. The goal is to reach a certain assurance of the existence of the Unseen and a direct knowledge of the relationship of the individual to that Unseen. If it were not for the fact that the process is purely one of emotion and intuition, we might say that what is provided here is a metaphysical basis for life. Perhaps Dr. Maxwell’s “metapsychical” would again be the better term.

But, further, this is reached by very evident “suggestion” in the hypnotic sense. We can see, too, that the path along which the neophyte is led

The author notes that this mystical path is defined by emotional and metapsychical intuition rather than the ethical requirements found in other Islamic traditions like those of al-Ghazzālī.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the psychological and spiritual path of the Sufi neophyte, specifically comparing different Sufi teachings regarding the stages of purification and the appearance of colored lights as indicators of progress.</meta>

<page-num>201</page-num>
<header>THE ASCETIC-ECSTATIC LIFE IN ISLĀM</header>

is a well-known one, with well-marked stages, as definite as those in the development of an old-fashioned conversion. 
The references to stages showing color and form, and to a later one where color vanishes, are interesting. In this case, their arrangement and sequence are not complicated, nor do they seem to be of a mechanical fixedness. But that was also found, and some Ṣūfī teachers held that the neophyte passed through seven stages of purification, each marked by the appearance of a different-colored light. Others rejected this entirely. They said such lights might be encountered, but it was a dangerous thing for the neophyte to pay too much attention to them. They belonged to the body and did not come from the spiritual world. They argued that the elaborate classification of these lights, and the expectation of each of them at a definite stage, was an attempt to universalize and turn into dogma what was purely subjective and personal. You will find more on this matter in Fleischer’s *Kleinere Schriften.*¹ His hope expressed then (in 1862), that others would take up the subject and study it further, has not, as far as I know, been fulfilled regarding Islām. In the discussions of recent years concerning the emotional religious life, however, such phenomena have occasionally been touched upon.²

<meta>This page continues the biography of Tawakkul Beg, detailing his transition from a darwīsh back to military service under Prince Dārā Shukōh, and the theological dangers faced by the disciples of Mollā-Shāh due to their extreme interpretations of Sufi doctrine.</meta>

To return to Tawakkul Beg. He had now been initiated as a darwīsh into the Qādirite order and might have spent the rest of his life in a cloister or as a wandering ascetic. However, no vows as binding as those in the monastic orders of the Roman Church prevent a darwīsh from returning to the world. And so, at his father’s petition addressed to Mollā-Shāh, Tawakkul Beg returned to secular life and spent ten years in state service—not in a peaceful role, as one might imagine, but as a soldier. He eventually returned to his master and spent more than a year with him; afterward, his master dismissed him again, telling him that his profession was that of a soldier and giving him a letter of introduction to Prince Dārā Shukōh, the son of Shāh Jahān. With him, he must have lived through the troubled days of the struggle for the empire during the final years of Shāh Jahān’s life, when Aurangzīb seized power and Dārā Shukōh met absolute ruin and death.

But these changes have left no reflection in the story of Mollā-Shāh’s influence as retold by von Kremer. The only danger to him seems to have come from the occasional careless statements of his disciples. These followers sometimes too openly proclaimed the doctrine of absolute unity and were naturally accused of heresy, or they applied its consequences so strictly that they neglected their ritual and even their moral duties. Islām has generally

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Mollā-Shāh’s influence and the religious atmosphere of the time, specifically focusing on the general tolerance for religious ecstasy in Islam and the mystical initiations of Prince Dārā Shukōh and his sister Fāṭima.</meta>

# THE ASCETIC-ECSTATIC LIFE IN ISLĀM
<page-num>203</page-num>

...shown itself strangely tolerant toward both of these deviations; and, indeed, it will permit anyone to say and do almost anything he pleases if he only shows signs of clear religious frenzy. We have already seen that formally established by Ibn Khaldūn.¹ But occasionally the Muslim conscience becomes aroused, or there is a scrupulous ruler who takes his position seriously, and then there is danger for free-thinkers and those who live without restraint. Once, at least, Mollā-Shāh was saved from imminent danger by the friendship of Prince Dārā Shukōh, and on another occasion he had to temporize with Aurangzīb.

Descriptions are given by Tawakkul Beg of two other such hypnotic initiations, but they add little to Tawakkul’s own narrative. One was that of Dārā Shukōh himself, who had the greatest difficulty in persuading Mollā-Shāh to perform the spiritual operation on him. Another was that of Fāṭima, a sister of Dārā Shukōh, who had a long correspondence with the master; she was initiated by her brother acting on the master's behalf, passed through all the normal visions, and attained pure union with God and intuitive perception. Mollā-Shāh said of her, “She has attained such an extraordinary development of mystical knowledge that she is worthy of being my representative.”

She describes some of her experiences in this way:

> I then seated myself in a corner with my face turned toward Mecca and concentrated my entire mind on the image

> as I have always ardently desired. Whoever does not possess the knowledge of the absolute Being is not a man—he belongs to those of whom it is said, ‘They are like the beasts, and even more ignorant.’ <note>Qur'an 7:178.</note> Every man who has attained this supreme happiness becomes, through this very fact, the most accomplished and the most noble of beings, and his individual existence is lost in the absolute existence; he becomes like a drop in the ocean, a mote in the sunshine, an atom compared to the whole. Having arrived in this state, he is above death, future punishment, the Garden, and the Fire. Whether he is man or woman, he is always the most perfect human being. This is a favor from God, who dispenses it to whomever seems good to Him. The poet ‘Attār said of Rābi‘a, ‘This is not a woman but a man, absorbed as she is by the love of God.’ ”

You will now, I think, understand how there are women saints in Islām. It is only in the ecstatic religious life that the difference between the man and the woman falls away; both are simply human beings before their God. In that presence, they are equal by virtue of their common human nature, even though a woman can inherit only half of what a man can and can never divorce her husband. Even the distinction in Roman Christendom that I pointed out above—that a woman cannot be a priest—does not exist for Islām. The relationship between the princess Fāṭima and Mollā-Shāh was only that of pupil and teacher.

It would be easy to continue almost indefinitely with concrete examples of the mystical life—or, because it is the same thing, the devotional attitude in Islām.

But I will come down now to modern times and to a European observer. Edward William Lane, during his long residence in Cairo, entered as fully as any non-Muslim has ever done into the life and ideas of the people. In one of the notes to his *Arabian Nights*,¹ he gives the following account of a Cairo friend, and it will serve as an example of another type of the Muslim mystic: the wandering ascetic.

> One of my friends in Cairo, Abu-l-Kásim of Geelán, mentioned in a former note, told me a long story of the mortifications and other methods which he employed to attain the rank of a <term>welee</term>. These were chiefly self-denial and a perfect reliance upon Providence. He left his home in a state of voluntary poverty and complete nudity to travel through Persia and the surrounding countries, and even more distant regions if necessary, in search of a spiritual guide. For many days he avoided human settlements, fasting from daybreak until sunset, and then eating nothing but a little grass or a few leaves or wild fruits, until by degrees he accustomed himself to almost total abstinence from every kind of nourishment. His feet, at first blistered and cut by hard stones, soon became calloused; and in proportion to his reduction of food, his body—contrary to the common course of nature—became (according to his own account) more stout and robust. Bronzed by the sun, and with his black hair hanging over his shoulders (for he had given up the use of the razor), he presented, in his nudity, a wild and frightening appearance. On first approaching a town, he was surrounded and pelted by a crowd of boys; he therefore retreated and, following the example of our first parents, made

In passing over a parched and desolate area, where for three days he had found nothing to eat—not even a blade of grass—nor a spring from which to refresh his tongue, he became overpowered with thirst and prayed that God would send him a messenger with a pitcher of water. “But,” he said, “let the water be in a green Baghdádee pitcher, so that I may know it is from You, and not from the devil; and when I ask the bearer for a drink, let him pour it over my head, so that I may not too much gratify my carnal desire.” “I looked behind me,” he continued, “and saw a man bearing a green Baghdádee pitcher of water, and said to him, ‘Give me a drink;’ and he came up to me and poured the contents over my head, and departed! But the burning thirst returned shortly after, and he felt himself sinking under it, when he beheld before him a high hill with a rivulet running by its base. He determined to ascend to the summit of this hill by way of mortification before he would taste the water, and he reached this point with much difficulty at the close of the day. Today <note>November 6, 1834</note>, as I was sitting in the shop of the Báshà's booksellers, a reputed saint, whom I have often seen here, came and sat beside me and began, in a series of abrupt sentences, to relate various matters concerning me—past, present, and to come. He is a poor man, supported by alms; tall, thin, and very dark, about thirty years of age, and currently wears nothing but a blue shirt, a girdle, and a padded red cap. I must now draw toward a close on the saintly life. Let me, then, return to the thesis with which I started and sum up our results. These, of necessity, are very fragmentary and incomplete, and can be regarded only as opening a perspective and suggesting its possibilities. Consider that down that perspective all the religious life of all the Muslim peoples has flowed for thirteen centuries, and you will realize how ridiculously inadequate a few lectures must necessarily be. I believe that, broadly, I have touched on the true key themes and, in the details, have given only what is generally characteristic; but I cannot say more. "Ridiculous inadequacy," I repeat, is the only expression.

But to the results. The reality of the background of the Unseen for the Muslim is now before you—that unknown spiritual order from which his life has come, which it constantly touches, and to which

<meta>This page continues the transition from the discussion of individual saintly lives to a broader consideration of the Muslim's relationship with the "Unseen" world and whether it is perceived as an ordered, law-abiding reality.</meta>
