it will return. I have spoken of that background until now simply as the Unseen, and have not, so far, considered it as an "unseen order," as Mr. William James expresses it. So we must now ask to what extent the Muslim regarded, and regards, that world beyond the pale as an ordered world—a world subject to laws at which he may dimly grasp and a world dependable in its actions and reactions. The Muslim, it is true, takes life bit by bit, but can he be sure that he can take hold of the right bit and in the right way, with regard to this unseen background? He may not have any very definite sense of law; but can he be sure that all his ethical conceptions will not be upset tomorrow by some voice out of the darkness?

This question tends to be much more a matter of dogmatic theology than of the religious psychology with which we are now engaged. Yet we must make some attempt at it. First, then, the idea that behind the curtain there is at work an absolute, inflexible law—the same yesterday, today, and forever—must, for the enormous mass of Islām, be set aside. Only the few scattered and steadily dwindling philosophers ever held such a view. Their basis was the Aristotelian conception of law, infused, it is true, with the warmer fervor of the ecstasies of Plotinus, but still law. Personalities—the Active Intellect and the Spirits of the Spheres—were in that law, and indeed were that law; but dominant over and through them was

<summary>The author examines whether the Islamic world perceives the spiritual "Unseen" as a law-bound, predictable order or an unpredictable realm. He notes that while some philosophers attempted to apply Aristotelian concepts of law to this world, the majority of Islamic thought rejects the idea of absolute, inflexible law in favor of divine will.</summary>
<keywords>Unseen, William James, religious psychology, dogmatic theology, Islām, Aristotelian law, Plotinus, Active Intellect, Spirits of the Spheres</keywords>

--- Page 237 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of how different groups within the Muslim world perceive the order of the universe, contrasting the philosophical view of natural law with the theological view of God's constant, direct will.</meta>

<page-num>213</page-num>
<header>THE ASCETIC-ECSTATIC LIFE IN ISLĀM</header>

the conception of the universe as a great mechanical animal governed by its own law.

Yet such philosophers and their disciples were a dwindling group, and now they have nearly disappeared from the Muslim world. At the opposite extreme from them is the traditional Muslim position—which is still the largely unconscious view of the masses—that Allāh is over everything, and everything depends on His personal will. An extreme school of scholastics has refined this into a theory of the universe in which the universe, both as a whole and in every detail, is constantly being recreated by Allāh from moment to moment, as He wills at each instant. It is will, and not law, that lies behind our world. From this perspective, no ethical theory of science is possible. All that can be sought is the will of Allāh regarding a matter, as it is revealed supernaturally.

Yet it is admitted that there is a certain unity in that revealed will, which corresponds to the unity that must exist in the mind of Allāh. We can be fairly certain that our ethical imperatives will remain; they will remain because they are imperatives—commands originating from the will of Allāh. Between these two dogmatic and rationalistic extremes lie various mystical conceptions. One who has seen God knows Him, trusts Him, and depends on Him; and that is sufficient. He does not reason about law or personality; these concepts have merged within the vision of the inner life. For the majority of Muslims, then, order in the unseen world means either the accepted will

He also notes a middle ground in mysticism, where the visionary finds order through a direct, personal connection with God.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the transition from discussing the nature of the universe to the practical means by which an individual can know God's will and personality within the Islamic tradition.</meta>

of God or the assurance of His personality. In one way or another, it is for man to learn that will and to put himself under the control of that personality. In harmonious adjustment to that lies man's supreme good; his chief end is to glorify God and enjoy Him forever.

The pathways to that knowledge and interaction are now before you. The distrust of reason, except as a teacher to bring the soul to the Prophet or those like him; the frank agnosticism, as far as reason is concerned; the equally frank supernaturalism and dependence on extra-rational guides; all these are plain. The prophet, the soothsayer, the dreamer, the wizard, the familiar spirit, and the saint all have their place in the system. Prophecy, dreaming, and sainthood are the lawful and accepted means by which man may know God. Prophecy is historical. The prophets are all gone, and none will now come until Armageddon and the millennium draw near. The guidance of dreams is open to every man. So, too, are the varied ranks of sainthood, from the humblest <term>darwīsh</term> to the <term>Axis</term> himself, the saintly vicegerent of God on earth.

Further, the path of the mystic is the path of the religious life, and we must now turn to that in detail. The preceding narratives must have already suggested its course and methods to you. One inevitable question, however, I must address here, however briefly. The types of the religious life

<meta>This page continues the transition from the previous page, completing the sentence regarding the various types of religious life and how they compare to those in the Islamic tradition.</meta>

<page-num>215</page-num>

# THE ASCETIC-ECSTATIC LIFE IN ISLĀM

among us are numerous, and among them the ascetic, emotional, and ecstatic play a comparatively small part. Does all the religious life of Islām move in those paths, or are there others which should fairly be taken into account? The reply does not need to be long. It is true that there are some minor and opposing drifts, but they may fairly be disregarded. First, the canon lawyers still feel that salvation may be found in their studies and, like the Pharisees, they emphasize the importance of a scrupulous observance of the ritual law. But while the masses respect them, the people can hardly be expected to find satisfaction for their religious cravings in legal subtleties. Secondly, the puritan element of the Wahhābites has always denounced mystical attitudes and the reverence for saints, and has sought to lead Islām back to the supposedly simple monotheism of Muḥammad. But their power is fast waning and has lost the reforming energy which it at first showed. Thirdly, there may be some small remains of philosophical speculation concealing itself behind *Ṣūfīism*; but that does not affect the masses. In practice, the concept of the mystical, saintly life and the organization of *darwīsh* fraternities cover all Islām and are the stimulants and vehicles of Muslim piety. The religious institutions tend to foster this. Above all comes the pilgrimage to Mecca and the many imitation pilgrimages all over the Muslim lands to the tombs of celebrated saints. These are the

It concludes that these mystical paths and saint-focused rituals are the primary drivers of Muslim piety.</summary>

> While I was admiring the unpretentious grace of the holy shrine and meditating from its threshold on the golden age of *Islām*, my guide interrupted my thoughts, saying, “You are allowed to make two prostrations at the base of any one of the pillars. Let me advise you, for the welfare of your immortal soul, to choose the one facing the Black Stone outside, which is the most sacred spot under the canopy of heaven.” The difficulty was to force my way there. The whole house was packed with pilgrims. Some were praying, some were weeping, others were groaning or beating their chests, and all—except the Bedouins—were clad in their sacred garments. A great awe fell on me. It was as though the graves had yielded up their dead at the blast of Israfil’s trumpet. All eyes were blind, all ears deaf. The thought of home, of country, of wife, and child seemed drowned in a sea of passionate devotion to the creator of those human blessings. And from outside, in the Haram <note>The sacred sanctuary surrounding the Kaaba.</note>, there arose the chant of the *Talbiyah* <note>A devotional prayer repeated by pilgrims.</note>, which every pilgrim must sing on sighting Mecca, on putting on the *Ihram* <note>The sacred clothing worn by pilgrims.</note>, on entering the Haram, on starting for the Valley of Desire and the Mountain of Compassion, and on performing the little pilgrimage of *'Umrah*. If I did not actually shed the casing of the flesh, I came to realize in a flash that the soul is immortal. These introspective thoughts were not mine at the moment of the transformation. They were retrospective, forced on me when, upon returning to a sense of my surroundings, I found myself kneeling at the Door of Repentance and heard myself crying, “*Labbaik, la Sherika lak Labbaik.*” Yes; there I was—“an agnostic who would like to know”—rubbing my brow on the marble floor of the <term>Kaᶜbah</term>, without the slightest idea in my mind as to how I came to be there. Only a month before, I had been sipping lemon squash in a London restaurant. Strange. The first thing I did was to look around in search of my guide, as skeptical a rascal as ever lived. He was on his knees at my side, his eyes bulging from their sockets. I put my hand on his shoulder. “Come,” I said, “let us go out. I am suffocating.” He rose to his feet, looking scared and humbled; but his face assumed its usual expression of cheerful mirth upon reaching the Harem. This last piece of comment, of course, shows signs of being manufactured; but the emotional outburst is evidently genuine, and gives a true picture of how the pilgrimage ceremony, and especially the ancient sanctity of the <term>Kaᶜbah</term>, affects the pilgrim. And the same scenes are being repeated at saintly shrines throughout the Muslim world.

It is, then, as I have already suggested several times, with Roman rather than Protestant Christendom that Islām must be compared regarding its emotional life. There the likeness is singularly close, reaching down even to the <term>Ghazzālian</term> combination of philosophical agnosticism and supernatural faith. And for this theological likeness there is good ground. Almost certainly, Thomas Aquinas was deeply influ-

If you consider, then, how in the Roman communion the religious life almost necessarily connects itself in some way or degree with an order, and finds its support in a mystical attitude toward the universe, the overwhelming preponderance of such institutions and attitudes in Islām will not appear so strange. This parallel might be worked out in detail; but that, like so much else, I must now leave with this mere touch.

## LECTURE VIII

### THE DISCIPLINE OF THE TRAVELER ON HIS WAY TO THE UNSEEN AND THE NATURE, WORKING, AND USE OF THE HEART

I now wish to present to you a sketch of the theory and, to some extent, the practice of the discipline of the traveler on his way toward direct knowledge of the divine and during his life within it. We have already encountered narratives telling the stories of such journeys as made by individuals. What we currently lack is a broad and philosophically generalized description. For that, I turn once again to al-Ghazzālī.

<insert>The first half of his great work, *The Revivifying of the Sciences of Religion*, is devoted—as he tells us himself <note>1. *Iḥyā*, edition with commentary by Sayyid Murtaḍā, Vol. VII, pp. 201 ff.</note>—to a consideration of what is expressed externally in the traveler through acts of devotion and religious custom.</insert> Under these, he includes creeds and their foundations, religious ritual, and the religious manner of conducting the ordinary operations of life in the broadest sense. The second half deals with the internal and hidden side of life—the heart and its workings, both good and evil. Within it, he considers the lusts and passions, as well as the virtues and perfections. To this second half, he then prefixes an introduction dealing with the wondrous qualities

But our interest lies with the general subject, and I shall therefore now present to you <insert>al-Ghazzālī’s doctrine of the heart.</insert> I translate the Arabic *qalb* literally in this way, but it is worth noting that there is an essential difference in the ideas associated with the derivative uses of *qalb* and "heart." With the English "heart," when used in this way, there is always, I think, the concept of emotions, affections, desires, and sentiments; the emotional nature in general is fundamental, and the intellectual has a comparatively small part. That cannot be said of the Arabic *qalb*. <insert>It is much more the seat of the mind, and it resembles the English "heart" only in that it suggests the most inward, secret, and genuine thoughts—the very basis of a person's intellectual nature.</insert> <insert>In the words of a Muslim commentator, it is a "transcendental (or theological) subtlety" (*laṭīfa rabbānīya*);</insert> that is, a refined, non-material thing connected with the unseen world. We must, therefore, dismiss the idea that "heart," used in this sense, implies a blind reaching out of the affections toward the divine and a submergence of the intellectual powers; it is rather the bringing to bear of another, more trustworthy, and absolute organ of the mind. However, al-Ghazzālī himself will now make that clear.

He opens his statement with an ascription of praise to Allāh involving a declaration that man, physically and mentally, is confused and perturbed if he tries to reach an absolute understanding of God, while God himself absolutely apprehends and comprehends everything. <insert>What man understands of God is that he cannot absolutely understand him.</insert> So Paul (*Phil. 3:12*) felt that he had been laid hold of rather than having laid hold of things himself. But man has a glory and excellence which distinguishes him from all other created beings and equips him for that knowledge of God which in this world is his beauty and perfection, and in the world to come is his equipment and provision. <insert>This is his "heart" in the sense mentioned above.</insert> It knows God, draws near to God, works for God, and labors toward God. It reveals what is with God; it is accepted by God when free from anything but him, and is curtained off from God when immersed in anything but him. It is sought, addressed, rebuked, and punished. It is happy when near God and prospers when a person has purified it, and it is disappointed and miserable when a person pollutes and corrupts it. At one time it is obedient to God, and then what appears externally as acts of piety comes from its illumination; and at another time it is rebellious against God, and what appears externally as corruption and rebellion is an effect from it. Whoever is ignorant of his heart is even more ignorant of everything else; and most people are ignorant in this way. For “God intervenes between a man and his heart” <note>Qur'an 8:24</note> in such a way as to prevent him from observing God, knowing His qualities, and perceiving how he is turned between two of God’s fingers; and how, at one time, he lusts after the lowest things and is cast down to the realm of devils and, at another time, is raised to the loftiest things and ascends to the world of those angels who are nearest to God. He who does not know his heart well enough to watch it, guard it, and observe what shines on it and in it from the treasures of the heavenly kingdom—he is among those of whom God has said, “They forget God, so He makes them forget themselves; they are the evildoers.” <note>Qur'an 59:19</note> Therefore, knowledge of the heart and its essential qualities is the root of religion and the foundation of the path for travelers toward it.

<image-desc>The page contains handwritten ink markings: a large bracket runs down the left margin alongside the second and third paragraphs. Several phrases are circled or underlined throughout the text, including "(or theological)", "perceives and knows.", "Al-Ghazzālī", "further on this for two reasons: (1) The question belongs to theoretical science rather than to practical,", "And (2) it is connected with the question of the spirit (rūḥ)", and "Yet the commentator remarks that this 'heart' has been called the rational soul;".</image-desc>

But first, some terms must be defined: “Heart” (*qalb*) does not mean the heart of flesh but, as we have seen, a certain transcendental (or theological) spiritual essence in some connection with the physical heart. It is the essence of a person, the part which perceives and knows. How it is connected with the physical heart is a very perplexing question. Its connection resembles the connection of accidents with substances, or qualities with the things they qualify, or the user of a tool with the tool, or things located with their location. Al-Ghazzālī does not wish to delve further into this for two reasons: (1) The question belongs to theoretical science rather than to practical science, which is his present subject. The practice of life requires consideration of the qualities of this “heart,” not of its essence. And (2) it is connected with the question of the spirit (*rūḥ*) about which the Prophet kept silence; in this, as in everything, it is appropriate for all Muslims to imitate him. Yet the commentator remarks that this “heart” has been called the rational soul; that the spirit (*rūḥ*) is its inner part and the animal soul is its vehicle.

As to the use of “spirit” (*rūḥ*), the second term, a similar ambiguity exists. On one side, it is the refined substance or vapor issuing from the hollow of the physical heart, being matured by its heat, and spreading by means of the arteries through the whole

However, in another sense, the term apparently indicates much the same knowing and perceiving subtlety as "heart" does. Al-Ghazzālī does not make a clear distinction here. God himself has told the Prophet in the Qur’ān (xvii, 87) to say, “The spirit is my Lord’s affair.”

This, however, applies only to the masses—those who cannot think except in terms of matter, or, if they have freed themselves that far, cannot clear their minds further of the concept of physical location. The full doctrine of the spirit must not be presented to such people; they would accuse the one explaining it of claiming qualities unique to the divine nature. They do not realize that the real essence and *differentia* of the divine nature is aseity—existence through itself—while all other things have only an existence borrowed from it. But for those who have realized this, the definition of spirit is simple. It exists in itself, being neither an accident, nor a material substance, nor a bounded thing. It is not located in a place or a direction; it is not joined with the human body and the world, nor separated from them; it is not within the bodies of the world (*i.e.*, the concen-

Thus, the divine goodness is the cause of the beginning of the light of being in every entity fitted to receive it. This, in short, is al-Ghazzālī’s secret; the very core of his doctrine of the nature of man.¹

That a strategy of teaching as plain as this was so openly used and admitted is one of the greatest puzzles in the history of the development of Muslim theology. “You cannot bear it,” we hear again and again, and no “now” is added. Through Muslim thought runs an intellectual elitism which cannot believe that the masses can ever be taught. And it is so complacent and self-satisfied that it does not hesitate to state itself openly. The people, it is implied, must be quite willing to accept this limitation. With al-Ghazzālī, we have simply a strategy of teaching, resulting in several stages of intellectual truth, with absolute certainty attainable in the mystical revelation, which is open, more or less, to all. But when we come to Averroes, the method has hardened in his hands into the philosophical doctrine of the twofold truth. And there is the additional difference that, for al-Ghazzālī, all methods reached one truth, though with varying degrees of spirituality, while between Averroes’ philosophical

<insert>The third term is the most difficult of all to translate. By a curious accident, the Arabic word which literally should mean “soul” has become the closest equivalent to our word “flesh” in the theological sense. This word is *nafs*. Etymologically, it is closely connected with the idea of “breath,” and is the same as the Hebrew *néphesh*, frequently translated in our Bible versions as “soul,” and sometimes as “breath,” “life,” or “appetite.”</insert> <insert>The last meaning is primary, for the essential idea of the word is life from the perspective of its passions and appetites—it is, in a word, the appetitive soul.</insert> Of the many meanings, then, which *nafs* can have, al-Ghazzālī says that <insert><u>two</u></insert> serve our purpose. <insert>It is used to express the idea that combines the force of anger and carnal appetite in man.</insert> The Arabic word for carnal appetite (*shahwa*) can be used in either a good or a bad sense. It is “truthful” (*ṣādiqa*) when it indicates a physical need that must be met if the body is to be healthy, and “lying” (*kādhiba*) when that is not the case. It is used regarding the desire for food, etc., and for sexual appetite. This usage is the prevailing one among Ṣūfīs, for by the *nafs* they mean that which combines man's blameworthy qualities, and they say that man must fight against the *nafs* and break it. The Prophet used it in this way when he said, “Thy *nafs*,"

Here, of course, we have exactly our idea of "the flesh." And ascetics, as Sufis generally are, would naturally describe all physical appetites as movements of the flesh, and regard their suppression to the limit of possibility as a religious duty.

The second usage is to indicate that same subtlety <note>A hand-drawn ink bracket on the right margin encloses the following text: "which has already been mentioned, and which is the truth, soul, and essence of a person."</note> which has already been mentioned, and which is the truth, soul, and essence of a man. It is indicated when a man says, "I," by which he means a spiritual, abiding substance. But this "soul"—if we can call it that—can be described in different ways according to how its states differ.

When it is submissive to the command of God and undisturbed by contending desires, it is called "the soul at rest" (*an-nafs al-muṭma'inna*). <note>A hand-drawn ink bracket on the right margin encloses the following text: "So God addresses it in the Qur'an (xxxix, 27),"</note> So God addresses it in the Qur'an (xxxix, 27), "O you soul at rest, return to your Lord, well pleased and accepted!" This, of course, can never be said of the *nafs* of the first usage. Its return to God is inconceivable, for it belongs to the host of the devil. But, secondly, when the soul's state of rest is incomplete and it is still struggling with the lustful soul, it is called "the upbraiding soul" (*an-nafs al-lawwāma*); of it God speaks in the Qur'an (lxxv, 2), "And no! I swear by the soul that upbraids." But, thirdly, if the soul ceases to oppose the enticements of the desires and the summons of the devil, and yields itself to them, it is called "the

It is completely blameworthy, while the soul of the second usage is praiseworthy as the very essence of a person who knows God and all things that can be known.

<margin>]</margin>The fourth term is $^c$aql, by which Arabic writers on philosophy have generally translated the Greek νοῦς.<margin>[</margin> For the present purpose, it may be rendered <insert>“intelligence,”</insert> and it has two usages. In the first instance, it means knowledge of the true nature of things and is a term for the knowledge whose seat is the heart. And in the second instance, it is **that which** perceives knowledge—the heart itself—namely, that subtle spiritual substance of which we have spoken. <insert>—</insert>Thus, a tradition states, “The first thing that God created was Intelligence (*al-^caql*).” $^c$Aql, then, means either the quality of intelligence in one who perceives, or the perceiving mind itself in which that quality resides.

These, then, are four terms, and behind each lie two ideas. Behind *qalb* there is the physical heart; behind *rūḥ* is the physical vapor which issues from the physical heart; behind *nafs* there is the sensual being, the "flesh"; behind *ʻaql* there is knowledge. But there is also a fifth idea—that knowing and perceiving subtlety in man which lies behind all four, and to which the four terms apply in common. It is specifically called the heart because its primary connection is with the physical heart, although it rules and uses the entire body. Its seat is there, just as the seat of God is on His throne in heaven while He rules the universe.

But having established these terms, the next point is the equipment and working of this "heart." <insert>God has said,</insert>¹ "And who knows the armies of your Lord except Himself?" To God belong, in hearts and spirits and in all the worlds, serried armies whose nature and number no one knows except Him; the world is full of armies in conflict, but all are His. So in the human heart there are armies, and some are relevant to our present purpose. Of these, there are two: an army that can be seen with physical eyes, and one that only the eyes of the spirit can see. <insert>The heart is lord of both, and they are its servants.</insert> The visible ones are such as the hand, the foot, the eye—all the organs of the body, internal and external. <insert>They are fashioned for obedience to the heart and cannot disobey it,</insert> just as the angels are related to God, with the one

difference being that the angels know their own obedience, and the eyelid, for example, has no knowledge of itself when it opens or shuts. Thus we are to understand that all things in earth and heaven, material and spiritual, belong alike to the armies of God. And the heart has need of these armies as a vehicle and as provisions on the journey for which it was created—namely, the journey to God. "I did not create mankind and Jinn," says Allah, "except to serve me."¹ That is the end goal; the vehicle is the body; the provision is knowledge; and knowledge is not attained and stored except through sound action. <note>A handwritten bracket is drawn in the left margin next to the following two lines.</note>Thus the creature cannot reach God except by inhabiting the body; it is a necessary stage on the journey, a seed-field for the world to come. Therefore the world is called *ad-Dunyā*, the nearer one, because it is the first of those stages. "Man is a wanderer from his birth," the commentator quotes from 'Alī; and from an unnamed poet, "He who is in this world, though confined to a city, is a traveler, and his journey continues even though he does not know it."

<note>A large handwritten bracket is drawn in the left margin next to the final paragraph.</note>The body, then, is the vehicle, or boat, by which man reaches this world. So it is his duty to care for it by bringing to it what it needs by way of food, etc., and by warding off from it what may hurt it or destroy it. The heart, therefore, needs two armies to provide food for the body—an internal army, the appetite, and an external army, the organs of the body.

The necessary appetites are created in the heart; and the necessary organs, the instruments of the appetites, are created in the body. Similarly, for driving away destructive things, there is anger in the heart and its instruments in the body. Furthermore, if food were not known, what use would the appetite for food be? The heart has need, therefore, of two other armies—one internal, the perceptions of hearing, seeing, smelling, etc., and one external, the eye, the ear, the nose, etc. This could be developed at great length, as al-Ghazzālī does in his *Book of Thanksgiving*. But, in short, the armies of the heart can be divided into three classes: a class which stimulates either the obtaining of that which is necessary, or the repelling of that which is hurtful, and which is often called the will; a class which moves the limbs, scattered equally through them, often called power; and thirdly, a class which perceives and recognizes, being situated in the individual organs of sense, and which is often called knowledge and perception. <insert>⌈</insert>Corresponding to each of these is an external army—organs of flesh and blood adapted to be their instruments. But this last class, which belongs to the physical, sensory world, is not our subject now.
Furthermore, this third class divides, on one hand, into the five senses inhabiting the five external organs of sense and on the other, into five internal senses which inhabit the cavities of the brain.<insert>⌋</insert> <insert>⌈</insert>Thus, if a man closes his eyes after seeing something, he perceives

Al-Ghazzālī goes on to expand and apply this psychological scheme in a series of allegories. The armies of anger and fleshly appetite are sometimes completely submissive to the heart, and that submission aids it on its way to eternal happiness; and sometimes they revolt and even overcome and subdue it, and thus destroy the heart and its success. <note>A handwritten ink bracket surrounds the start of the following sentence.</note> The heart also has yet another army—knowledge, wisdom, and reflection—the host of God—and this army assists it against the other two armies which sometimes join the hosts of the devil. The slave, especially, can be subdued by subjecting him to the warnings and rule of the police. But each in turn can be played off against the other. So, exactly, the fleshly appetites and the power of indignation can be used to subject one another.

But the body of man can also be likened to a city, and the intellect to its king. The senses, internal and external, are the people. Against it wars the "soul commanding to evil" (the fleshly lusts and anger) and strives to destroy the people. We have the siege of Mansoul in Bunyan's *Holy War*. So the body is like a castle on a hostile frontier, and the soul as its keeper must always be on guard against the forces of evil. God will call it to account on the last day for the people who have been in its charge. This is the truest *jihād*, or "holy warfare," and so *Muḥammad* said, "We return from the lesser

<meta>This page continues the allegorical description of the human soul, specifically concluding the "greater jihād" and transitioning from metaphors of a kingdom and a city to a hunter, as discussed by al-Ghazzālī.</meta>

<page-num>236</page-num>

*jihād* to the greater," from contending with unbelievers to contending with ourselves.

Or man’s intellect can be likened to a horseman who has gone hunting with his fleshly appetite as his horse and his anger as his dog. If the horseman is skilled, and his horse trained and his dog broken and disciplined, his hunting will prosper. But if he is awkward and his horse restive and his dog vicious, then he can neither rule his horse nor guide his dog, and he himself is more deserving of blame than success in his hunting. And what this means in the regimen of the soul is plain.

In these three allegories the whole structure of man is reckoned with and used. Everything, from the lowest appetites, has its place and purpose. The scheme is not one of absolute asceticism, but of a balanced development of all of man’s being. Nor does this apply only to man so far. Up to this point it might apply to all animals, for all have these appetites and senses, internal and external. Even a sheep sees a wolf coming and has an inward sense of fear and flees from it. Apparently al-Ghazzālī would even assign to the lower animals some power of reflection, or of making combinations, but his commentator protests at that.

In this stage, there are all sorts of degrees, extending through the ordinary experiences of men to the direct vision of saints, and finally to the divine revelation given to prophets. To some, the ascent is slow; to others, it is fast; but there are no bounds to the knowledge concerning God. Each person knows their own level, but cannot know the essence of that which is beyond them. He may believe in prophecy and be certain that there are prophets; but unless he is a prophet, he cannot know the essence of prophethood. All this comes freely from the grace and bounty of God, who is always ready to hear and answer. If the pure yearn to meet him, he yearns even more intensely to meet them. If they advance toward him a span, he advances a cubit. There is no stinginess on his part; nothing hinders men but the darkness of their own hearts and the curtains drawn by the cares of this world.

**Man**, therefore, is between the beasts and the angels. But his true "**differentia**" is his knowledge of the essences of things. He, then, who **employs** all his members and powers by seeking aid from them to attain knowledge and well-executed labor, has become like the angels and worthy to be joined with them and called one of them. But if he turns toward the body and its appetites, then he will become as stupid as an ox, as greedy as a pig, as fawning as a dog or a cat, as malicious as a camel, as insolent as a leopard, or as shifty as a fox. This belongs apparently to the *rūḥ* of man, for, in explanation, *Qurʾān*, xvii, 87, is again quoted: “Say, ‘The spirit (*rūḥ*) is of the affair of my Lord.’” This is because he claims for himself lordship, and loves rule and superiority and distinction and exclusiveness in all things, and being single in rule and removed from servility and humility. And he longs to learn all sciences; claims for himself science and knowledge and comprehension of the true natures of things; and rejoices when thought wise and sorrows when thought ignorant. Comprehension of all truths and seeking of rule by force over all creatures describe the quality, lordship; for that man’s desire is strong.

--- Page 264 ---
<meta>This page continues al-Ghazzālī’s psychological analysis of the four elements within the human soul, specifically focusing on the "lordly" and "Satanic" qualities and how they interact with base instincts.</meta>

<header>RELIGIOUS ATTITUDE AND LIFE IN ISLĀM</header>
<page-num>240</page-num>

of the absolute sage, as al-Ghazzālī understood him—an outgrowth of the spirit of leadership, developed through the desire for knowledge.<gloss>1</gloss> But here is the other, the Satanic side. It exists in man because he possesses, in addition to physical appetites and anger, a capacity for discernment that is not found in lower animals. Thus, he becomes uniquely evil by using this capacity for rational discrimination to seek out forms of wickedness, achieving his evil ends through guile and deceit.

Beneath the skin of every man, therefore—to use al-Ghazzālī's phrase—there is a pig, a dog, a devil, and a sage. It is his task to ensure that the sage exposes the tricks of the devil and keeps the pig and the dog in subjection by playing them against each other. In this way, everything may proceed smoothly, and even the wrath of man may be made to praise God. The quality of lordship, when fulfilling its highest purpose, will transform into wisdom, knowledge, and insight, and have a true claim to precedence. The pig quality will transform into chastity, patience, temperance, gentleness, and reverence; the dog quality into courage, generosity, mercy, and dignity. But more often than not, this does not happen. The strange thing is how men will criticize those who worship idols, yet if the veil were removed from them and their true inner state revealed, they would see themselves bowing down to pigs and dogs.

# THE DISCIPLINE OF THE TRAVELER

<page-num>241</page-num>

Another allegory, which describes the heart as a mirror exposed to all kinds of influences, follows in detail. But I do not need to provide it here, although there is an interesting passage describing how the remembrance (<term>dhikr</term>) of God “gives rest to the heart,”<note>Qur'an 13:28.</note> and leads to the unveiling that results in great happiness—the meeting with God Himself. As Augustine said, “Our hearts are restless until they rest in You.”

However, in his next section, when al-Ghazzālī considers the heart as the instrument of knowledge, he makes more elaborate use of the same allegory. This heart, which controls all the members of the body, is the location of knowledge regarding the essences of known things. Its relationship to them is like that of a mirror to the changing forms of the physical world. In the one case, you have the mirror, the form of the object to be reflected, and the reflection itself; in the other, you have the heart, the form of the essence of the thing perceived, and the creation and presence of the latter within the heart. “Knower” is a term for the heart that contains the likeness of these perceived essences. “Known” is a term for the likeness that is produced in the heart. But five reasons may prevent a mirror from reflecting! It may be unformed and unpolished; it may be dirty; it may be turned away from the object to be reflected; there may be a curtain over it; or there may be ignorance regarding the direction of the object to be reflected. In the same way, the mirror of the

<summary>This page explores al-Ghazzālī's allegory of the heart as a mirror that reflects the essence of things and explains how the remembrance of God brings it peace. It also identifies five specific obstacles that can prevent the heart from accurately reflecting divine and essential knowledge.</summary>

<keywords>al-Ghazzālī, heart as mirror, dhikr, divine knowledge, Augustine, essence, perception, spiritual obstacles, Islamic philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 266 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegory of the heart as a mirror, detailing the five obstacles that prevent it from reflecting divine truth.</meta>

->RELIGIOUS ATTITUDE AND LIFE IN ISLĀM<-

<page-num>242</page-num>

heart. It may be unformed and incapable, like the heart of a child. It may be soiled by sin. This is a stain that can never be perfectly removed; subsequent good deeds may take it away, but even then, without the stain, the heart would have been even brighter. Thirdly, the heart may be developed and capable; it may be pure and bright; but it might not be turned toward the truth that it should reflect because it is immersed in the details of pious actions and obedience, and in zeal for its own purification. Thus, the heart is not truly occupied with God, but with the means of reaching Him, and by these means, it is deprived of the ultimate goal. Fourthly, the heart may be veiled by some deep-seated prejudice of traditional faith. This is the case with most scholastic theologians and those who zealously uphold specific schools of thought. Even many of the pious, when they think of heavenly things, are limited by their early training and do not achieve an unveiled, direct vision. Fifthly, there may be ignorance regarding the direction in which the desired vision must be sought. No knowledge, except for that which is innate, is gained except by combining such preceding knowledge of the same nature. Ignorance, then, regarding this preceding knowledge and how to use it is a fatal but common defect. We must always combine two pieces of knowledge to gain a third. Without these five hindrances, then, the heart attains knowledge of the essences of things, for by its created nature (*fitra*) it is adapted for it. It is a divine and noble thing, differing from all other substances of the world in this uniqueness and nobility. Of it God has said,
> “We offered the trust to the heavens and the earth and the mountains, but they refused to bear it and were afraid of it; yet man bore it.”¹

Thus, the heart of man is different from the heavens and the earth, in that it can bear the burden of God’s trust—which is the knowledge of and unity with God. The heart of every human being is, in its origin, suited for and equal to that task, but is often hindered by these five causes. Muḥammad said,
> “Every child is born according to God’s plan (*‘alā-l-fiṭra*); it is only the parents who make them a Jew, a Christian, or a Magian.”

And again he said,
> “If it were not that the devils were hovering around the hearts of men, they would truly behold the heavenly kingdom (*al-malakūt*).”

So, too, he was asked, “Where is God? In the earth or the heavens?” He replied,
> “In the hearts of His believing creatures.”

# RELIGIOUS ATTITUDE AND LIFE IN ISLĀM

<page-num>244</page-num>

> "My earth cannot contain me, nor my heaven, but the tender and tranquil heart of my believing creature contains me."

In this vision, then, all things are revealed. ‘Umar said, "My heart saw my Lord, when He had raised the veil through godly fear." For whoever has the veil lifted between them and God, the forms of the world of sense and of the heavenly kingdom appear in their heart, and they see a paradise, the breadth of only a part of which is as the heavens and the earth. This is simple, for "the heavens and the earth" is only an expression for the world of the senses, and it, wide as it is, is finite. But the world of the heavenly kingdom consists of those secrets which are hidden from the sight of the eyes and are perceived by spiritual vision only. It is true that the portion of it which shines in the heart is a limited amount; but it, in itself, and in relation to the knowledge of God, has no limit. The worlds of sense and the kingdom, taken together, are called the Divine Presence, because that Presence encompasses all existing things. Nothing exists except God and His works and His realm, and His creatures are part of His works. Further, what appears of this to the heart is Paradise itself, according to some; this is the basis of the claim to Paradise by those who see it.

In this way, then, knowledge and purification of the heart and vision are bound together. "Whom

“Whomever God wills to guide,” says the *Qur’ān* (vi, 125), “He opens his heart to Islam.” And again (xxxix, 23), “Shall he, then, whose heart God has opened to Islam, and who then follows a light from his Lord . . . . ?” The beginning starts with God and His beneficence. From there come an increase of faith, light, knowledge, and an understanding of and in God—a nearness to God, who is near to everyone. This involves a proportional amount of certainty and assurance in order; of these, each person has their share, reaching the point where there is clear knowledge that nothing exists but God, and that “Everything is perishing except His face.”¹ It is, therefore, natural that there should be degrees to this revelation and faith. These are three: (1) the faith of the common people, which is a purely traditional faith; (2) the faith of scholars, which is partly deductive and not far removed from that of the common people; (3) the faith of those who know by experience and have seen for themselves in the light of certainty.

But the commentator further subdivides these based on al-Ghazzālī’s statements elsewhere. Under (1) three others are included: (*a*) Faith because of absolute trust in the narrator, such as that of children in their parents or teachers; (*b*) Faith because of accompanying circumstances which are not really decisive; (*c*) Faith because the belief appeals to and matches the nature of the believer. Under (2)

there also come three others: (a) Faith based on exhaustive and complete proof, worked out step by step in detail to the very roots; (b) Faith based on verbal, descriptive proofs, founded upon premises believed in because they are generally accepted by the greatest scholars and because there would be disgrace in rejecting them; (c) Faith based on simple rhetorical proofs, commonly used and accepted.

Finally, under (3), there are also three subdivisions: (a) The belief that everything besides God, whenever its essence is considered—*as* its essence—has no existence; rather, its existence is borrowed from something else. This borrowed existence has no subsistence in itself but in something else, and this relationship of borrowing is a pure metaphor. Whenever this truth is revealed to a creature in the light of certainty, he knows that existence is a possession belonging to its Owner, for Him alone; no one shares it with Him. (b) They rise from the level of metaphor to the peak of reality and complete their ascent and <insert>]</insert> see with their own eyes that there is nothing in existence except God, and that “Everything is perishing except His face.”¹ This does not mean that it will perish at some future time, but that it is perishing from eternity to eternity and cannot be thought of in any other way. has passed from them, and they have returned

state prevails, it is called, in relation to the person under
it, “passing away” (*fanā*), or rather, the passing
away of passing away. For the subject passes away
from himself, and passes away from his passing
away; he does not feel himself nor the lack of
feeling himself. If he felt the lack of his feeling,
he would feel himself. This state, in relation to
him who is plunged in it, is called, metaphorically,
“union” (*ittiḥād*), but its real name is “unifying”
(*tawḥīd*; that is, the perception of and belief in God’s
unity).

So much for the commentator, who follows al-Ghazzālī’s classification found elsewhere. I will now return to the simpler division. <insert>reader mark: ┌</insert> An illustration of it, says al-Ghazzālī, can be drawn from the different levels of your knowledge regarding whether a certain man is in his house. <insert>reader mark: ┌</insert> First, someone in whom you have absolute trust may tell you so; you believe it based on his authority. A similar case in religion is saving faith, though it will not guarantee a place near to God in Paradise. This, I may add, is a highly debated point in Islam. Some have even held that no faith can save unless it is based on elaborate proof.¹ But, secondly, you may hear the person’s voice in his house and may deduce his presence there from that. This is more personal and certain; however, even in this case, you might have mistaken the voice. But thirdly, when you enter his house and see him there, you know for certain he is present. This is like the religious knowledge of angels and saints; yet even this has degrees, for you may see this man with greater or less clarity depending on the light and other conditions.

# LECTURE IX

## THE MYSTERY OF MAN’S BODY AND MIND AND THE TWO SOURCES OF KNOWLEDGE

Knowledge that is not self-evident but is present in the mind at one time and not at another falls into two categories according to al-Ghazzālī’s classification. One is through study and deduction. It is called reflection and meditation, and is the method used by the *‘Ulamā*, the formal authorities on theology and law in general. The other arrives as a sudden impact upon the heart, as though something were cast (*qadhafa*) into the heart without its awareness. The individual does not know how it comes to him, or from where, or the reason why that knowledge is granted to him. It is simply a contemplation of the world of angels which he finds in his heart. The name given to this form of revelation when it is granted to saints is *ilhām*, which literally means “causing to swallow or gulp down.” It is used once in the *Qur’ān* (91:8), but whether it is meant in the primitive or the theological sense is unclear. The earliest commentators interpret it in both ways (Ṭabarī, *Tafsīr*, Vol. 30, pp. 115 ff.), and in later Muslim usage the word came to indicate, normally, the minor inspiration of *walīs*, or saints. This is significant because of the external force with which this knowledge is supposedly given. The major inspiration, that of prophets, is called *waḥy* (literally “sending” or “writing” a message)—fixed

<vocab>al-Ghazzālī, ‘Ulamā, qadhafa, ilhām, Qur’ān, Ṭabarī, Tafsīr, walīs, waḥy, revelation, inspiration</vocab>

<summary>This page outlines al-Ghazzālī’s distinction between knowledge acquired through academic study and knowledge received through sudden divine inspiration (ilhām). It explores the linguistic and theological origins of inspiration as a forceful "casting" of truth into the heart of a saint or prophet.</summary>
<keywords>al-Ghazzālī, epistemology, ilhām, waḥy, revelation, Sufism, Islamic theology, ‘Ulamā</keywords>

The commentator adds a brief statement of the types of knowledge: (1) axiomatic knowledge, or knowledge coming directly from the impact on the senses; (2) knowledge gained by the consideration of premises, whether intellectual or through the senses; (3) knowledge from the reports of others, whether by hearing or reading; (4) knowledge from inspiration (*waḥy*), either through the words of an angel who is seen, or by hearing his speech without seeing him, or by having it cast into the mind while awake or in a dream.²

The Ṣūfīs, then, turn toward knowledge gained by *ilhām* (inspiration) rather than toward that acquired through study. <insert>[...]</insert> They shun books and intellectual proofs. Their path (*ṭarīq*), instead, is to commit themselves to spiritual striving, to remove blameworthy qualities, to sever all ties, and to advance toward God with the utmost zeal. Whenever these things occur, God takes charge of the heart of His creature and illuminates it with knowledge, opening the breast of the seeker so that he accepts guidance and trusts in God. There is revealed to him the secret of the heavenly kingdom (*al-malakūt*), and the veil of error is cleared away from the surface of his heart, so that the essences of divine things shine within it. All he must do is prepare himself through simple purification, by showing zeal joined to a pure will, and by thirsting, watching, and waiting. If anyone turns to God in this way, God will turn toward him. —

In practice, the advised course is as follows: let the seeker sever all the ties of this world and empty it from his heart. <insert>[...]</insert> Let him cut away all anxiety regarding family, wealth, children, and home, as well as for knowledge, power, and ambition. Let him bring his heart to a state where the existence and non-existence of anything are the same to him. Then, let him sit alone in some corner, limiting his religious duties to what is absolutely required, and not occupying himself with reciting the *Qurʾān*, considering its meaning, or studying books of religious traditions or anything similar. And let him see

Finally, he will reach a state where the motion of his tongue will cease, and it will seem as though the word flows from it. Let him persevere in this until every trace of motion is removed from his tongue, and he finds his heart persevering in the thought. So far, everything is dependent on his will and choice; his persistence, too, in this state and his warding off the whisperings of Satan are also thus dependent; but bringing the mercy of God does not rest in his will or choice. He has now laid himself bare to the breathings of that mercy, and nothing now remains but to await what God will reveal to him, just as God has done in this manner for prophets and saints. If he follows the course above, he may be certain that the light of the Real will shine out in his heart. <note>The page contains handwritten annotations by a reader, including a line over the word "curious," an underline under "single phrase," and a bracket around "There is the case on record of."</note>

...times all is of one kind. The achievements of God's saints in this regard cannot be counted, just as the differences in their characters cannot be counted.

It seems fairly certain that a state of auto-hypnosis, with very curious consequences, could be produced by the physical and mental withdrawal described above and by the mechanical repetition of a single phrase. There is a recorded case of Tennyson who, by repeating his own name, could bring himself into a similar dreamy state, resulting in ideas that he regarded as genuine.¹ Similarly, the performance of Mr. Kipling’s “Kim” is undoubtedly a fair representation of Indian practice.² It is certainly significant that the boy trained in India and the Englishman should try to reach the Unseen by emphasizing their own personalities, while the Muslim does the same using the name of Allah. The former likely connects with the pantheism that sees the "All" as God, and the latter with the view that sees God as the "All."

We have already seen another example of this with Lane’s Persian friend (on page 208 above). He used as his formula not *Allah*, but *La ilaha illa-llah*, “There is no god but Allah.” Sayyid Murtada, al-Ghazali’s commentator, remarks here on the different values of these two phrases.

¹ James, *Varieties of Religious Experience*, pp. 383 f.; Tennyson, *Memoirs*, Vol. II, p. 473.

--- Page 282 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the phrases used in Sufi meditation, specifically examining the distinction between the simple name "Allāh" and the full creedal statement "Lā ilāha illā-llāh."</meta>

<page-num>258</page-num>
<header>RELIGIOUS ATTITUDE AND LIFE IN ISLĀM</header>

*Allāh* is the formula used by those for whom the attraction (*jadhba*) from God’s side predominates, while the longer phrase is used by those for whom their own journeying (*sulūk*) toward God comes first. In the later development of the dervish (*darwīsh*) fraternities, this distinction between *jadhba* and *sulūk* went much further. Those who preferred orderly progress under definite law were *sāliks* (“journeyers”), while those who embarked without restraint on the broad sea of their feeling of God drawing them and attracting them to Himself were *majdhūbs* (“the attracted”). One party held fast to a theological statement—half of the fundamental Muslim creed—while the other plunged into those subjective dreams of Allāh which His name alone might provoke. It should be remembered that Allāh, for Muslims, is the proper name of God like Jehovah, and not a common noun like the word God. In the end, the distinctions resulted in the now primary division between the dervishes who observe the outward rituals of the Muslim faith and those who have abandoned all such observance, although they continue to call themselves Muslims. One class is called, in the Persian phrase, *bā-sharʿ* (“with law”), and the other, *bī-sharʿ* (“without law”). Those who are *bī-sharʿ* not only reject the ritual law, considering themselves raised above such requirements, but are also, in teaching and practice, simple antinomians. This, of course, was very far from al-Ghazzālī’s

<vocab>Allāh, jadhba, sulūk, darwīsh, sāliks, majdhūbs, bā-sharʿ, bī-sharʿ, antinomians, al-Ghazzālī</vocab>

<summary>This page explores the theological and practical differences between Sufi dervishes who emphasize divine attraction (jadhba) and those who follow a disciplined path (sulūk), eventually leading to the distinction between those who follow religious law (bā-sharʿ) and those who reject it (bī-sharʿ).</summary>

teaching, nor, certainly, does his commentator have any such distinction in mind here.

The commentator now begins to discuss some further interesting details. The method (*ṭarīqa*) established here by al-Ghazzālī is traced back through two lines of teaching descent to the Prophet himself. It had also reached the commentator in a similar way from one of al-Ghazzālī’s contemporaries. However, the name by which it is known is the Naqshbandi method, after a certain much later Muhammad an-Naqshbandi, who founded the order of Naqshbandi <term>darwīshes</term> with this as their rule, and died in <term>A. H.</term> 791 (<term>A. D.</term> 1389). It was not until more than fifty years after al-Ghazzālī’s death that any still-existing <term>darwīsh</term> fraternities were founded as continuous organizations.

These authorities all agree that the essence of the method is that the seeker should remain continuously in thought in the presence of the Reality (*al-ḥaqq*, meaning Allah), without perceiving anything else and being heedless, <insert>through the existence of Allah, of being in His presence.]</insert> This can happen only through the operation of divine attraction and is most powerfully aided by companionship with a <term>shaykh</term> who is drawn in the same way. They say, also, that it can be attained either by this companionship alone, or by the remembrance (*dhikr*) of Allah, or by contemplation (*murāqaba*).

1. The remembrance of God (*dhikr*) is expressed by

<page-num>260</page-num>
<header>RELIGIOUS ATTITUDE AND LIFE IN ISLĀM</header>

the phrase *Lā ilāha illā-llāh*, “There is no God but Allāh,” and its effect is part denial and part assertion. The first phrase is denial, and with it, the independent existence of humanity is banished; the second phrase is assertion, and with it appears one of the effects produced by divine attraction. This effect differs according to one’s preparation. In the case of some, the first effect to appear is absence (<term>ghayba</term>) from everything except God; in the case of others, the result is “thankful praise” (<term>shukr</term>) and absence, and thereafter, the state of non-existence is confirmed for him, and, finally, he is honored with complete “passing away” (<term>fanā</term>). Another interpreter took <term>Qur’ān</term>, xviii, 23, “And remember your Lord when you forget,” and explained:

> “when you forget everything other than Him; then you forget yourself; then you forget your remembering within your remembering; then you forget, in Allāh’s remembering you, all of your own remembering.”

The highest and most complete of the stages is “passing away” (<term>fanā</term>), when the traveler no longer possesses awareness of anything except Allāh. The goal of this school is the beholding of Allāh—the Reality—exactly as though you saw Him; the habit of being present with Him is called “beholding” (<term>mushāhada</term>), and this occurs through the heart.

holy idea, lofty beyond limit and without equal, which is understood from the blessed name Allāh without the intermediary of any expressions, <insert>Arabic or Persian or otherwise.</insert> <insert>[</insert>After this understanding comes the holding of that idea in the imagination and facing with all one’s strength and perceptions toward the physical heart, continuing that and taking pains to cling to it until the effort is no longer needed and it becomes a habit. Then let him set this before his inner eye and, holding it fast, turn with all his strength and perceptions to the physical heart, until the inner eye is strengthened and the form disappears; and the appearance of the sought-for idea then becomes firm. Because of the greater ease and immediacy of this method, al-Ghazzālī limited himself to describing it above. It also leads directly to the miraculous powers of *walīs* as God’s representatives. Through it, too, the control of thoughts is made possible, as is—by divine gift—the power of looking into others and illuminating <insert>their inner being.]</insert> When it is a habit, the result is a continuous union with God and the receiving of His speech; this is the idea behind the words “joining” (*jamʿ*) and “receiving” (*qabūl*).

helps to bring about companionship with God, who is "the object of thought." The seeker should, as much as possible, preserve the effect he perceives resulting from his companionship with his shaykh. If there is any interruption, he returns to that companionship until the effect is restored. He does this repeatedly until that state becomes a habit. Sometimes this companionship results in such love and attraction that the image of the shaykh is held in the imagination, and there is a turning toward the physical heart with it until absence (*ghayba*) and the passing away of the self (*fanā*) occur.

To this method, one of the later shaykhs added a technique which he said had been taught and urged upon him by al-Khaḍir. This was the restraining of the breath during the course of "remembrance" and "contemplation." He made it one of the fundamentals of the method and stated that effort should be devoted to a specific restraint between breaths, so that the breath should not enter or leave without conscious attention. This became a widely followed practice and an addition that the commentator approved of. Whether al-Ghazzālī would have approved is a different matter. Some very unusual mechanical aids later gained a degree of reputation. We can now return to al-Ghazzālī himself. As might be expected, the speculative theologians objected to the general use of this method of reaching religious truth. They admitted its existence and that it was the method used by prophets, but they argued it was difficult and slow, and its requirements were hard to combine. The favorable state of insight lasted only a moment; a breath from the <insert>handwritten underline begins</insert>outside destroyed it.<insert>handwritten underline ends</insert> The heart, too, was essentially unstable and uncertain. It was easily affected by physical conditions, and, under these, pathological and destructive imaginings might arise, which might then be trusted and followed with disastrous results. These we would now call non-veridical hallucinations, and al-Ghazzālī, too, was well aware of their possibility.<insert>✓</insert> Finally, a prophet might become a <term>canon lawyer</term> by inspiration, but <insert>handwritten bracket in right margin begins</insert>for ordinary men there was no way except study; any other course was like giving up farming to go digging for treasure.

But the essential difference between the two methods—insight through the heart and academic study—must now be made clear. al-Ghazzālī explains it again by using two illustrations. <insert>handwritten box begins around the following sentence</insert>One is a truly startling anticipation of Wordsworth’s “eternal deep, haunted forever by the eternal mind,”<insert>handwritten box and marginal bracket end</insert> and of the even more recent conception of a subliminal consciousness in

It also highlights how al-Ghazzālī's ideas preceded modern concepts of the subliminal mind and Wordsworth's poetry.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of al-Ghazzālī’s spiritual philosophy, moving from the debates between theologians and mystics to a specific metaphor illustrating how the heart receives divine knowledge.</meta>

<page-num>264</page-num>
<header>RELIGIOUS ATTITUDE AND LIFE IN ISLĀM</header>

direct touch with the infinite. Imagine a pond into which water flows in streams from higher ground. And imagine, also, that the bed of the pond is dug up until water rises into it from the springs of the earth—water purer and more abundant than what the streams provide. Then, if the streams are blocked, the water will still rise in the pond, and it will do so even more spontaneously and steadily. This pond is the heart; the water that pours in from the streams is knowledge coming by way of the five senses; the water that rises from the springs is the knowledge that comes directly to the heart. When the paths of the senses are closed, knowledge that is even purer and more abundant will rise from the depths of the heart.

But it may be asked, "There are veins of water in the earth that rise in this way, but from where does knowledge flow directly into the heart?" The answer comes by asking another question: How does knowledge come to the heart in any case? Al-Ghazzālī views it in this way. For everything that exists or is done in the world, God has written on the Preserved Tablet (*al-lawḥ al-maḥfūẓ*) something that can be compared to an architect's plan for a house. Of course, this is only using the Quranic expression to say that all earthly things and actions exist, and have always existed, definitely recorded in the spiritual world. According to this plan, then, the world has been created and is being managed. It has now

<meta>This page continues Al-Ghazzālī's explanation of how earthly things and actions, which are recorded on the spiritual "Preserved Tablet," manifest in the material world and are perceived by the human heart.</meta>

...become material (*<term>jismānī</term>*) and factual (*<term>ḥaqīqī</term>*). From this second stage, a third corresponding existence is derived within the senses and the imagination, or the visualizing power of the mind (*<term>al-khayāl</term>*). From there, finally, comes a fourth—an intellectual form of existence (*<term>‘aqlī</term>*)—within the heart. Thus, things exist in four ways, and through this process, we gain our ordinary knowledge of the outside world. But is it truly so ordinary? How can the eye take in the unbounded universe, or the mind know what is outside itself, or retain knowledge of what has passed away? It can only be through an act of grace from divine wisdom that these forms of existence can occur in the senses, the imagination, and the heart. We could never perceive a thing that did not reach us, and unless there were something in our own being that corresponded to the world, we could never retain any knowledge of what has gone by. This means that the human body is a mysterious structure, linking the material and spiritual worlds. <term>Al-Ghazzālī</term>, in short, forces us back to the primary mystery of the relationship between mind and body. Man’s body is the link, yet it and its functions are, nonetheless, perpetual miracles.

If we are then asked how the heart sees, the relevant counter-question is: how do the eye, the imagination, and the mind see, retain, and know? The vision that the heart has directly of the plans on the <term>Preserved Tablet</term> is no more and no less miraculous. The

<page-num>266</page-num>

The evidence for this as a fact is the testimony of those who have attained such knowledge; it is the same in kind, though not as abundant, as the evidence for the fact of physical sight. Direct vision of the heart is a permanent quality belonging to human nature, but God has blinded the hearts of most people so that they cannot see. If they can, through suitable meditations and exercises, lift the veils, then they will see and know. Every person is potentially a seer and a saint.

The heart, then, has two doorways: one opening onto the spiritual world, and the other onto the five senses which take hold of the physical world. And one of these worlds corresponds, in a certain fashion, to the other. How the door is opened to knowledge through the senses is already clear. Certainty regarding the other doorway can be gained by considering the wonders of vision and how the heart is informed of the future or the past during sleep. That doorway is opened only to one who has devoted himself to the thought of God and keeps himself apart, through that thought, from everything else.

But, turning from these two means of access, <term>al-Ghazzālī</term> proposes another parable to explain the different methods of working used by the <term>Ṣūfīs</term> and by the speculative students of theology. The people of China and the people of Byzantium once competed before a king regarding their respective abilities in decorating. So, the king gave them a vestibule to be adorned: one side by the

<margin>This concerns the doctrine of the faith that leads to salvation. He who has more than a grain of faith will never enter the Fire; he who has a grain or less, if he has any at all, will not remain in the Fire. It is certain, from the teaching of the *Qur'ān*, that there are many ranks in Paradise just as there are divisions in knowledge, ranging from the most ignorant believer who relies on authority (*muqallad*) to the most experienced saint (*'ārif*).</margin>

But proof may still be needed that this is a legally sound method. Al-Ghazzālī now turns to such a proof. This section is, naturally, not of the same importance to us, and I provide only as much here as may be valuable for our knowledge of the spiritual life of Islām. Ibn Khaldūn’s argument on pages 165 and following, mentioned above, should be compared. Al-Ghazzālī says that no one who has had any experience, however slight, of such knowledge falling into the heart—not knowing from where it came—will ask for any other proof that he is acting within the law. As for the person who has had no such personal experience, let him consider the following proofs. Nothing seems to go beyond the devout life, seeking support and guidance from its Lord. One somewhat famous text is *Qurʾān* 18:64: “And we taught him some knowledge of our own (*min ladunnā*).” All knowledge comes from God, but transcendental knowledge (*al-ʿilm ar-rabbānī, al-ladunī*) is that which is given directly to the heart without passing through human teaching. So the true scholar (*ʿālim*) is not the one who learns a book by heart—which he may forget—but he who receives knowledge from his Lord (*min rabbihi*) at whatever time He wills, without memorization or study.

The stories of saints provided are largely about thought-transference, and very simple cases at that. Khādim, and brought me fifty dīnārs, saying, ‘Use them for your affairs.’ So I rose and went out, and there was a poor blind man sitting before a barber who was shaving his head. I went to him and offered him the dīnārs, but he said, ‘Give them to the barber.’ I said, ‘But they amount to such and such.’ He said, ‘Did we not say to you that you are a miser?’ So I offered them to the barber, but he said, ‘When this poor man sat down before me, I determined not to take anything on his account.’ “So,” finishes ash-Shiblī, “I threw the dīnārs into the Tigris and said, ‘No one honors you without God abasing him.’” Here ash-Shiblī had a flash of insight into himself, which he would not at first believe, but which was justified by his protest to the poor man; and the poor man read from the mind of ash-Shiblī his previous experience. So he comments upon himself in bitter humor that he must be so low that no one can honor him with a present without being dishonored by the throwing away of his present. His trial had shown him that no one could come to any good through him.

The second is the fact that a prophet provides information about the unseen world and future events; everyone admitted that. But a prophet is simply a person to whom the essential nature of things has been revealed, and who is dedicated to the betterment of humanity. Therefore, it is not impossible for others to exist who possess the first characteristic but have nothing to do with the second. These are the *walīs*, or saints, who have no mandate to preach but only to cultivate their own souls. This is another example of the argument from classification mentioned earlier on pages 63 and 152.

Anyone, then, who admits these two things must also admit the two gateways of the heart: an external one through the senses and an internal one opening directly into the heavenly kingdom. Knowledge must come through both—both mediated and immediate—and the truth of describing the heart as wavering between the world of the senses and the Unseen is evident. It should also be clear how significant this is. Through the heart, humans can come into direct and intimate association with God and know things that angels do not. <insert>[</insert>Angels cannot see what is in the heart, and the heart of a person with spiritual experience (*ʿārif*) can tell them things that the angels cannot. For the Muslim, humanity is higher than the angels.

# LECTURE X
## THE TEMPTATIONS OF THE HEART AND THE NATURE OF EVIL SPIRITS

We now turn to the temptations that attack the heart. The fundamental concept here is the devil's whispering (*waswās*) to the heart. Twice in the *Qur'ān*, the expression is used clearly in reference to the devil (vii, 19; xx, 118); it is used once in an uncertain way, but regarding something evil (cxiv, 4, 5), and once (L. 15) in reference to the soul (*nafs*), clearly as an incitement to evil. In Muslim theological and ethical works, the word has become the standard term for such satanic incitement.

Al-Ghazzālī divides this subject into five sections: (1) how the devil gains control over the heart through “whispering” and the general meaning of “whispering”; (2) the different ways the devil approaches the heart; (3) which “whisperings” in the heart a person is punished for and which are forgiven; (4) is it possible for “whisperings” to be completely stopped during the remembrance (*dhikr*) of God? (5) how quickly the heart turns and changes, and how hearts differ in this regard.

I. The heart is like a round building with doors open on all sides, or a target struck by arrows from all directions, or a mirror over which forms are continually passing, or a pond into which waters are

<keywords>al-Ghazzālī, waswās, whispering, Qur'ān, nafs, soul, dhikr, remembrance, heart, temptations, devil, evil spirits</keywords>

They come through the senses externally, and

an effect is produced on their heart. This is also true when
the physical appetite is moved by overeating. And
even if the senses are closed, the imagination moves

intended or willed; then the will moves the body.
Next, these ideas can be divided into those which

The first are called <term>ilhām</term> (inspiration) and the second <term>waswās</term>,

devil" is a term for a creature whose business is the opposite of that, promising evil and commanding vileness and scaring by the threat of poverty when there is concern for the good.¹ So "whispering" is the opposite of *ilhām* and the devil is set against the angel and "help" against "abandonment." As has been said in the *Qur'ān* (51:49), "Of everything we have created a pair," and all things are coupled in pairs except God himself.

So the heart is pulled about between the devil and the angel. It has two traveling companions, as Muhammad has said; for the one, God should be praised; and against the other, his aid should be sought. This, too, is the meaning of his saying that the heart of the believer is between two of God's fingers. It is a metaphorical expression for God's working upon it through the angel and the devil. By its created nature the heart is equally fitted to be affected by either. Passion (*hawā*) may be followed or opposed. In the one case, the devil settles in the heart and rules it; in the other case, the angels. But no heart is free from physical appetite and anger and desire, and therefore the devil and his whisperings always haunt it. The Prophet said, "There is not one of you who does not have a devil." They said to him, "Even you, O Messenger of God?" He replied, "Even I; only God aided me against him, and he submitted and commands only good,"

the meaning of which is that when the fleshly appetite stays within proper limits, the devil who has clothed himself in it can command only what is good. On the other hand, those whose hearts are filled with strong passion and lusts come to be ruled by the devil, who becomes their true god. The cure is to empty the heart, though more unusual prescriptions are sometimes provided. Someone complained to the Prophet that the devil came between him and his prayers. "That is a devil," said Muḥammad, "who is called Khinzib. Whenever you feel his presence, seek refuge in God from him and spit three times to your left." However, the thought of God in general is the most effective method. To eliminate the devil's whisperings, another thought must be placed in the mind. Thoughts of God and the things connected with him should therefore be used, for in them the devil can never find any room. This explains the virtue of phrases such as:

> "I seek refuge in God from the stoned devil,"

and

> "There is no strength or power except in God, the High, the Mighty."

Lusts, then, flow so through the flesh and blood of men, and the devil’s rule over them is consequently so normal, that the devil lies in wait for them at every turn. Even in their embracing of Islām, in their migration with the Prophet to al-Madīna, and in their fighting in the path of God, the devil has been able to suggest evil. He also has the skill to suggest things that are praiseworthy in themselves and yet

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how the devil subverts even good intentions, focusing on al-Ghazālī's personal experiences and his prescriptions for spiritual defense through solitude and constant vigilance.</meta>

<page-num>278</page-num>

so to develop them that they lead to destruction. A description is given, for example, of how a man can be led into preaching and, through it, be brought into spiritual pride, a love of popularity, and ambition. This is almost certainly autobiographical; al-Ghazālī preached at one time and gave it up for the sake of his own soul.<note>“Life,” *Journal of American Oriental Society*, Vol. XX, pp. 101 ff.</note> Another example is that Satan (Iblīs) appeared once to Jesus and said to him, “Say, ‘There is no God but Allāh.’ ” Jesus replied, “That is a true word, but I will not say it because you told me to.” Beneath its apparent good are hidden implications. It is the duty, then, of the human creature to watch every concern that runs through his mind and test where it comes from. There is only one way to absolutely cut off these things. Let him retire into solitude in a darkened house—this will block the senses, the outward avenues; let him strip himself of family and fortune—this will diminish the avenues of whisperings from within; let him occupy himself with the thought of God—this will keep away the imaginings which flow into the heart; let him constantly draw his heart toward God and strive on in this way—the striving will last his whole life. The gates of the devil to the heart cannot be finally locked; they must always be watched. The devil never sleeps; “Then we should be able to rest!” said one saint. Yet he can be worn out for a time, just as a traveler wears out his beast during a journey.

<summary>Al-Ghazālī describes how spiritual activities like preaching can lead to pride and explains that the only way to resist the devil's constant influence is through total solitude, renunciation of worldly ties, and lifelong devotion to the thought of God.</summary>
