<meta>This page continues the discussion of spiritual defense, moving from the need for solitude to the specific metaphors of the one true path versus the many paths of the devil, as taught by al-Ghazālī.</meta>

<page-num>279</page-num>

-># THE TEMPTATIONS OF THE HEART<-

And again, while the devil has many gates, there is only one through which the angels come. Similarly, while he has many paths to which he calls men, there is only one true path. The Prophet once drew a line upon the ground. “That is the path of God,” he said. Then he drew many lines to the right and left. “On each of these,” he said, “stands a devil calling you.” Finding his way here, a man is like a traveler on a dark night in a desert crossed by innumerable trails. Only a discerning eye and the morning light from the Book of God and the example of the Prophet can guide him.

In what has preceded concerning the relationship of man to his physical nature, a curious contradiction has prevailed—perhaps a reflection of the unresolved paradox of human nature. At one time, al-Ghazālī recognizes the necessary part which the physical appetites and emotions play in the maintenance of human life; he even includes Satan in this and finds him to be a creation of God for God’s purposes, a necessary balance to the angels. But at another time, a strictly ascetic note is struck; all sensory life must be excluded; existence must be the contemplation of God; and Satan is the Accursed One. How this develops will be seen. Al-Ghazālī’s sense of humanity rose above his theories.

2.  But a knowledge of the devil's avenues of approach is also necessary and is as absolute a duty for the individual as warding them off. These

Satan met Moses and said, “You are he whom Allah has chosen to be His Apostle and with whom He has spoken, <note>*Ibid.*, iv, 162.</note> and I am one of His creatures. I have sinned and desire to repent; so intercede for me with my Lord, that He may accept my repentance.” To this Moses agreed. Then when he had gone up into the mountain and spoken with his Lord, and was about to descend, his Lord said to him, “Deliver that which was entrusted to you.” So Moses said, “Your creature, Satan, desires to repent.” And Allah revealed to Moses, “O Moses, your request is granted; command him that he adore the grave of Adam, so that his repentance may be accepted.” Then Moses met Satan and said to him, “Your request is granted; you are commanded to adore the grave of Adam so that your repentance may be accepted.” But he was angry and proud, and said, “I did not adore him while he was living, and shall I adore him now that he is dead?” Therefore he said, “O Moses, you have a claim against me, because you interceded for me with your Lord. If you remember me, then, on three occasions, I shall not destroy you. Remember me when you are angry, for my spirit is in your heart and my eye is in your eye, and I affect you just as your blood does, and whenever a man is angry I blow into his nostrils, and he knows not what he does. And remember me when you meet an army in battle formation, for when one of mankind meets an army in battle formation I make him to remember his wife and child and

This story is regarded as a warning against anger, worldly desire, and fleshly lust. It is a curious and distinctive feature of Muslim instructional legend that the warning is so often put into the mouth of Satan himself.

> when Noah entered the ark, he took into it a pair of every kind, as Allah had commanded him. Shall I tell you of three of them or of two?” And Allah revealed to Noah, “You have no need of the three; let him tell you of the two.” So Noah said, “What are the two?” And he said, “They are the two which do not fail me; they are the two which do not let me down; by them I destroy mankind; they are envy and greed. For by envy I was accursed, and became a pelted devil.”

(*d*) Love of adornment in furniture, clothing, and home. When that begins, there is no end except death. The devil only needs to start it, and it will proceed on its own.

(*g*) Money and all kinds of wealth. That is everything beyond what is absolutely necessary. The heart of one who has only that much is at rest; with more, desires arise. This is illustrated oddly. If a man finds one hundred dīnārs on the road, ten desires spring up, and to accomplish each of them would require a hundred dīnārs. So the man is now actually in need of nine hundred dīnārs instead of being ahead by a hundred. It is told that Jesus once used a stone as a pillow. Satan passed by and said,
> “O Jesus, you have a desire for the world!”
But Jesus took the stone from under his head and threw it away, and said,
> “Take it along with the world!”
So even a stone may cause harm. A devotee who has one is tempted by it as he stands in prayer; the thought that he could use it as a pillow and lie down to sleep stays in his mind and disturbs his devotions. How much more, then, do the trappings of luxury!

(*h*) Miserliness and the fear of poverty. There is much in the *Qur’ān* concerning these, for it constantly encourages alms, the generous spending of money, and trust in God. Fear of poverty is thus almost like unbelief. It also leads one to frequent marketplaces, which are the particular homes of devils.

they should believe, be submissive, and occupy themselves with worship and earning their daily bread, leaving specialized knowledge to the <term>‘Ulamā</term>. It would be better for such a person to commit adultery or steal than to busy himself with theological knowledge; for he who does so without a sure foundation falls into unbelief without even realizing it.

(k) Evil suspicion of Muslims. It is written in the <term>Qur’ān</term> (49:12), “Avoid suspicion carefully; for some suspicion is a sin.” Through it, the devil gains a hold over a person and leads them around until they think themselves better than others. However, giving cause for suspicion should also be avoided. Even the Prophet guarded not just himself, but others, by being careful in that regard. So no one should think that they do not need to be careful in this way, no matter how high and unshakable their reputation. The wicked think poorly of everyone. Whenever a person has evil suspicions of people in general and looks for faults in them, know that he is vile within and that his vileness is merely oozing out of him.

Such are some of the avenues of approach for the devil; to list them all would be impossible.

How, then, can we guard these? Is the remembrance (*<term>dhikr</term>*) of God enough?

In answer: The treatment is to close these avenues by purifying the heart of evil qualities. This is a lengthy matter, but in short, once these qualities have been rooted out from the heart, the devil is left with only a temporary passage through it;

If you have anything to eat in your hand, you cannot drive him away; but if you have nothing, a word is enough. If there are desires in the heart, the thought of God remains outside and cannot penetrate to the interior where the devil sits. On the other hand, he can work against the saints only when they are momentarily careless.

There follow a number of formulas used in such “thoughts” of God, the repetition of which drives the devil away. Stories are also told of how he even confessed their effectiveness and tried to bribe saints not to teach them to others. They evidently possessed a magical value in themselves. A devil carrying a firebrand in his hand used to come to the Prophet himself during his prayers and disturb him. He would position himself before the Prophet and could not be driven away by any formula. So Gabriel came and taught the following, to which this devil yielded:

the heavens and ascends into them, from the temptations of the night and the day, from the events of the night and the day, except for an event that brings good, O Merciful One!” On another occasion he had a personal struggle with the devil, and the traditions about it seem to be the confused and contradictory record of an actual episode in his psychological development. He took the devil by the throat and choked him, “and I did not let him go until I had felt the cold of his tongue upon my hand. But these formulas are of value only when used by the saintly. Let no one think that by reciting them at any time—as an *opus operatum*—he can drive away the devil. That is as absurd as to imagine that a medicine will take effect when the stomach is burdened with food. So, “remembrance” (*dhikr*) is a medicine, and piety (*taqwā*) is abstinence from food; apply piety first to the heart, empty it of physical desires, and these formulas will drive the devil from you.

But all this leads naturally to a weighty question: Is this inciter to different acts of rebellion a single devil, or are there many different devils? Al-Ghazzālī

This would be clear in itself; the variety of effects points back to a variety of causes.

As for the armies, many different accounts are given of their origin. But they all spring from <term>Satan</term>, who is their father. He has five sons in particular, each with his own *specialty*: <term>Thabr</term>, <term>al-Aʿwar</term>, <term>Miswāṭ</term>, <term>Dāsim</term>, and <term>Zalanbūr</term>. <term>Thabr</term> stirs up trouble; he causes the tearing of garments and the slapping of cheeks. <term>Al-Aʿwar</term>’s department is fornication; <term>Miswāṭ</term>’s is lying; <term>Dāsim</term> makes a husband reproach his wife and be angry with her; <term>Zalanbūr</term> is in charge of markets—this is the reason for the continual wrangling and striving there. <term>Khinzib</term> interferes with prayer, and <term>Walahān</term> with ritual ablution. These are the best-known names, but other writers go into greater detail.

So, too, there are armies of angels, and each specializes in the same way. Every believer, says one authority, has a guard of one hundred and sixty; if he were left to himself for an instant, the devils would snatch him away. The relations between men, devils, and angels are described in this curious way:

After Adam had fallen to earth—the Muslim Fall was literal, from the top of the mountain of the Earthly Paradise—he said, "O my Lord! The Lord said, "A son will not be born to you without an angel being put in charge of him." But he said, "O my Lord! Give me more." And the Lord said, "I will repay an evil deed once, and a good deed tenfold, besides what I increase." But he said, "O my Lord! Give me more!" The Lord said, "The gate of repentance is open as long as the spirit is in the body." But Satan said, "O my Lord! If You do not aid me against this creature whom You have honored over me, I shall not be strong enough for him." The Lord said, "There shall not be born to him a child, but one shall be born to you also." But Satan said, "O my Lord! Give me more." The Lord said, "You shall flow in them like their blood, and take their hearts for your dwellings." But Satan said, "O my Lord! Give me more." The Lord said, "Gather against them with your cavalry and your infantry, and share with them wealth and children, and promise them—and the devil promises them nothing except deceit."¹

The Lord and Satan are on such easy terms in Islam, much as in the Book of Job. Emphasis on the absolute sovereignty of Allah naturally negates the independence of an evil power, and the sense of Allah's immediate working negates Satan's isolation. It is true he no longer appears in the court of heaven, but he says, "My Lord," and regards himself as part of the necessary system of things.²

<margin>¹ Qur'an 53.</margin>
But the question is bound to come up: How does the devil appear to some and not to others; and when anyone sees a form, is that the actual form (*ṣūra*) or only a symbol (*mithāl*)? If it is his actual form, how is he seen in different forms, or at one time in two different places and in two forms? The answer is that angels and devils have, it is true, each a form which is their real form; but it cannot be seen by the eye, and only in the light of the prophetic gift. Even the Prophet saw Gabriel only twice in his true form: once when he stood up before him, blocking the entire horizon from east to west, and again on the night of the ascent to heaven (*al-Mi'rāj*) beside the lote-tree of the extremity.¹ Apart from these occasions, he mostly saw him in a human form.

So it is with Satan. By far the most frequent case is that those who have attained the power to see him while awake see him in a form which is a symbol or likeness of his actual form. There are many tales of this. And this experience takes the place of seeing his actual form. The heart is of such a nature that a truth must necessarily appear in it which comes from the side facing the world of the heavenly kingdom. Then an effect from that flashes out on the side facing the world of sense, because the two are joined. What appears in this way is only a construction of the imagination (*mutakhayyila*), as are all things in the world of sense. In that world the

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of spiritual visions, explaining how the imagination interprets sensory data and spiritual "flashes" from the heavenly kingdom to create symbolic or true forms of angels, devils, and Jinn.</meta>

## THE TEMPTATIONS OF THE HEART

Only the outside is reached by the senses, and the imagination, taking that result, is sometimes led entirely astray; the world of sense is a world of much ambiguity. But what comes to the imagination by the flashing out of the world of the kingdom upon the secret heart is similar to and corresponds to the quality itself; for in the kingdom, the form follows the quality, and certainly a vile concept there is not seen except in a vile form.

The result, then, is that the devil is seen sometimes by way of symbolism and likeness, and sometimes—though much more rarely—in his true form. He who sees him through a symbol differs from a dreamer only in the fact of actually beholding him with the eye.

The commentator adds a very interesting note from <term>Ibn ʿArabī</term>, asserting the same thing of the <term>Jinn</term>, whom he joins with the angels under the single name "spiritual being" (<term>rūḥānī</term>). One curious further detail may be worth giving, as it agrees exactly with Irish folklore. As long as a human eye looks fixedly at a spiritual being in one of its forms, it cannot change that form but is, as it were, fettered by it. The <term>rūḥānī</term> must then use a stratagem which seems not to have occurred to the Irish <term>leprechaun</term>. He produces a form before himself like a screen and then causes this screen to move away to one side; the observer's eye follows it involuntarily, and he escapes.<note>1. *Iḥyā*, Vol. VII, p. 272; Keightley, *Fairy Mythology*, pp. 372 ff.; compare the case of an apparently veridical hallucination.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of spiritual beings, moving from the visual perception of these entities to Al-Ghazzālī's deeper philosophical definitions of their nature and essence.</meta>

But though al-Ghazzālī declined to go further on this subject for his current audience, it may be appropriate for me to introduce a statement here regarding his philosophy of spirits. I have extracted it from his *Maḍnūn*, a book containing developments of his teaching that he considered suitable only for specialist students. There he continues the subject as follows:¹

> Angels, Jinn, and devils are substances that exist in themselves, and they differ in essence in the same way that species do. An example of this is "power," for it differs from "knowledge," and "knowledge" differs from "power," and both differ from "color." Thus, "power," "knowledge," and "color" are accidents, existing within something other than themselves. Similarly, there is a difference between angels, devils, and Jinn, and yet each of these has its own nature. It is certain that there is a difference between Jinn and angels, but it is not known whether it is a difference between two species—like that between a "horse" and a "man"—or whether the difference lies in accidents—like that between a "complete man" and an "incomplete" one. The same applies to the difference between an angel and a devil. In that case—if the species is the same and the difference applies to accidents—it is like the difference between a good person and a bad person, or between a prophet and a saint. To all appearances, the difference is one of species; however, only God has knowledge of this.
>
> These beings mentioned above are indivisible. I mean that the locus of the knowledge of God is one and indivisible, for unitary knowledge is not located except in a single locus. Similarly,

the essence of man. Knowledge and ignorance of the same thing, when situated in one *locus* (place), are contradictory; however, when they are in two different *loci*, they are not contradictory. Regarding the indivisibility of this substance and the question of whether it is limited or not, the consideration of these questions refers back to our opinion on the existence of units that cannot be further divided <note>the indestructible atom</note>. If an indivisible unit is an impossibility <note>if there are no atoms</note>, then this substance is undivided and unlimited; if the existence of an indivisible unit is not impossible, then it is possible that this substance is limited. Some have said that it is not permissible for it to be either undivided or unlimited, because God is both, and what then would distinguish this substance from Him? But that does not necessarily follow, because perhaps they differ in the essence of their individuality. If their divisibility, limitedness, and spatial conditions are stripped from both of them—which is simply removing qualities belonging to human beings from the concept of God (*sulūb*)—then there remains only the consideration of the essences. This is because that which is stripped from the essences is like two accidents differing in definition and nature but located in the same *locus*; asserting their need for a *locus* and their presence in the same *locus* does not mean that they are identical to one another. Similarly, stripping away the need for a *locus* and a physical place does not mean that this removal of qualities is common to both these things in a way that makes them the same.

And it is possible that these substances—I mean the substances of angels—may be seen, even though they are non-sensible (not perceptible to the senses). This seeing is of two kinds: first, by way of taking on an appearance, as Allah said,
> "And he took for her the appearance of a well-formed man." <note>Qur'an 19:17, regarding Gabriel appearing to Mary.</note>

and as the Prophet saw Gabriel in the form of Dihya al-Kalbi. The second kind is that some angels have a sensible (physical) body, just as our souls are non-sensible but have a sensible body, which is the *locus* of their

The case is similar with the Jinn and devils.

3. The references in the *Qur’ān* and in the traditions from Muḥammad regarding the guilt of evil thoughts in the heart are contradictory to a degree. Some statements make such thoughts punishable, and others allow them to go unpunished. But al-Ghazzālī believed that he could clear up the confusion through a fourfold analysis. First, he says, there is the idea thrown into the mind; second, an inclination of one's nature toward the thing suggested; third, a decision or conviction of the heart in favor of the thing; fourth, a determination and purpose to do the thing. Then comes the actual deed which, of course, is punishable.

The first two stages are to be considered guiltless; they arise from the nature of man. As for the third, everything depends upon whether the decision is voluntary or not. The fourth is plainly culpable, but if it does not lead to action, the guilt may be erased. It may have been only a moment’s heedlessness, and in the books of the recording angels, good is recorded at ten times the value of evil. Yet if anyone dies with the intention of mortal sin, he goes to the Fire <note>Hell</note>. Deeds are judged by intentions. When Muslims fight together, both the slayer and the slain are guilty;

<meta>This page completes the fourfold analysis of the guilt of internal thoughts by Al-Ghazzālī, specifically addressing the role of intention (niya) in legal and moral culpability, and begins a discussion on whether the devil's "whisperings" can be entirely removed from the heart through the remembrance of God.</meta>

the one because he has killed his brother; the other, though dying wronged, because he had intended to kill his brother. The doctrine of intention (<term>niya</term>) in Muslim ethics plays a large part. Ignorance of an essential fact may make a person guilty or innocent, but the law itself is unaffected. If, as in the case above, a person willingly kills a fellow Muslim, he is guilty; if he kills him, believing him not to be a Muslim, he is innocent; if he kills a non-Muslim, believing him to be a Muslim, he is guilty.

4. Regarding whether the “whisperings” of the devil can be entirely cut off by the thought of God, those who have studied and know the human heart hold five different opinions. Some say flatly that they can, and base this on the saying of the Prophet, “Whenever God is remembered, the devil retreats.” This they take literally. A second group teaches that they cannot be completely destroyed. They remain in the heart but produce no effect; the heart is too occupied by the thought of God. A third group holds that their effect also remains; the “whisperings” still exist, but they are distant and weak. A fourth believes that they are destroyed when God is remembered, but only for a moment; then the “remembrance” is destroyed for a moment; and so the two follow at such close intervals that they seem to be competing, and the effect is like a row of dots around a ball; if you make it rotate rapidly

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Al-Ghazzālī's analysis of the heart, specifically examining different views on how the devil's "whisperings" interact with the remembrance of God and how faith can dispel specific temptations.</meta>

<page-num>296</page-num>
<header>RELIGIOUS ATTITUDE AND LIFE IN ISLĀM</header>

they become a circle. So the withdrawal of the devil really takes place, but we see his “whisperings” along with our “remembrance.” A fifth group holds that the “whisperings” and “remembrance” compete continuously to remain in the heart, just as a person can see two things at one time.

But the fact, in al-Ghazzālī’s opinion, is that all these views are sound; it is only that each does not cover all the phases of “whispering.” He would divide these phases as follows. First, there is a kind that is mixed with the truth. The devil says to a man, “Do not abandon the pleasures of life; life is long, and resisting the lusts of the body for one’s whole life is a heavy burden.” Then if the man remembers the great reality of God and of reward and punishment, and says to himself that resisting lusts is painful, but enduring the Fire is more painful, and that one of these must be chosen, the devil turns and flees; for he cannot say that the Fire is easier than abstaining from sin, or that sin will not lead to the Fire. So his whispering is cut off by the man’s faith in the Book of God. And so, too, if he whispers flattering admiration and says, “What creature knows God like you, or serves him as you do, and how great is your standing with God!” The man remembers that his knowledge, heart, and limbs with which he knows and works are of God’s creation; why, then, should he be admired? So the devil must necessarily retire. This kind of “whispering” can

<vocab>al-Ghazzālī, whispering, remembering, devil, heart, faith, Fire, Islam</vocab>

<summary>Al-Ghazzālī harmonizes various views on demonic "whisperings," explaining that they can be effectively dispelled through specific acts of faith and the remembrance of God. He illustrates how reflecting on the consequences of sin and the divine origin of one's virtues forces the devil to retreat.</summary>
<keywords>al-Ghazzālī, temptation, devil, whispering, remembrance of God, ethics, vanity, the Fire, Islam</keywords>

--- Page 321 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Al-Ghazzālī's analysis of the "whisperings" of the devil and how they can be cut off through faith. It moves into the second and third phases of these whisperings, specifically focusing on lust and intrusive thoughts during prayer.</meta>

<page-num>297</page-num>
# THE TEMPTATIONS OF THE HEART

be completely cut off in the case of those who know and see in the light of faith.

The second phase of “whispering” is that which arouses and moves lust, and it is either of a kind which the person recognizes certainly as sin, or suspects vehemently. If he knows it certainly, the devil retires from the kind of arousal which actually results in moving lust, but not from simple arousal. And if it is only a suspected kind, it often remains active so as to call for vigorous treatment in repelling it. So this “whispering” exists but is repelled and does not have the upper hand.

The third phase is that it should be a “whispering” of ideas (<term>khawāṭir</term>; <term>Einfälle</term>) only, reminding one of past states and a thinking of something other than prayer, for example. Then when the man turns to “remembrance,” it is possible that the whispering should be repelled at one time and return at another; so the two things keep alternating in the heart. So it is conceivable that they should compete with one another until the understanding embraces an understanding of the meaning of what is recited and of those ideas, as though they were in two different places in the heart. And it is hardly conceivable that this kind can be repelled completely; yet it is not impossible, since the Prophet said, 

> “He who prays a two-bow prayer without his soul bringing into it something of the world, all his sins that have

<summary>Al-Ghazzālī describes the second and third phases of demonic "whispering," explaining how faith can repel lustful thoughts and how intrusive ideas compete with remembrance during prayer.</summary>
<keywords>Al-Ghazzālī, whisperings, lust, prayer, khawāṭir, remembrance, devil, temptation, two-bow prayer</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Al-Ghazzālī's analysis regarding the possibility of achieving perfect focus during prayer and the extreme difficulty of avoiding "whisperings" or worldly distractions. It specifically references a Hadith regarding the forgiveness of sins for those who complete a two-bow prayer without worldly thoughts.</meta>

<page-num>298</page-num>
<header>RELIGIOUS ATTITUDE AND LIFE IN ISLĀM</header>

gone before are forgiven him."'¹ If this were not conceivable, the Prophet would not have mentioned it; but it can be expected only in a heart that love has so mastered that it is infatuated. We sometimes see, in the case of a man whose heart is so occupied with an enemy who has injured him, that he will think—not just for two bows but for many—about disputing with him, without anything other than the thought of his enemy entering his mind. So, too, with one who is deeply in love and thinks of his beloved; he neither hears nor sees anything else. How, then, might the same not be expected from fear of the Fire and desire for the Garden? Yet it is rare, for faith in God and the Last Day is weak.

All the views, then, that have been held regarding the possibility of repelling the whisperings of the devil are possible as applied to the different kinds and phases of that whispering. Safety from it, too, for a short period of time is possible; but remaining safe for a whole lifetime is highly improbable—even, as things are, impossible. The Prophet himself was distracted from prayer by the border of his own robe and by a gold ring on his finger. So the slightest possession, beyond absolute necessity—a single *dīnār*—will distract and bring thoughts of the world. There can be no compromise. He who

---

¹ <note>Compare the story of St. Bernard and the man, the horse, and the saddle in the *Golden Legend*, Vol. V, p. 23, Temple edition.</note>

<summary>Al-Ghazzālī explains that while total focus in prayer is possible through intense love or fear of God, it remains rare due to weak faith and the distractions of worldly possessions. He concludes that while one may briefly repel the devil's whisperings, remaining entirely free from them throughout life is nearly impossible.</summary>
<keywords>Al-Ghazzālī, prayer, focus, whisperings of the devil, dīnār, Prophet Muhammad, St. Bernard, faith</keywords>

--- Page 323 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Al-Ghazzālī’s analysis regarding the distractions of the world and the difficulty of maintaining spiritual focus, concluding the analogy of the man holding the world and the honey.</meta>

<page-num>299</page-num>
<header>THE TEMPTATIONS OF THE HEART</header>

holds the world fast with his claws and yet desires to be free from the devil is like one who has been plunged in honey and thinks that flies will not land on him. The world is an enormous gateway for the devil; or rather, a multitude of gateways. First, he approaches a man through his sins; if he is repelled, then through advice, until he makes him fall into some innovation (<term>bid'a</term>); if he is repelled in that, he leads him into abstinence until he regards something as unlawful that is not so; if he is repelled in that, he raises doubts as to whether his ablution or his prayer has been legally sound; and if he is repelled in that, he makes his deeds of piety easy for him, so that men regard him as most abstinent and patient; their hearts turn to him; he admires himself and perishes. This is the last stage of temptation; the saint who escapes self-admiration is safe in reaching the Garden.

4. To illustrate the quickness of change in the heart and its sensitivity to influence, the Prophet compared it to a sparrow, turning at every moment; to a pot boiling over; and to a feather blown across the surface of the desert. But it is always in the hand of God. Thus, the Prophet was fond of using as an oath or form of address the title of God, “Turner of Hearts.”

From the point of view of stability in good or evil, or swaying between the two, hearts are of three kinds: 
First, the fixedly good, however the devil may assail it;

<vocab>temptation, heart, devil, sins, innovation, bid'a, abstinence, ablution, prayer, piety, self-admiration, Garden, Prophet, Turner of Hearts</vocab>

<summary>This page describes the devil's subtle and progressive methods of temptation, which can lead even the most pious individuals into the trap of self-admiration. It also uses prophetic metaphors to illustrate the inherent instability of the human heart, which remains ultimately under God's control.</summary>
<keywords>temptation, devil's tactics, bid'a, self-admiration, Turner of Hearts, spiritual stability, Al-Ghazzālī</keywords>

--- Page 324 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the types of human hearts in relation to spiritual stability, moving from the description of the fixedly good heart to those that are hopelessly bad or constantly struggling between good and evil.</meta>

<page-num>300</page-num>
<header>RELIGIOUS LIFE AND ATTITUDE IN ISLĀM</header>

To such a heart God turns His face; it is the heart at rest, as described in the *Qur'ān* (xiii, 28): "Do hearts not find rest in the thought of Allāh?" Second is the hopelessly bad heart. In it, black smoke rises from passion, fills it, and extinguishes its light; reason cannot see to guide and is subdued by lusts. But third is the heart in which there is a constant swaying and contest between good and evil. The devil urges upon it the pleasures of the world and the example of learned theologians; but the angel urges the abiding joys of heaven and the pains of hell. This position is frankly otherworldly, and the fear of the Fire is the great motive being urged.

> "If it were a hot summer day, and all mankind were standing out in the sun while you had a cool house, would you stand with them, or would you not rather seek safety for yourself? How, then, would you set yourself against them out of fear of the heat of the sun, and not out of fear of the heat of the Fire?"

Accordingly, as the satanic qualities or the angelic qualities in each heart are predominant, so will the outcome be; and all that happens will be in agreement with the decree of God. For him who is created for the Garden, the causes of obedience will be made easy; and for him who is created for the Fire, the causes of rebellion are made easy.

To him whom Allāh wills to guide, He opens his breast to Islām; and him whom He wills to lead astray, He narrows his breast.<note>¹ *Qur'ān* vi, 125.</note> He is the one who guides rightly and the leader astray; He does what

<summary>This page describes three types of hearts—the peaceful, the hopelessly corrupted, and the conflicted—and explains that spiritual outcomes and obedience are determined by God's divine decree. It emphasizes that the struggle between angelic and satanic influences is ultimately governed by whether Allāh chooses to guide or mislead an individual.</summary>
<keywords>heart, Qur'ān, Allāh, satanic qualities, angelic qualities, divine decree, Garden, Fire, Islām, guidance, predestination</keywords>

--- Page 325 ---
<meta>This page continues the theological exploration of divine predestination and the absolute sovereignty of God's will, concluding the discussion on how different spiritual states of the heart relate to eternal salvation or damnation.</meta>

<page-num>301</page-num>
<header>THE TEMPTATIONS OF THE HEART</header>

He wills and decides whatever He wishes; there is no one to oppose His decision and no one to repel His decree. He created the Garden and created a people for it, then employed them in obedience; and He created the Fire and created a people for it, then employed them in rebellion. He informed His creation of the sign of the people of the Garden and the sign of the people of the Fire; then He said, "The pure are in bliss and the impure are in Jaḥīm"<note>¹ *Qur'an* 82:13, 14.</note> <gloss>"blazing fire," i.e., Hell</gloss>. Then He said, as has been passed down from the Prophet, "These are in the Garden, and I do not care; and these are in the Fire, and I do not care." So He is Allāh Most High, the King, the Reality; "He is not questioned about what He does, but they are questioned."<note>² *Ibid.*, 21:23.</note>

This is the conclusion of the whole matter, and to this must return the vision of the Muslim mystic and the ecstasy of the Muslim saint; the dreams of a lover and a beloved, and the groans and travails of creation. Whenever the devout life, with its spiritual aspirations and fervent longings, touches the framework of Muslim theology, it must bend and break in this way. There is no place for it within Islām itself. The enormous burden of the dogmatic system is too great; if it is to survive, it must wander into the heresies of either the mystic or the philosopher. Safety and dignity for the individual must be sought in some pantheistic scheme, beginning either with God or with man. The darwīshes "without law" (bī-sharᶜ) are the natural result of this paradox of al-Ghazzālī.

<summary>This page describes God’s absolute sovereignty and predestination, where He determines who is saved and who is damned according to His will. It argues that the rigid nature of Islamic dogma forces mystical and spiritual aspirations to seek expression through philosophical or pantheistic heresies.</summary>

<keywords>Allāh, predestination, divine decree, Al-Ghazzālī, Sufism, Jaḥīm, darwīshes, Muslim theology, pantheism, Islamic mysticism</keywords>

--- Page 326 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the detailed discussion of Al-Ghazzālī's theology of the heart to a broader summary of Islamic religious life and suggestions for future study.</meta>

<page-num>302</page-num>
<header>RELIGIOUS ATTITUDE AND LIFE IN ISLĀM</header>

Al-Ghazzālī’s general introduction to the internal and hidden side of life has now been presented to you, and the nature and functioning of that organ of communication with the spiritual world which he calls the “heart” should now be reasonably clear. I trust that the religious attitude and the religious life of Muslims are also clear in broad terms—the general development for Islam of the text, or thesis, which I borrowed from Mr. William James. You have seen how real that invisible world is to Muslims; you have seen how they think of it and turn toward it; and you have seen how they try to adjust themselves to it and live within it. The general direction is now before you.

Yet it would be easy to outline further and certainly productive lines of investigation. The precise pathology of Muhammad’s psychology is one. Another would be the history of the pantheistic development in the later Sufi schools, under Buddhist and Vedantic influence—a vast field. A third would be just as broad and even more significant—the current religious attitudes and movements of the Muslim peoples. There is no question that there are stirrings of new life within them, born of many causes. But for now, these must remain untouched.

<vocab>Al-Ghazzālī, Islam, Muslims, William James, Muhammad, Sufi, Buddhist, Vedantic</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes the examination of Al-Ghazzālī’s concept of the spiritual "heart" and the Muslim perspective on the invisible world. The author also identifies potential areas for further research, including the psychology of Muhammad and the historical development of Sufism.</summary>
